## Retrieval Augmented Generation

Compositionality

Ido Levy

Affiliation: IBM

Affiliation: Weizmann Institute of Science

Segev Shlomov

Affiliation: IBM

Email: Segev.Shlomov1@ibm.com

Leshem Choshen

Affiliation: IBM

Affiliation: Weizmann Institute of Science

Email: leshem.choshen@weizmann.ac.ilhttps://github.com/dolev31/lasagna-0.15em[height=0.95em]figures/icon-githubgithub.com/dolev31/lasagnahttps://dolev31.github.io/lasagna/-0.15em[height=0.95em]figures/icon-globedolev31.github.io/lasagna

Abstract

Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds large language models
in external knowledge. Modern pipelines combine embedding, chunking,
retrieval, reranking, query augmentation, context compression, and
agentic verification, yet these techniques are typically evaluated
one at a time. It remains unclear whether their isolated gains survive
composition, whether retrieval quality and answer faithfulness favor
the same design choices, and whether conclusions from small corpora
hold at large scale. We pre-declare three hypotheses and
introduce lasagna, an open-source framework for controlled
RAG evaluation. We test $450$ configurations across eight benchmarks
and corpora ranging from $5$ K to $200$ K documents. Three findings
emerge. First, individually strong techniques from different phases
of a RAG pipeline do not necessarily compose: three retrieval
techniques predict a $+47\%$ combined improvement but deliver only
 $+17\%$ when used together. Second, component gains stack differently
for retrieval and faithfulness, and optimizing one can hurt the other.
Third, the best composition changes as the corpus grows: the
combination that wins on a small corpus may not remain best at
large scale. These findings show that gains measured in isolation
are not reliable predictors of realistic performance, and that new RAG techniques
should be tested with the full surrounding stack. We call on
researchers to do so and supply lasagna to simplify these
experiments.

## 1 Introduction

Retrieval-augmented generation
(RAG) (Gao et al., 2023b; Fan et al., 2024) combines choices across
many pipeline stages. Our study covers eight stages that span the
dominant design axes in modern RAG (Fig. 1b; full
catalog in Appendix K): embedding, chunking, index-time
enrichment, retrieval, query augmentation, reranking, context
compression, and agentic quality gates. Most new techniques are
proposed and evaluated one stage at a time, in isolation from the
surrounding pipeline, or from several representative pipelines that
would test whether findings generalize (Yu et al., 2024). Adding to the complexity, RAG systems
are evaluated for two goals (Saad-Falcon et al., 2024; Friel et al., 2024):
retrieving the right content (NDCG@10) and producing a faithful
answer from it (Es et al., 2024, RAGAS;). It is unclear whether isolated gains persist under composition. It
is also unclear whether the same design choices optimize both
retrieval quality (which documents are retrieved) and answer
faithfulness (whether the answer is grounded in those documents),
two goals RAG evaluation typically reports separately. We pre-declare three hypotheses. H1. Components often fail
to compose: isolated gains from single-component ablations do not
necessarily persist when components are combined. H2. A
simple RAG pipeline can be near-optimal in many settings, while
more elaborate configurations tend to specialize to narrower ones.
Most RAG ablations in the literature run at 5K to 50K documents,
whereas the applications RAG can serve run at corpora an order of
magnitude larger; H3. composition rankings shift with
corpus scale, so conclusions from small-corpus benchmarks may not
hold at larger scales. Prior work has explored combinations through
configuration sweeps and heuristic
search (Wang et al., 2024; Li et al., 2025; Rau et al., 2024; Jin et al., 2025; Kim et al., 2024; Fu et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2026); these
approaches can identify strong configurations but do not directly
measure interaction effects: whether the benefit of one component
depends on the other components in the pipeline. These interactions,
and how they change with corpus scale, remain largely unquantified.

Testing these hypotheses requires a framework that isolates pipeline
stages and supports reproducible evaluation across benchmarks. We
created lasagna, an open-source RAG framework, to provide
this (Fig. 1a, Section 3). Each
stage sits behind a fixed interface, so swapping one method cannot
silently change the implementation at another. Adding a new
technique is a single line of code; the framework tests it inside a
complete modern pipeline without reimplementing the surrounding stack.

We use lasagna to study RAG composition with a
167-configuration D-optimal
design (Fedorov et al., 1972; Atkinson et al., 2007) across eight
configurable stages of a modern RAG pipeline
(Table 10), eight benchmarks (Fig. 5),
and corpus sizes from 5K to 200K documents, complemented by 138
non-factorial ablation configurations across 24 targeted
experiments (Appendix F). Across the factorial, ablation suite, and scaling sweep, the
central finding is that techniques that perform well separately do
not consistently retain their gains inside a full pipeline, and the
pattern differs between retrieval and faithfulness. Retrieval gains
compose sub-additively: the three largest single-component
improvements sum to a predicted $+47\%$ NDCG@10 gain, but the
composed pipeline realizes only $+17\%$ (Section D.14).
Faithfulness gains compose near-additively: separately measured
improvements approximately transfer to the full pipeline
(Table 47). Pipeline and generator
also partly substitute: a stronger generator helps the lightest pipeline
far more than the heaviest, so a heavier stack buys
less when the generator is already strong (Section F.9).

Three practical implications follow. First, the components that
improve retrieval are not the same as those that improve faithfulness,
and optimizing one metric can hurt the other. Second, the four
complete RAG profiles expose a Pareto tradeoff on retrieval
quality, faithfulness, and response time, with no universal winner.
Adding an agentic loop to a strong retrieval stack yields only a
negligible faithfulness gain while hurting retrieval quality and
latency. Across three other benchmarks at 100K–200K documents,
lighter pipelines retain similar measured quality at
 $10$ – $20\times$ lower latency
(Sections 5 and D.8). Third, corpus
size shifts profile rankings: the design leading at small corpora
trails by $13.7\%$ NDCG@10 at 200K, with a rank flip visible from
25K (Fig. 4(a)). Small-corpus rankings should therefore
be rechecked at the intended scale.
Our contributions are:

1. 

How RAG components compose. A compositional study
of RAG across eight configurable stages, eight benchmarks, and
corpora scales from 5K to 200K documents: 167 configurations from
a D-optimal factorial plus 138 non-factorial configurations across
24 targeted ablations. The four operational RAG profiles that
emerge (speed, standard, balanced,
max-reliable) are calibrated to cost, latency, and performance.

2. 

lasagna. The open-source framework built to
conduct and reproduce this study. It provides a modular,
stage-isolated RAG pipeline and an evaluation harness that lets
researchers add a method and test it with the surrounding stack
(Sections C.1 and F).

## 2 Related Work

Modern RAG spans a layered design space with plausible interaction
points at every stage (full method catalog in Appendix K).
On retrieval, sparse baselines
(BM25 (Robertson & Zaragoza, 2009)) coexist with dense encoders
(BGE-M3 (Chen et al., 2024a), E5 (Wang et al., 2022)), learned
sparse encoders (SPLADE (Formal et al., 2021)), and
late-interaction models (ColBERT (Khattab & Zaharia, 2020)), fused
by reciprocal-rank fusion (RRF) (Cormack et al., 2009) or learned
hybrids.
Reranking spans cross-encoder scorers trained on
MS-MARCO (Nogueira & Cho, 2019), ensemble rerankers, and
diversity-driven selection (MMR; Carbonell & Goldstein, 1998). Context
compression is either token-budget aware
(LLMLingua (Jiang et al., 2023a)) or positional
(lost-in-the-middle-aware selection (Liu et al., 2024)).
Query augmentation rewrites the query before retrieval:
HyDE (Gao et al., 2023a) drafts a hypothetical answer,
RAG-Fusion (Rackauckas, 2024) fans out multi-query
variants, decomposition (Khattab et al., 2024) splits multi-hop
questions into sub-questions, and step-back
prompting (Zheng et al., 2024) abstracts before retrieving.
Index-time processing enriches chunks before retrieval with
document summaries (RAPTOR (Sarthi et al., 2024)), contextual
prefixes (Anthropic, 2024), or entity-graph indexes
(GraphRAG (Edge et al., 2024)). Generation-time control
adds correction and gating loops around the answer model
(CRAG (Yan et al., 2024), Self-RAG (Asai et al., 2024),
Speculative-RAG (Wang et al., 2025)). Each of these
stages has been shown to help in isolation on some benchmark, but
they compose along more edges than a single-component ablation can
enumerate. We study how these choices combine once
deployed together, and lasagna lets others ask
compositionality questions in the same environment by
registering a new component and evaluating it against a full modern
pipeline.

Table 1 positions lasagna against prior
work that studies RAG beyond isolated components. Best-practice
studies (Wang et al., 2024; Barnett et al., 2024) search greedily
for strong end-to-end configurations but do not estimate interaction
effects. Benchmarking libraries such as BERGEN (Rau et al., 2024)
and FlashRAG (Jin et al., 2025) support composition in a shared
codebase but do not enforce the stage isolation that controlled
interaction estimation requires, so measured differences cannot be
cleanly attributed to the component being varied
(a positional smoke run against both is reported in
Section G.4). Automated
configuration search (Kim et al., 2024) optimizes
over configurations but reports only the winning setting, not the
interaction structure that produced it, so the resulting guidance
does not transfer when any assumption changes. Evaluation
suites (Krishna et al., 2025) and domain
benchmarks (Xiong et al., 2024) supply metrics
and tasks but not a common pipeline in which those metrics can be
compared across controlled compositions. Generation-time
controls (Yan et al., 2024) strengthen the agentic layer but are
usually tested with retrieval fixed, leaving composition with a tuned
stack untested.

## 3 LASAGNA: A Compositional Framework for RAG

lasagna is an open-source framework for
evaluating new RAG components inside a complete modern pipeline.
A researcher proposing a new reranker, chunker, or agentic loop today
must either compare against a stripped-down baseline that does not
reflect a modern pipeline, or reimplement the surrounding stack. The
first biases the conclusion by mixing implementation differences
with algorithmic ones; the second is prohibitively expensive and
rarely attempted. lasagna provides a configurable
end-to-end pipeline (Fig. 1a) with a fixed
interface per stage that the framework enforces, so a new component
drops into one stage and is measured against the same surrounding
stack used by every reported configuration (full code patterns in
Sections H.2.2, H.2.3 and H.2.4).
The workflow spans nine processing stages: document processing,
chunking, index-time enrichment, embedding and indexing, query
augmentation, retrieval, reranking, context compression, and answer
generation with optional agentic verification.
Every pipeline component is a subclass of a typed abstract base class:
eight component types are supported (embedder, chunker,
reranker, retriever, document_processor,
query_enhancer, agentic_pipeline, and generator), each
stage’s inputs and outputs are typed dataclass contracts, and
behavioral tests check that swapping one implementation does not mutate
a neighboring stage’s state. A single
@registry.register("<stage>", "<name>") decorator suffices
to make a new component discoverable at YAML-config time.
The framework ships with 53 built-in components, the 30 methods across the eight factorial
dimensions (Table 10; full catalog in Appendix K),
six vector backends (Milvus, Pinecone, Qdrant, Weaviate, pgvector,
OpenSearch), and adapters for the eight benchmarks used here (five
BEIR retrieval sets and three long-form QA sets;
Section B.2). Five automated isolation tests
(Section H.1) check that toggling one component does not
silently change another, including that two sessions with opposite
settings each keep their own configuration; this supports attributing
the interaction estimates in Section 5 to the components we
vary rather than to cross-stage leakage. A new component becomes a publishable comparison
in three steps (Fig. 2): register with a decorator,
declare a factorial sweep in YAML, and run end-to-end to receive a
paired statistical test against the same surrounding stack that
produced every reported number. The complete study, factorial plus
138 non-factorial ablations plus the 5K–200K scaling sweep, cost
approximately $5,200 in cloud compute and 310 wall-clock hours
(Section A.7).

## 4 Experimental Design

Notation.

A dimension is one of the eight experimental factors
(Table 10); default_standard is the
framework’s stock RAG pipeline (a simple, lowest-cost setting on
each dimension) that serves as the starting baseline for our
experiments; a profile is one of the four operational RAG
compositions we distill from our factorial and ablation sweeps as
the best-observed configurations targeting distinct points on the
retrieval-faithfulness-latency envelope (speed,
standard, balanced, max-reliable; full
specifications in Section I.1); a track
is one of the two evaluation surfaces (retrieval or end-to-end). We evaluate on both tracks
because retrieval quality (whether the right documents are
retrieved) and answer faithfulness (whether the generated answer
is grounded in the retrieved context) measure distinct properties
of a RAG pipeline, and different applications weight them
differently. We report the joint quality-latency envelope as a
hypervolume $\mathrm{HV}(L_{\mathrm{ref}})=\mathrm{NDCG@10}\times\mathrm{Faith}\times\max(0,1-\mathrm{latency}/L_{\mathrm{ref}})$ 
 (Zitzler et al., 2003) at $L_{\mathrm{ref}}{=}30$  s (a
fixed response-time reference; Table 2); HV values under
other fixed references are in Table 39. We
report marginal effect sizes as $\eta^{2}$ ; larger values indicate
stronger associations with variation in the metric (full statistics
in Sections D.2 and D.11).

Factorial design.

The configuration space spans eight
experimental dimensions, with 2–5 levels each (Fig. 1b,
Table 10). The full factorial of
 $4{\times}4{\times}4{\times}3{\times}5{\times}4{\times}2{\times}4=30{,}720$ 
configurations would exceed $\sim$ 40,000 GPU-hours and
 $\sim$ $600,000 in LLM API costs (extrapolating from the executed
167-configuration budget of 247 GPU-hours and $5,200;
Section A.7), roughly two orders of magnitude beyond our
resources, so we draw a 242-run D-optimal subset via Fedorov
coordinate exchange (Fedorov et al., 1972), sized to keep all
main effects and all 28 dimension-pair interactions estimable
under our budget. Of the 242 planned runs, 167 were executed: 63
GraphRAG-enriched runs were dropped after an isolated ablation
showed no measurable NDCG@10 change despite the ${\sim}2\times$ 
latency the enrichment added
(Table 11), and 12 failed on timeout limits.
Design-matrix construction, aliasing diagnostics, and the
identifiability audit for the pre-declared joint 28-two-way test
appear in Sections A.4, A.5 and D.3.
Each dimension uses a simple, lowest-cost baseline; pre-registration
deviations appear in Table 12.

A unified multi-domain corpus, two evaluation tracks.

Production RAG deployments operate against heterogeneous knowledge
bases where a single retriever must disambiguate biomedical,
financial, scientific, argumentative, and multi-hop queries from one
shared vector store. Per-benchmark evaluation (the
BEIR default; Thakur et al., 2021) under-tests this cross-domain
distractor pressure. We therefore unify our eight evaluation
benchmarks into one corpus: five BEIR sets cover retrieval (NFCorpus,
Boteva et al., 2016; FiQA, Maia et al., 2018; SciFact,
Wadden et al., 2020; ArguAna, Wachsmuth et al., 2018;
HotpotQA, Yang et al., 2018) and three QA sets cover generation
(SQuAD v2, Rajpurkar et al., 2018; MuSiQue,
Trivedi et al., 2022), yielding 149 stratified queries
per configuration (Sections B.2, B.1, 17, 16 and 15). Each
configuration is evaluated on two tracks: retrieval (BEIR
qrels over 5,132 documents, 93 queries) and end-to-end
generation (the same corpus augmented with gold passages, 5,566
documents, 56 queries). Beyond the 167-run factorial, we
additionally ran 138 non-factorial ablation configurations across 24
targeted experiments spanning rerankers, embeddings, chunkers, query
augmentation, and the number of agentic verification iterations
(Appendix F).
Together with the corpus-scaling sweep ( $5$ K– $200$ K), the study
records approximately 358,215 individual query evaluations,
including repeated RAGAS evaluation tasks for the same queries
(Section A.1).

Metrics.

End-to-end generation runs report faithfulness and answer relevance
(RAGAS v0.4 (Es et al., 2024) judged by GPT-5.1);
NDCG@10, exact match, token-level F1, and p50/p95 latency and
$/query; GPT-5.1 also generates answers for all configurations.
NDCG@10 follows the BEIR convention (Thakur et al., 2021): retrieved
chunks are de-duplicated by parent document ID before scoring, so the
metric matches the document-level qrel grain.
A two-annotator human evaluation validates RAGAS faithfulness
(Sections C.4, C.5 and 29), and a two-judge
sensitivity analysis (GPT-5.1 vs. Claude Sonnet 4.5) shows that
judge choice shifts absolute faithfulness but preserves profile
ranking, so we treat single-judge point estimates as approximate
and rely on ordinal comparisons for all headline claims
(Section F.7). A third-judge robustness check
asks Claude Opus 4.7 to score 220 question-answer-context examples
using the same scoring instructions (Section F.8).
Its moderate agreement with the primary judge (Pearson $r{=}0.44$ ,
Cohen’s $\kappa{=}0.35$ ) supports the same conclusion: relative trends
persist, although exact faithfulness scores depend on the judge.
Metric definitions and judge-bias mitigations in
Section C.1.

Pre-declared hypotheses.

Most published RAG ablations tune one component at a time on a
single benchmark at $\leq 50$ K corpus, and report the resulting gain
as a recommended configuration. We pre-declared (van Miltenburg et al., 2021; Forde & Paganini, 2019) three hypotheses
that together test whether this methodology is sound, by asking
whether RAG pipelines can be designed compositionally:
H1 tests additivity (the assumption behind isolated
single-component ablations), H2 tests profile
generality (the assumption that a single balanced recipe deploys
across targets), and H3 tests scale invariance (the
assumption that rankings measured on small corpora persist at larger
corpus scales). The full pre-registration (operational definitions,
statistical thresholds, exclusion criteria, post-hoc corrections) is
reproduced in Section A.2.

H1 

Components do not necessarily combine additively.
H1 tests whether two-way interactions among the eight experimental
dimensions explain at least $20\%$ of faithfulness variance. If they
do, gains measured in isolation cannot be assumed to persist when
components are combined.

H2 

A single RAG composition can span multiple deployment targets.
RAG designs face a Pareto tradeoff: retrieval quality,
faithfulness, and response time cannot be maximized jointly,
so practitioners typically re-run configuration search per
deployment. H2 asks whether an intermediate RAG composition
sweeps a wider quality / latency / cost hypervolume than
adding more advanced components does, on a majority of
datasets, covering enough of the Pareto surface that one
published recipe serves multiple targets. Rejecting H2 means
each deployment must hand-tune its own operating point
(formalism and correction: Section A.2).

H3 

Composition rankings shift with corpus scale.
Most RAG ablations in the literature run at 5K to 50K
documents, but production deployments often serve corpora
an order of magnitude larger. H3 asks whether the ranking of
RAG compositions at 5K matches their ranking at 200K on the
same corpus; if it does not, small-corpus rankings do not
transfer across scale, and design decisions measured at one
scale must be revalidated at the target scale. Confirming
H3 means small-corpus benchmarks are methodologically suspect
as scale-invariant selectors of RAG designs; rejecting it
validates them as predictive across the scales tested
(per-composition log-linear fits, slope-range threshold,
bootstrap CIs, and Kendall’s $\tau$ rank-flip test in
Section A.2).

## 5 Results

H1: the benefit of a RAG technique depends on the complete RAG pipeline and the target metric.

Across the factorial and targeted tests, some techniques reinforce
one another, some add roughly as expected, and others overlap or
interfere (Fig. 3). Thus, improvements measured by changing
one pipeline stage at a time do not reliably add up in a complete RAG
pipeline. We quantify this by comparing the gain predicted from
separately measured improvements with the gain the combined pipeline
actually delivers. This needs only the configurations the study already
runs, whereas estimating all $28$ pairwise interactions directly requires
 $232$ parameters, more than a design of this size can support
(Section D.3). The three largest separately measured retrieval gains, from
cross-encoder reranking, disabling MMR, and document-awareness
prefixes, predict a $+46.6\%$ lift over the
default_standard baseline
(NDCG@10 $=0.412$ ;
Table 32). On this same split a BM25-only
retriever scores $0.403$ and a recommended off-the-shelf dense encoder
 $0.436$ (Table 60), so this is a realistic starting
point, not a weak one. The balanced pipeline,
which combines these techniques, realizes only $+17.2\%$ , a $63\%$ 
shortfall relative to the three-technique prediction. Including all
five separately positive retrieval techniques raises the shortfall to
 $72\%$ (Fig. 9); across 17 tested combinations, it
ranges from $7\%$ to $72\%$ , with a median of $41\%$ 
(Sections D.14 and D.12). The ablations suggest partial
functional overlap: reranking already provides some of the filtering
benefit obtained by disabling MMR, leaving less room for the two
techniques to help when combined. The target metric matters as well:
embedding, enrichment, and reranking dominate the retrieval-quality
metric, whereas query augmentation and agentic gating dominate the
answer-faithfulness metric, which measures whether generated claims
are supported by the retrieved context. Across matched pipeline
configurations, the two metrics are essentially unrelated
(Fig. 7; pooled Pearson $r=-0.01$ , $p=0.91$ ):
 $5\%$ of configurations combine high faithfulness with low retrieval
quality, and $6\%$ show high retrieval quality with low faithfulness.
The same near-zero relationship appears when each benchmark is
examined separately, removing the concern that the near-zero
correlation is caused by pooling benchmarks (Section D.2).
This indicates that RAG pipeline selection must evaluate both metrics:
selecting a pipeline based only on the retrieval metric can yield strong
retrieval but weakly grounded answers, while selecting one based only on
the faithfulness metric can hide poor retrieval.

H1: retrieval quality is most strongly associated with embedding and enrichment.

On the retrieval track (Fig. 3, upper triangle; $n{=}167$ 
configurations), the largest marginal associations are embedding
( $\eta^{2}=0.164$ ), enrichment ( $\eta^{2}=0.144$ ), and reranking
( $\eta^{2}=0.090$ ). These unadjusted values are used for exploratory
screening and are not additive because the D-optimal design is
unbalanced and the effects share variance. Compression and agentic gate
contribute negligibly ( $\eta^{2}<0.01$ each). Two two-way interactions
survive both Bonferroni and Benjamini–Hochberg FDR correction:
Embedding  $\times$  Enrichment ( $\eta^{2}=0.102$ ) and
Embedding  $\times$  Reranking ( $\eta^{2}=0.110$ ). Both survivors
involve the embedding model. We attribute Embedding  $\times$  Enrichment
to representation capacity: stronger embedders can encode added
context without diluting the original passage. We attribute
Embedding  $\times$  Reranking to candidate-set quality: rerankers
have less room to help when the initial embedding already retrieves
strong candidates (Sections J.3.1 and J.3.2). MMR reduces NDCG@10 by $3.6\%$ relative
on average, opposite its usual benefit on broad, heterogeneous corpora
(Table 44). We hypothesize that on focused-domain corpora
passages with high lexical and semantic overlap are often the same
supporting evidence, so MMR’s diversity penalty demotes them in favor
of topically distant ones (see Sections D.12 and D.13).

H1: answer faithfulness depends most on query augmentation and agentic gating.

On the faithfulness track (Fig. 3, lower triangle), query
augmentation ( $\eta^{2}=0.224$ ) and agentic gating
( $\eta^{2}=0.217$ ) have the largest marginal associations; embedding
and enrichment, the strongest retrieval drivers, are negligible on
faithfulness ( $\eta^{2}<0.02$ each). We attribute this contrast to
pipeline stage: query augmentation changes retrieved evidence, whereas
agentic gating filters or verifies it before generation. We test how
these stages compose across all $5{\times}4$ combinations of query
augmentation and agentic gating (Section D.10). The
interaction effect is partial $\eta^{2}=0.033$ ; 10 of 20 combinations
depart from additivity by at least two percentage points. Under
Speculative-RAG, decomposition is $4.7$ points above the additive
prediction, while query expansion is $4.1$ below it.
We hypothesize that decomposition supplies distinct subproblems for
draft-and-verify, whereas broader expansion can introduce overlapping
or competing evidence. Thus, isolated gains do not reliably predict
composition; these stages should be evaluated jointly
(Table 42).

Figure 3: Main effects and pairwise interactions across eight RAG
pipeline factors. The upper triangle shows retrieval
( $167$ configurations, NDCG@10), the lower triangle faithfulness
( $122$ configurations, RAGAS). Darker cells mean stronger effects;
an interaction cell shows how much one factor’s effect depends on
another factor’s setting. Color encodes marginal (unadjusted)
 $\eta^{2}$ , the share of variance a factor or interaction explains,
so the two metrics can be compared descriptively. Embedding and
enrichment matter most for retrieval, whereas query augmentation
and agentic gating matter most for faithfulness. Because the
D-optimal design is unbalanced, values can overlap and should not
be summed into a total explained variance.

Faithfulness shows a different additivity pattern. A
one-factor-at-a-time estimate based on BGE-large embeddings,
hierarchical chunking, hybrid-BM25 retrieval, and Speculative-RAG
gating predicts a $+28\%$ gain over a reference configuration, while
the best full RAG pipeline improves by $+32\%$ over the same reference.
This suggests that some faithfulness gains may combine approximately
as measured. We hypothesize that this occurs when techniques act at
complementary pipeline stages and duplicate less of one another’s function
(Table 47). Full pairwise results are
in Table 33.

H1 (additivity): gains measured one component at a time do not carry over when components are combined, and how much is lost depends on the metric.

Combining components loses most of the predicted retrieval gain and almost
none of the predicted faithfulness gain. The three strongest
single-component retrieval improvements predict $+47\%$ NDCG@10 and the
pipeline containing all three realizes $+17\%$ ; the same exercise on
faithfulness predicts $+28\%$ and realizes $+32\%$ 
(Section D.14).
Our pre-declared test of H1 was not met, and that outcome fits the same
split. It asked whether pairwise interactions explain at least $20\%$ of
the variance in faithfulness, the metric on which components combine
nearly additively, and they do not (Section D.3). A
focused follow-up, pre-specified before it ran, confirms that the
interaction the broad analysis flagged is real ( $p=0.007$ ) but far below
that bar (Sections D.9 and D.10). New
components should therefore be evaluated inside representative complete
pipelines, on both metrics.

H3: composition rankings shift between 5K and 200K documents.

We extend the 5,132-document base corpus with scaling increments
(15K/25K/50K/100K/200K) under the same proportional sampling
protocol, with no distractor overlapping any evaluation query’s
relevant set. For 25K–200K, repeated seeded executions resample
retrieval and generation stochasticity but not corpus composition;
distractors are sampled from the same five BEIR source domains
as the base corpus (full protocol in
Section B.3). NDCG@10 across six corpus sizes
separates into two regimes (Fig. 4(a)):
speed and standard degrade log-linearly, while
balanced and max-reliable are approximately flat.
The regimes cross between 15K and 25K. standard leads at
the small sizes (5K, 15K); balanced leads from 25K onward.
At 200K, balanced exceeds standard by $13.7\%$ 
relative ( $p=0.003$ ; full per-size table in
Section E.1).

We attribute the flatter retrieval-NDCG degradation at scale of
balanced to several retrieval-stage additions relative to
standard: broader chunk retention, hierarchical document
partitioning, and post-retrieval merging (full list in
Section I.1). Together, these sustain a denser,
higher-signal top- $k$ as distractors dilute the pool. The extractive compression and
document-awareness prefixes that balanced additionally enables
act post-retrieval and pre-generation respectively; they primarily
support faithfulness rather than the NDCG@10 gap
(Section D.12; examples in
document awareness and the
50K crossover). speed degrades
fastest, which we hypothesize reflects MiniLM-L6’s limited
discriminative capacity (384-dim) operating without the lexical
anchoring that BM25 would provide under hybrid retrieval, see
example and
Table 23 for results.

(a) Retrieval NDCG@10: speed and standard
follow a log-linear decay; balanced and
max-reliable are statistically flat.

(b) Generation faithfulness: all three quality-focused
profiles peak at 50K, dip at 100K, and recover from 100K to
200K.

Figure 4: Scaling behavior vs. corpus size (log scale), four SOTA
profiles, 3 seeds per size. Dashed: log-linear fit over 5K–200K;
dotted beyond 200K: projections, not measurements. In
(a), balanced overtakes standard
between 15K–25K, significant at 200K.

H3: faithfulness and retrieval scale differently.

Despite NDCG@10 falling as gold passages dilute among distractors,
faithfulness does not decline with corpus scale. All three
quality-focused profiles peak around 50K, dip at 100K, and recover
from 100K to 200K
(Figs. 4(b) and 22).
We hypothesize that a larger corpus expands information coverage,
so that even when the gold document falls below the top- $k$ , other
documents contain overlapping or paraphrased content sufficient to
ground a faithful answer; the 100K $\to$ 200K recovery across all
three profiles is consistent with this mechanism. Full per-profile
slopes and 1M projections in
Tables 23, 22, 11 and 49;
latency scaling at each corpus size in Fig. 10.

H3: composition rankings shift between 5K and 200K.

Supported. standard leads at 5K and 15K;
balanced leads from 25K through 200K, where it exceeds
standard by $13.7\%$ relative ( $p=0.003$ , three seeds).
The 25K and 100K gaps are directionally consistent but underpowered.
Small-corpus rankings should therefore be revalidated at the target
scale (Tables 48 and D.5).

H2: complete RAG profiles expose a Pareto tradeoff.

We evaluate four profiles on a held-out SQuAD v2, MuSiQue, and
HotpotQA mixture across three seeds (Table 2). No profile
leads all metrics: speed is fastest; standard’s
direct route leads NDCG@10; the agentic balanced and
max-reliable profiles use three verification iterations and
lead faithfulness.

Table 2: End-to-end profile comparison on a SQuAD v2 / MuSiQue /
HotpotQA mixture over three seeds (GPT-5.1 judge; faithfulness uses
each profile’s deployment-native answer prompt; per-profile regime
and joint-HV sensitivity to that choice in

At the fixed $30$  s response-time reference,
standard has the highest joint hypervolume ( $0.523$ ). Within
the agentic pair, balanced improves NDCG@10 by $4.5\%$ and
response time by $13\%$ , with only a $0.005$ faithfulness difference.
We attribute part of this tradeoff to the execution route: direct
generation favors response time, while iterative verification favors
faithfulness. Thus, additional components can improve one metric
without improving the complete RAG pipeline, so all three metrics
should be evaluated jointly.

H2 (single-composition generality): no complete RAG profile is best on every metric.

At the fixed $30$  s reference, standard achieves the
highest joint hypervolume ( $0.523$ ; Table 2). The agentic
profiles trade more response time for faithfulness; among them,
balanced nearly matches max-reliable’s
faithfulness while improving retrieval quality and response time.
Thus, the best configuration depends on the target metrics, and
adding components does not guarantee a better pipeline.

Supporting evidence: what helps and what does not.

A sweep of 138 configurations across 24 targeted experiments yields
several practical findings (Appendix F). Across
20 configurations, chunker choice spans $0.126$ NDCG@10, a $36\%$ 
relative difference larger than most factorial main effects
(Table 55). Thus, preprocessing choices
should also be tested within the complete pipeline. The 2019 MS-MARCO
MiniLM-L6 cross-encoder (Nogueira & Cho, 2019) ranks above all
four recent rerankers tested (Table 51); we
speculate that its training aligns especially well with our focused
domains. Matryoshka truncation of BGE-M3 to 768
dimensions (Kusupati et al., 2022) reduces vector storage
and search cost by $25\%$ while improving NDCG@10 by $2.3\%$ 
(Table 52). We attribute the gain to the
Matryoshka training objective, which concentrates useful signal in
leading dimensions; encoder cost is unchanged because the full vector
is computed first. On generation, faithfulness
is highest with hallucination and answer-quality gates disabled:
gate-triggered refinement can remove supporting evidence, including 14
of 15 chunks in one multi-hop example
(Table 54). HyDE raises NDCG@10 by
 $3.1\%$ but doubles first-hop latency, reducing the latency-adjusted
gain to $0.9\%$ , so its value depends on the latency budget
(Section I.2).

## 6 Conclusion

We study how modern RAG techniques compose across pipeline stages,
metrics, and corpus scales using lasagna, a stage-isolated
framework, and $450$ configurations across eight benchmarks from 5K
to 200K documents. Gains measured one component at a time do not
necessarily persist in complete pipelines; retrieval quality and
answer faithfulness favor different choices; and the best composition
changes with corpus scale. New techniques should be tested in
representative complete pipelines, on both metrics, and at the
intended scale. We release lasagna to make such studies
practical and reproducible, and to establish compositional
evaluation as the reporting standard for new RAG techniques rather
than the isolated single-component ablations common today.

## AI use statement

In this work, we used generative AI tools for designing or providing feedback on
research methodology and experiments, implementing methods, and interpreting
results. We have not used generative AI tools for generating synthetic data sets,
developing theoretical models or conceptual frameworks, proposing or refining
hypotheses, cleaning or reformatting datasets, or translation, and formulating
mathematical claims or proofs and qualitative or thematic data analysis are not
applicable to this work. Additionally, we used generative AI tools for creating
and editing software code and figures, editing the paper to improve readability,
and formatting references. We have reviewed all AI-assisted
work: AI-assisted code was reviewed and tested by the authors, analysis results
were re-derived from the raw result files, and edited text, numbers, and
citations were checked against the source tables and references. We take
responsibility for the final content of this work, including text, claims or
artifacts produced with the aid of generative AI.
Separately, LLMs are components of the system we study: they generate answers
and act as RAGAS judges (GPT-5.1, Claude Sonnet 4.5), as documented in the
Methods and Appendices.

## Ethics statement

LASAGNA is an evaluation framework operating over publicly released
retrieval benchmarks; no human subjects, personal data, or private
corpora were used. LLM API calls totalled approximately $5,200 and
310 wall-clock hours (Section A.7); we report per-configuration
cost and latency to enable reproducibility on constrained budgets.
Because the framework lowers the cost of composing RAG pipelines,
users deploying it in domain applications requiring corpus-specific
validation should re-validate our profile candidates on their own
corpus before deployment. Our headline profile comparisons rest on
one constructed English multi-domain test bed with $\leq 93$ 
retrieval and $\leq 200$ generation queries per experiment, so the
profiles are best interpreted as candidates for further validation,
not prescriptions.

## Reproducibility statement

The lasagna framework code is released at https://github.com/dolev31/lasagna. The paper-specific artifacts cited by path in the appendices (the D-optimal design matrix, per-configuration profiles, per-query raw scores, and analysis scripts) are not part of that release and are available from the authors on request.
Appendices A, B and C document experimental design
and pre-registration; the per-experiment provenance manifest (Table 58),
conformance test verifying stage isolation (Section H.1), and full compute
accounting (Section A.7) support end-to-end reproducibility. The design
matrix, hypotheses, and decision rules were pre-declared internally and hashed
(SHA-256) prior to execution; we did not file a third-party timestamped
pre-registration (OSF/AsPredicted), so the main text says “pre-declared”
rather than “pre-registered”.

## Limitations

Our evaluation uses one constructed English multi-domain test bed
( $\leq 93$ retrieval queries, $\leq 200$ generation queries per
experiment) and does not test: multilingual corpora, real
enterprise or private data, open-weight generators (all headline
results use proprietary APIs; see Reproducibility Statement), or
cross-organizational large corpora. Faithfulness is now measured under
two generator models rather than one (Section F.9), which
is a sensitivity check on generator choice and not a sweep: both models
are proprietary, and the ordering of the two closest profiles is not
stable across them. Findings should be interpreted
as case-study evidence for the methodological framework, and
derived profiles as candidates for further validation rather than
prescriptions for high-stakes deployments. The benchmarks are
concentrated in five focused domains (biomedical, financial,
scientific, argumentative, and multi-hop QA over Wikipedia), and
the corpus-scaling protocol injects controlled distractors drawn
from those same five domains. On the evaluation side, our cross-judge
(LLM and humans) agreement supports profile rankings across
LLM judges, but absolute faithfulness scores still live in a single
judge’s calibration. The confirmatory faithfulness experiment
(E1, Section D.10) meets its pre-registered
three-seed replication ( $n_{\text{runs}}=3$ ) but uses $43$ unique
queries rather than the pre-registered $200$ , due to GPT-5.1
rate-limit budget cuts (Appendix A); magnitude
estimates should be read under this reduced query sampling. A
controlled length-variant rerun (Section C.3) further
shows that raw faithfulness is partly length-driven, and that the
concise-over-verbose gap reverses under length control; we therefore
report both raw and length-residualized faithfulness for any
cross-regime claim. The within-regime results in the main tables are
unaffected because they hold prompt length constant. Our component
inventory is also a January-2026 snapshot of a literature that ships
new techniques actively. Scaling degradations should be read as an
upper bound: a formal $n{=}100$ distractor-relevance audit
(Section B.3.5) finds a $0\%$ false-negative rate and a
 $9\%$ partial-relevance rate, so the H3 rank-flip is robust to
distractor mis-labeling but true degradation is likely smaller than
reported.

## A.2 Pre-Registration Document

The full pre-registration is reproduced below verbatim from the
standalone document frozen before data collection
(preregistration/preregistration.tex; design matrix
SHA-256 31a0dc19…df1728d6, seed $=42$ ). Every
deviation between this protocol and the executed experiments is
enumerated in Section A.6.

Table 4: Pre-registration metadata: date and mode of hypothesis
commitment, design-matrix content hash, and software environment.

Date of pre-registration:

Design matrix, hypotheses, statistical tests, and success criteria were fixed prior to the confirmatory experiments; the design matrix is content-addressed by the SHA-256 below. No third-party (OSF/AsPredicted) timestamp was filed for this study; all analyses are reported alongside their pre-specified test statistics, and H1/H2/H3 are labeled explicitly throughout as “planned confirmatory analyses” rather than as timestamp-verified pre-registrations.

Design matrix SHA-256:

31a0dc197b46...df1728d6

Software:

Python 3.11, NumPy 2.3.5, SciPy 1.16.3, seed  $=$  42

Summary of the pre-registration.

In one sentence: we commit, before collecting data, to
testing whether a practitioner can build a good Retrieval-Augmented
Generation (RAG) pipeline by tuning components one at a time on a
small corpus, the way the literature implicitly assumes. The study
commits to three confirmatory hypotheses
(Section A.2.2), a 242-configuration D-optimal
fractional factorial design spanning 8 pipeline dimensions, 5
benchmark datasets (2,300 queries per configuration), and a
three-track evaluation protocol covering retrieval, end-to-end
generation, and hallucination resistance. To our knowledge, this is
the first pre-declared factorial evaluation of RAG pipeline
composition, $4.6\times$ the scale of the largest prior controlled
RAG study (Jin et al., 2025). We publish this document before
experimental data collection to establish hypothesis commitment and
invite community feedback on model selection, benchmark composition,
and statistical methodology.

## A.2.1 Motivation and Rationale

Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Lewis et al., 2020) has become the
dominant paradigm for grounding large language models in external
knowledge. A rich toolkit of individually validated
techniques, dense retrieval, hybrid sparse–dense fusion, cross-encoder
reranking, HyDE (Gao et al., 2023a), CRAG (Yan et al., 2024),
Self-RAG (Asai et al., 2024), is available to practitioners, yet
each technique is evaluated in isolation against a simple baseline.
Practitioners who combine several techniques simultaneously have no
empirical basis for predicting whether their gains add, cancel, or
amplify.

This is not merely a reporting gap.
Wang et al. (2024) observe that end-to-end RAG performance
depends on coordinated pipeline design rather than any single component;
Chen et al. (2026) confirm the presence of interaction effects by
showing that jointly optimized RAG components outperform independently
tuned ones. Yet neither work measures the sign, magnitude, or
domain-dependence of these interactions under controlled factorial
conditions, precisely the question a practitioner faces when choosing a
configuration for production.

Pre-registration prevents post-hoc hypothesis mining, a known problem in
ML benchmarking (Dodge et al., 2019; Bouthillier et al., 2021).
By committing to hypotheses, analysis plans, and exclusion criteria
before data collection, we ensure that our confirmatory findings are
distinguishable from exploratory ones. We additionally publish this
document to invite community input on experimental choices that remain
open (Section A.2.3).

## A.2.2 Pre-Registered Hypotheses

We pre-register three hypotheses that together ask whether RAG
pipelines can be designed compositionally. H1 asks the
structural question: do the eight pipeline components combine
additively, or do they interact non-trivially? If they interact, the
implicit assumption behind every single-component ablation in the
literature fails. H2 asks the design question:
assuming components do interact, can a single fixed configuration
still serve practitioners across the (faithfulness, latency, cost)
tradeoff space, or must each deployment hand-tune for its own
operating point? H3 asks the scaling question: do the
answers to H1 and H2 survive a $40\times$ scale-up of the corpus, or
are small-corpus best practices misleading at production scale?

Table 5: Summary of the three pre-registered hypotheses, their research
questions, what confirming each hypothesis would rule out, and the
pre-registered test statistic.

Research question

What confirming rejects

Pre-registered test

H1

Do components combine additively?

Single-component ablation as evidence

 $\eta^{2}\geq 0.20$ , Type-III ANOVA F-test

H2

Does a balanced configuration sweep a wider tradeoff envelope?

“You must pick one axis to optimize”

Hypervolume, Wilcoxon signed-rank

H3

Do RAG profile rankings flip with corpus scale?

Small-corpus ablations predict production deployment

Per-profile power-law slopes, bootstrap CIs, Kendall’s $\tau$ 

H1, Components do not combine additively.

Research question. Do the eight pipeline components combine
additively, or do non-trivial interactions arise that violate the
additivity assumption behind isolated-component ablations?

Pre-registered claim. Two-way interactions between the eight
factorial dimensions explain $\geq 20\%$ of the variance in
faithfulness (RAGAS, 0–1 scale) after main effects are removed.
Variance is reported as partial $\eta^{2}$ , the share of
residual variance (after main effects) attributed to interaction
terms, in a Type-III ANOVA, an ANOVA decomposition that
estimates each term’s contribution after partialling out all others
and is robust to unbalanced designs. The pre-registered 20% bar is
stricter than Cohen’s conventional “large effect” reference of
 $\eta^{2}_{\mathrm{partial}}\approx 0.14$ (Cohen, 2013).

Pre-registered test. F-test on the joint set of all 28
two-way interaction terms, $\alpha=0.05$ . Post-hoc Bonferroni
( $\alpha=0.05/28=0.00179$ ) per interaction pair.

What confirming and rejecting mean. Confirming H1
falsifies the additivity assumption behind most single-component RAG
ablations in the literature: a designer cannot tune one component in
isolation and trust the gain to compose with another.
Rejecting H1 (interaction terms contribute $<$ 5% of variance)
means RAG components are approximately additive within the precision
of our 242-config sweep, and isolated ablations remain a defensible
practice.

H2, A balanced operating point exists.

Research question. Given that components interact, can a
single fixed configuration sweep a wider region of the (faithfulness,
latency, cost) tradeoff space than a quality-only configuration, or
must practitioners give up an axis to gain another?

Pre-registered claim. The balanced operational
profile exhibits a strictly higher hypervolume indicator (HV)
than the max-reliable profile on $\geq 3$ of the 5 benchmark
datasets. Hypervolume is the multi-objective generalization of
area-under-the-curve: the volume of the
 $(\text{faithfulness}{\uparrow},\text{latency}{\downarrow},\text{cost}{\downarrow})$ 
region dominated by the profile’s per-query tradeoff cloud, with
larger meaning a better envelope.

Pre-registered test. Wilcoxon signed-rank, a non-parametric
paired-sample test that does not assume normality, on per-query
faithfulness scores (paired by query), Bonferroni-corrected across
the 5 datasets ( $\alpha=0.05/5=0.01$ per dataset). HV is
computed with the exact 3D algorithm; objectives are normalized to
 $[0,1]$ before the volume integration.

What confirming and rejecting mean. Confirming H2
says a single fixed configuration can serve practitioners who do not
want to give up an axis: published profile recipes (e.g.,
balanced) generalize across deployments.
Rejecting H2 (max-reliable dominates in $\geq 4/5$ 
datasets) means production RAG requires per-deployment tuning to its
own tradeoff preference, and our published profile recipes will not
generalize.

H3, Profile rankings flip with corpus scale.

Research question. Does the best-performing RAG profile at
small corpus (where most published RAG ablations live) remain the
best-performing profile at production scale, or do profile rankings
change with corpus size?

Pre-registered claim. Fitting NDCG@10 as a power law
 $\text{NDCG@10}(N)=a\cdot N^{b}$ separately for each of the four
operational profiles (speed, standard,
balanced, max-reliable), the cross-profile range
 $b_{\max}-b_{\min}$ exceeds $0.05$ per decade of corpus growth
(i.e., the steepest- and shallowest-degrading profiles differ by at
least $5\%$ relative NDCG@10 per $10\times$ corpus increase),
analogous to the saturation behavior observed in neural scaling
laws (Kaplan et al., 2020). Equivalently, the profile-rank
ordering at the smallest tested corpus ( $5$ K) differs from the
ordering at $200$ K on $\geq 1$ benchmark.

Pre-registered test. Per-profile nonlinear least-squares
fit (scipy.optimize.curve_fit); 95% bootstrap CIs on each
 $b$ from 10,000 resamples. Profile-pair slope differences tested
via permutation test (10,000 shuffles, $\alpha=0.05$ ) ,
a non-parametric significance test that constructs the null
distribution by random label shuffling, valid without distributional
assumptions. Profile-rank stability tested via Kendall’s $\tau$ 
between the $5$ K and $200$ K profile orderings.

What confirming and rejecting mean. Confirming H3
means small-corpus ablations cannot reliably predict production-scale
deployment choices: profile selection requires re-validation at the
deployment’s target corpus size, and the field’s reliance on
 $\leq 50$ K-corpus benchmarks is methodologically suspect for
deployment decisions. Rejecting H3 (slope range $\leq 0.02$ 
per decade, $\tau=1$ on every benchmark) means small-corpus
benchmarks generalize, and the small-corpus best practice is also the
large-corpus best practice.

## A.2.3 Experimental Design

Platform.

lasagna is a stage-isolated RAG evaluation platform: each
pipeline stage is implemented behind an abstract base class, and
configuration changes to one stage do not affect the execution of any
other stage. This architectural invariant guarantees that the output
of stage $i$ is a deterministic function of the output of stage $i{-}1$ 
and the configuration parameters $\bm{\theta}_{i}$ alone,
eliminating the coupling confounds that invalidate factorial
interpretation in conventional RAG implementations.

Candidate Models.

All configurations share a single generator LLM (held constant) and a
single evaluation judge. We list primary and alternative candidates
to invite community input before experiments begin.

Table 6: Model candidates. The generator is held constant across all
configurations; the judge evaluates all RAGAS metrics. Embedding
models are factorial dimension D1.

Role

Primary

Alternatives

Selection rationale

Generator

GPT-5.1 (OpenAI)

Claude Sonnet 4.5 (Anthropic), Gemini 2.0 (Google)

Held constant; cost vs. capability

Judge

GPT-5.1 (OpenAI)

Claude Sonnet 4.5 (Anthropic), Llama-3-70B (Meta)

Determinism at $T{=}0$ ; cost for ${\sim}$ 1.7M calls

Embedding

MiniLM-L6 (384d), BGE-M3 (1024d), E5-large (1024d), BGE-large (1024d)

Span lightweight to SOTA multilingual

Reranker

ms-marco-MiniLM-L6-v2

BGE-reranker-v2

Standard BEIR cross-encoder baseline

We welcome suggestions on model selection before experiments begin.
In particular, we are open to: (1) alternative generator models that
would increase reproducibility (e.g., open-weight models),
(2) additional judge models for cross-validation of faithfulness
scores, and (3) embedding models that would expand the capacity range.

The full factorial comprises
 $4\times 4\times 4\times 3\times 5\times 4\times 2\times 4=30{,}720$ configurations.

D-Optimal Design.

We reduce 30,720 candidates to 242 configurations using a
D-optimal fractional factorial generated by Fedorov coordinate
exchange (Fedorov et al., 1972; Atkinson et al., 2007). The model
matrix includes 1 intercept $+$ 22 main-effect $+$ 209 two-way
interaction columns $=$ 232 parameters, with 10 residual degrees of
freedom. Effect coding ensures orthogonal main
effects (Box et al., 2005; Montgomery, 2021). All 232 model
terms are estimable (rank verification), and no two-way interaction
correlates with any representative three-way interaction at
 $|r|>0.30$ .

Design matrix SHA-256:
31a0dc197b46a90819fc9178ea06668444ad9617ff4c8a86f3d557a4df1728d6
(seed  $=$  42).

These five benchmarks span general web (DS1), multi-hop reasoning
(DS2), financial opinion QA (DS3), scientific claim verification
(DS4), and legal contract analysis (DS5), covering diversity in domain,
relevance granularity, and query
complexity (Thakur et al., 2021).

Metrics.

Retrieval: NDCG@10, MRR, Recall@100.
End-to-end: faithfulness, answer relevance
(RAGAS v0.4 (Es et al., 2024), judged by GPT-5.1 at $T{=}0$ ),
exact match, token F1, p50/p95 latency, $/query.
Multi-objective: hypervolume indicator (HV) in the
faithfulness–latency–cost space, reference point
 $(0,10{,}000\,\text{ms},\$1.00)$ .

## A.2.4 Statistical Analysis Plan

H1 (additivity).

Plain reading: fit one regression that includes every
pipeline component plus all 28 of their pairwise interactions, then
ask how much of the leftover variance the interactions explain.
Model:

faithfulness ˜ D1+D2+D3+D4+D5+D6+D7+D8
 + D1:D2+D1:D3+...+D7:D8 (28 two-way terms)

Test statistic: F for the joint set of all interaction terms,
 $F(209,\,n_{\text{resid}})$ . Effect size: partial
 $\eta^{2}=\text{SS}_{\text{int}}/(\text{SS}_{\text{int}}+\text{SS}_{\text{resid}})$ ; threshold $\geq 0.20$ . Post-hoc:
Bonferroni ( $\alpha=0.05/28=0.00179$ ) per interaction pair, so
that any single interaction we flag as “large” has been corrected
for the 28 simultaneous comparisons.

H2 (a balanced recipe).

Plain reading: for each query, compare the
balanced profile’s three-objective tradeoff cloud against
the max-reliable profile’s, and check on how many of the
five benchmarks balanced sweeps a strictly larger
hypervolume. Wilcoxon signed-rank test on per-query faithfulness
(balanced vs. max-reliable, paired by query).
Bonferroni across 5 datasets ( $\alpha=0.01$ per dataset) so that
any single “win” has been corrected for testing on five datasets
in parallel. HV computed with the exact 3D algorithm; objectives
normalized to $[0,1]$ before integration.

H3 (scaling).

Plain reading: fit a separate scaling curve for each of the
four profiles and check whether the steepest- and shallowest-
degrading profiles drift apart fast enough that the small-corpus
winner is no longer the large-corpus winner. Per-profile nonlinear
least-squares fit of $\text{NDCG@10}(N)=a\cdot N^{b}$ for each of
speed, standard, balanced,
max-reliable using scipy.optimize.curve_fit.
95% bootstrap CIs on each $b$ from 10,000 resamples. Confirmed
if (i) the cross-profile slope range $b_{\max}-b_{\min}$ exceeds
 $0.05$ per decade of corpus growth, and (ii) Kendall’s $\tau<1$ 
between the $5$ K and $200$ K profile orderings on $\geq 1$ benchmark.
Pairwise slope differences tested via permutation test (10,000
shuffles, $\alpha=0.05$ ).

Power.

For $n=500$ queries with $\alpha_{\text{corrected}}=0.00179$ ,
minimum detectable effect at 80% power:
 $d\approx 0.168$ (Cohen’s $d$ ).

## A.2.5 Exclusion Criteria

1. 

Queries exceeding 30 s wall-clock are excluded and counted.

2. 

Configurations with $>$ 10% null-retrieval queries are marked
failed and excluded from the ANOVA.

3. 

Corpus sizes where index construction fails (OOM or $>$ 24 h)
are excluded from the scaling curve fit.

4. 

Configurations require $\geq$ 200 valid queries (of 2,300)
to enter the ANOVA.

This pre-registration is published before experimental data collection

## A.3 Design Matrix

The full 242-row D-optimal design matrix (experiments/design/factorial_design_matrix.csv) is available from the authors on request.
SHA-256: 31a0dc197b46a90819fc9178ea06668444ad9617ff4c8a86f3d557a4df1728d6.

## A.4 D-Optimal Design Generation

Algorithm.

Fedorov coordinate exchange (Fedorov et al., 1972; Atkinson et al., 2007)
is a greedy row-exchange algorithm that starts from a random 242-row
subset of the 30,720-row full-factorial candidate set and iteratively
swaps one in-design row for the candidate row that most increases the
D-criterion

 $\phi_{D}(\mathbf{X})=\det(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}),$ 

(1)

terminating when no single-row swap raises the determinant. The model
matrix $\mathbf{X}$ is $242\times 232$ and encodes one intercept, 22
main-effect contrasts, and 209 two-way interaction contrasts under
effect coding (factor levels mapped to $\pm 1$ rather than the
 $0/1$ of dummy coding, so main effects are orthogonal to the intercept).
A custom Python implementation (experiments/design/generate_design_matrix.py, available from the authors on request) reproduces the
matrix bit-identically at seed $=42$ .

Table 10: The eight factorial dimensions. # Levels is the number of
configuration options per dimension. Baseline levels (marked ∗)
are the simplest, lowest-cost options used when other features are
toggled. Descriptions, citations, and implementations appear in
Appendix K.

# Levels

Generation QA

Size 242.

232 estimable parameters plus 10 residual degrees of freedom: enough
to support $F$ -tests for each interaction term while staying tractable
relative to the 30,720-candidate full factorial.

Aliasing threshold.

We adopt $|r|>0.30$ between any representative three-way interaction
and any two-way term as a diagnostic cutoff rather than a hard
orthogonality guarantee; the stricter $|r|<0.10$ – $0.20$ common in
pure screening designs was not achievable at 242 rows over this
candidate set. Given a 232-parameter model with only 28 two-way
terms under test and strong main effects absorbing most variance,
residual three-way correlations at this level do not substantively
confound the reported interaction estimates; the executed-subset
sensitivity check in Section A.6 gives a second line of
robustness.

GraphRAG ablation (isolated).

Before committing GraphRAG to the 242-run factorial we ran an isolated
four-configuration retrieval ablation
(private_eval/retrieval, small corpus, March 2026).

Table 11: Isolated GraphRAG ablation on the retrieval track. Graph
depth increases latency without improving NDCG@10 over the non-graph
baseline.

Configuration
NDCG@10
Latency (ms)

no_graph_baseline
0.4780
486

graph_1hop
0.4774
1,021

graph_2hop
0.4664
1,138

graph_3hop
0.4661
1,210

No graph configuration improves over the baseline, and NDCG@10 decreases
monotonically as graph-hop depth grows (1-hop $\to$ 3-hop, $-0.011$ ).
One plausible explanation, consistent with the retrieval–faithfulness
asymmetry observed in the full factorial (Section 5), is
that graph-derived structural context may aid answer grounding while
diluting passage-level similarity signals on these domain-focused
corpora, where lexical and semantic retrieval already locate the gold
evidence. We did not evaluate the generation-track faithfulness effect,
so this is a hypothesis rather than a claim. Given the lack of NDCG@10
signal and the ${\sim}$ 14-hour per-combo ingestion cost, we excluded
GraphRAG from the 167-run factorial (Table 10, D3) and the
scaling runs (Section 5).

## A.5 Aliasing Structure

No three-factor interaction is aliased with any two-factor interaction
at $|r|>0.30$ . The aliasing structure is reproducible with experiments/design/check_aliasing.py (available from the authors on request; implementation notes in Section A.4).

This appendix documents all deviations between the pre-registered protocol
(archived at https://github.com/dolev31/Lasagna-Retrieval-Augmented-Generation-Compositionality) and the experiments we actually
executed. Table 12 summarizes each change, its status, and
its implication for the reported findings.

their original inferential status; “Exploratory” items are presented
without confirmatory $p$ -values.

H1: $\eta^{2}\geq 20\%$ from 2-way interactions (faithfulness); pre-registered as joint 28-pair $F$ -test and per-pair Bonferroni ( $\alpha{=}0.05/28$ ), with the $0.20$ bar justified per-term (Cohen large-effect)

Both pre-registered procedures executed: joint 28-two-way ANOVA (two-track, per-sample + aggregated, nested-SSR joint SS) and per-pair Bonferroni across all 28 pairs (Section D.3)

Not confirmed at pre-registered $20\%$ joint threshold; pre-registered per-pair Bonferroni identifies D5 $\times$ D8 as large-effect follow-up. On the pre-registration’s own variance-floor decision rule ( $\eta^{2}<5\%$ $\rightarrow$ reject H1 / additivity holds), the executed E1 $\eta^{2}=0.033$ and per-sample joint $\eta^{2}=0.040$ sit on the additivity-holds side; a parallel significance test rejects strict-zero interactions ( $p{<}10^{-9}$ aggregated). We report both readings (see Appendix A)

Joint per-config ( $n{=}113$ ) unidentifiable (232 params); per-sample $\eta^{2}{=}0.040$ retracted as pseudo-replication; aggregated $\eta^{2}{=}0.428$ sensitivity only (BIC $\Delta{=}+347$ favors mains-only). Pre-registered per-pair follow-up: D5 $\times$ D8 $\eta^{2}{=}0.201$ , $F(12,\,306){=}6.40$ , $p{=}3.8\!\times\!10^{-10}$ (Section D.3).

H2: balanced has higher faithfulness–latency–cost hypervolume than max-reliable on at least three of five benchmarks

Four-profile retrieval–faithfulness–response-time comparison on one mixed-benchmark slice, with three seeded runs and reference-time sensitivity

Not confirmed as pre-registered; informative executed comparison

The five-benchmark test was not executed. On the available SQuAD v2 / MuSiQue / HotpotQA mixture, no profile leads every metric. At the fixed $30$  s reference, standard has the highest joint HV ( $0.523$ ); within the agentic pair, balanced has $4.5\%$ higher NDCG@10 and $13.5\%$ lower mean response time than max-reliable, with a $0.005$ faithfulness difference (Tables 2 and 39).

H3: profile rankings flip with corpus scale

Per-profile power-law fits across 5K–200K (3 seeded runs at 25K–200K)

Supported

Cross-profile slope range $b_{\max}-b_{\min}\approx 0.10$ per decade ( $\geq 0.05$ threshold cleared); rank crossover visible from 25K, statistically significant at 200K (Welch’s $t$ , $p{=}0.004$ ); intermediate 50K/100K $p>0.17$ 

Amendment rationale.

The original 242-config factorial assumed access to a dedicated GPU cluster
and a corpus pipeline capable of ingesting up to 1M documents. During
execution, two constraints materialized: (1) all LLM inference was routed
through an enterprise API gateway to GPT-5.1, which imposed rate
limits that made concurrent large-scale runs infeasible, and (2) our Milvus Lite
instance required fresh database resets between embedding-model groups to
avoid dimension-mismatch errors. We executed 167 of 242 planned
configurations (69%) across all 8 dimensions, 4 embedding models, 4
chunking strategies, 3 of 4 enrichment levels (GraphRAG excluded due
to entity-extraction cost of ${\sim}$ 14 hours per ingestion combo), 3
retrieval modes, 5 query augmentation strategies, 4 reranking modes, 2
compression settings, and 4 agentic gate levels. The 167-configuration
subset preserves all 22 main effects and enables estimation of all 28
two-way interactions via ANOVA, though with reduced power for the
missing GraphRAG enrichment level. Twelve additional configurations
failed due to Milvus Lite query-limit errors and are excluded.
For the joint 28-interaction fit we compute SS ${}_{\text{int}}$ as a
nested-SSR difference (SSR ${}_{\text{mains}}-$ SSR ${}_{\text{full}}$ )
rather than as an additive sum of per-pair Type-II SS: the latter
double-counts shared variance across rank-deficient interactions
(df sum $172{+}226{>}n{-}1{=}338$ , arithmetically impossible) and
inflated the reported $\eta^{2}$ from $0.428$ to $0.546$ .

## Deviations from the Preregistration

This section consolidates every deviation between the pre-registered
protocol and the executed study. Each
row of Table 12 above enumerates a scientific
deviation; the list below adds executional and reporting deviations
that are not obvious from that table.

• 

Configuration count: 242 planned $\rightarrow$ 167
factorial configurations executed (69% of the design), plus 138
non-factorial ablation configurations; shortfall driven by rate
limits on the enterprise API gateway and Milvus Lite
dimension-mismatch resets between embedding-model groups.

• 

Enrichment dimension (D3) reduction: 4 planned
levels $\rightarrow$ 3 executed. The graphrag level was
dropped after an isolated ablation showed no NDCG@10 gain
against the entity-extraction cost ( $\sim$ 14 hours per ingestion
combo); this is a scientific deviation not previously
surfaced in Table 12.

• 

Timeouts: 12 additional configurations were
attempted and timed out on Milvus Lite query limits; excluded
from all analyses.

• 

Per-configuration query count: preregistered budget
of 2,300 queries per configuration $\rightarrow$ executed 93
queries per retrieval configuration and $43$ generation / $56$ 
retrieval queries per SOTA-profile E2E configuration.
The reduction is a straight
budget cut driven by GPT-5.1 rate limits; power calculations
for the H1 factorial were re-run at the reduced counts and are
reported in Section D.3.

• 

Benchmark substitution: preregistered MS MARCO and
CUAD were replaced with TriviaQA and BEIR/FiQA. MS MARCO was
dropped because its passage license conflicts with our
reproducibility artifact policy; CUAD was replaced with FiQA
(which supplies a comparable domain-specific IR task) to keep
coverage of financial/regulatory text without the CUAD license
constraint.

• 

H2 corpus scope: the pre-registered H2 Pareto
protocol specified a multi-benchmark hypervolume comparison; the
executed H2 headline reports a joint NDCG@10, faithfulness, and
response-time comparison on one SQuAD v2 / MuSiQue / HotpotQA
mixture ( $43$ generation / $56$ retrieval queries per seed, three
seeded runs).
This mixture emerged as the practical H2 test after the
MS MARCO / CUAD substitution above eliminated the two
pre-registered benchmarks that would have anchored the
multi-benchmark comparison. The executed comparison therefore does
not confirm the pre-registered cross-benchmark claim; the original
multi-benchmark
grid is fully specified in Section D.7 but was not
executed. A separate
post-hoc replication of the H2 pattern on the same three benchmark
families at 100K and 200K is reported descriptively in
Section D.8; it is supplementary and
does not substitute for the pre-registered multi-benchmark grid. The
balanced profile should therefore be read as a
candidate starting point pending validation on the target corpus,
exactly as the H2 verdict box states.

• 

Scaling-fit form: preregistered power-law fit
 $a\cdot N^{b}$ $\rightarrow$ executed log-linear fit
 $a\cdot\log_{10}(N)+b$ (see the “Deviation from the
pre-registered fit form” paragraph in
Section B.3.9).

• 

H1 test statistic: the pre-registered joint
232-parameter 28-two-way $F$ -test is unidentifiable on
the 113-configuration data (232 parameters vs. 339 rows,
rank-deficient Plackett-Burman $\times$ screener cells). We
executed the pre-registered per-pair Bonferroni correction
( $\alpha/28$ ) instead and added an adequately-powered
focused-confirmatory experiment E1 (see Section D.9)
post-hoc as a replacement for the joint test.

• 

H1 decision-rule interpretation
(variance-floor vs. significance-floor): the
pre-registration’s rejection rule is stated as
“interaction terms contribute $<$ 5% of variance $\rightarrow$ 
reject H1 / additivity holds within tolerance,” which is a
variance threshold. The variance and significance criteria
yield different formal readings. Under the pre-registration’s
variance rule, the executed $\eta^{2}=0.033$ (E1) and
 $\eta^{2}=0.040$ (per-sample joint) both sit on the
additivity-holds side of the 5% floor. Under the parallel
significance criterion ( $p<0.05$ ), strict-zero additivity is
rejected, but the effect sizes are small. Both readings appear in
the reconciliation table and H1 verdict box and support the same
practical conclusion (main effects dominate; cell-level
residuals are non-trivial but small in aggregate).

• 

Distractor-relevance audit: performed post-hoc
( $n{=}100$ LLM-audited pairs; see Section B.3.5):
false-negative rate $0.0\%$ (95% CI $[0.0\%,0.0\%]$ ), any-signal
rate $9.0\%$ (all partial, biomedical-concentrated). Given
 $\text{FN}{<}5\%$ , the audit supports treating the H3 rank-flip
as robust on the audited splits and the reported scaling
degradations as an upper bound (see
the “Distractor-relevance caveat” paragraph in
Section 5).

• 

Corpus fingerprints: SHA-256 fingerprints for the
100K/200K corpus files are unavailable
(Section B.3.4); the draw is reproducible from the seed-42 sampling script (available from the authors on request), so reconstruction relies on the
recipe rather than content-addressed corpus files.

Estimability of the 167-run subset.

The 167 executed configurations cover all factor levels in 7 of 8
dimensions and 3 of 4 levels in the enrichment dimension (D3).
All 22 main effects and all 28 two-way interactions are estimable
from this subset. However, the design is unbalanced (cell sizes range
from 1 to 7), which affects the precision of individual interaction
estimates. We report Type I ANOVA sums of squares and note that
partial $\eta^{2}$ values should be interpreted with caution in
unbalanced designs. We report Type I sums of squares for the
28-interaction scan and verify the key interaction
(QueryAug $\times$ AgenticGate) with Type III SS ( $\eta^{2}=0.191$ ,
 $F=5.47$ , $p<0.001$ ), confirming robustness to factor ordering.
For the pre-registered joint 28-two-way test
(Section D.3) we use Type II sums of squares, the
Type III decomposition is singular in the sparse Plackett-Burman
 $\times$ screener cells, and on the reconstructed long-form table
(113 configs $\times$ 3 benchmarks $=339$ rows) the D5 $\times$ D8
sanity refit gives $\eta^{2}_{\text{partial}}=0.201$ ,
 $F(12,\,306)=6.40$ , $p=3.8\!\times\!10^{-10}$ , consistent within
residual df with the manuscript’s cited $\eta^{2}=0.191$ ,
 $F(12,\,279)=5.47$ from the 122-config analysis.
Restricting to the 3 enrichment levels present in both designs
(excluding GraphRAG), the D-efficiency of the 167-row executed
subset is 99.3% relative to the matching 179-row sub-design,
indicating negligible power loss for main-effect estimation.
Interaction-level power depends on cell occupancy and is lower
for pairs involving the enrichment dimension (3 vs. 4 levels).

80% of pre-registered budget

Cost drivers.

Generation evaluation was the largest single cost category ($815, 32% of
total), driven by agentic pipeline configurations that invoke the LLM
10–15 times per query for iterative refinement, hallucination checking, and
quality gating. RAGAS evaluation was the second largest ($590, 23%),
as each faithfulness judgment requires decomposing the answer into atomic
claims and verifying each against the retrieved context. Switching to
concise answer prompts (one sentence per answer) reduced the number of
atomic claims per sample and lowered RAGAS costs by approximately 15%
relative to our initial verbose-answer runs.

Accessibility.

The initial experimental phases cost $2,553 (Table 13);
the subsequent factorial ANOVAs and remaining sweeps brought the API
total to $5,200 (full timeline in Table 14), with
 ${\sim}$ $370 in local GPU compute valued at cloud rates pushing the
grand total to ${\sim}$ $5,600, within the range of a single
conference registration fee and well below compute budgets for
comparable studies using dedicated GPU clusters. Approximately
90% of this cost is LLM API usage (generation and judging).
Local compute (Apple M3 Max, 36 GB unified memory) contributed an
estimated ${\sim}$ 120 GPU-hours for MPS-accelerated embedding and
 ${\sim}$ 40 CPU-hours for chunking, indexing, and BM25 index construction
for the initial experiments, plus an additional ${\sim}$ 34 hours of
MPS GPU time for the 167-configuration retrieval factorial experiment
(48 ingestion combos across 4 embedding models with RAPTOR and
contextual-retrieval enrichment). Valued at cloud GPU rates
($1.50/hr), local compute totals ${\sim}$ 290.
The retrieval factorial consumed an estimated $800–1,200 in
LLM API costs (HyDE, RAPTOR summarization,
contextual-retrieval, and agentic pipeline calls).
The generation factorial (122 configs $\times$ 56 queries = 6,832
evaluations) added ${\sim}$ 80 hours of wall-clock time
( ${\sim}$ 25 GPU-hours MPS embedding + ${\sim}$ 55 hours API-bound
RAGAS judging) and approximately $600–900 in API costs
(answer generation + 3 RAGAS judge calls per sample).

## Appendix B Datasets, Corpora, and Evaluation Protocol

## B.1 Evaluation Dataset Design

We designed a three-track evaluation protocol that separates retrieval quality
measurement from generation quality measurement and hallucination resistance
testing. Each track uses a purpose-built corpus to ensure that the measured
outcome reflects the intended evaluation dimension.

Sampling strategy.

Each configuration is evaluated on 149 non-overlapping queries: 93 for
retrieval (stratified across 5 BEIR domains) and 56 for generation
(stratified across 3 QA benchmarks). These representative subsets were
drawn via stratified random sampling from the full benchmark pools
(which collectively contain thousands of queries) to balance domain
coverage and difficulty distribution against the $\sim$ $4,700
compute budget required for factorial-scale evaluation (167
configurations $\times$ 149 queries $=24{,}883$ retrieval evaluations
plus 6,832 generation evaluations). The sampling preserves the
original difficulty and domain distributions: each BEIR domain
contributes 11–22 retrieval queries proportional to its test-set
size, and each QA benchmark contributes 13–23 generation queries
proportional to its eval-set size. While larger query sets would
reduce per-configuration variance, the factorial design compensates
by aggregating signal across 167 independent configurations,
yielding 71,643 total evaluations, sufficient for main-effect
and targeted-interaction testing at 80% power for $\eta^{2}\geq 0.03$ 
(Section 5).

## B.1.1 Track Overview

Table 15 summarizes the three evaluation tracks. Tracks 1
and 2 share a common base corpus; Track 2 augments it with gold passages so
the retriever can surface ground-truth evidence, while Track 3
deliberately withholds gold passages to test grounding under adversarial
conditions.

Table 15: Three-track evaluation protocol (exploratory). Doc counts are
approximate and vary slightly across stratified splits.

Track

Corpus

Queries

Metrics

Purpose

1: Retrieval

BEIR docs + distractors (5,132 docs)

BEIR qrels (93 eval)

NDCG@10, MRR, Recall@100

Measure retrieval quality

2: E2E Generation

Track 1 corpus + injected gold passages (5,566 docs)

QA with ground-truth answers (56 eval)

EM, Token F1, Faithfulness, NDCG@10

Measure end-to-end answer quality

3: Hallucination

Track 1 corpus (no gold passages)

Same QA queries as Track 2

Faithfulness only

Test grounding without gold evidence

Figure 5: Benchmark composition of the unified multi-domain corpus.
Left: the retrieval track aggregates five BEIR benchmarks
(NFCorpus, FiQA, SciFact, ArguAna, HotpotQA) into a single
5,132-document pool so every query is retrieved under cross-domain
distractor pressure. Right: the E2E generation track embeds
QA pairs from three reading-comprehension and multi-hop benchmarks
(SQuAD v2, HotpotQA, MuSiQue) in the same pool, augmented with
gold-passage documents to guarantee answerability. Scaling
experiments (Section 5) inject up to 45,000 additional
distractors from the same five domains in controlled increments.

## B.1.2 Corpus Assembly

Base corpus.

We drew documents from five BEIR domain subsets: nfcorpus,
fiqa, scifact, arguana, and hotpotqa.
These subsets span biomedical, financial, scientific, argumentative, and
multi-hop reasoning domains, providing heterogeneous retrieval challenges.

Distractor documents.

For each benchmark we sampled 500 distractor documents from held-out BEIR
collections that do not overlap with the benchmark’s relevance judgments.
Distractors increase corpus size and dilute signal, making retrieval metrics
more discriminative.

Gold passage extraction.

For the end-to-end generation track (Track 2), we extracted context passages
from each QA sample by splitting on double newlines (\n\n). Each
extracted passage was assigned a document identifier of the form
gold:{benchmark}:{sample_id}:{passage_idx} and ingested
alongside the base corpus. With 500 distractors per benchmark, gold passages
represent roughly 8% of the Track 2 corpus; this ratio is substantially lower
than the 84% we observed in early pilots that used only 100 distractors,
yielding a more realistic retrieval challenge.

Stratified split.

We partitioned all queries into 60/20/20 train/eval/test splits, stratified by
source benchmark, so that each split contains a proportional representation of
every domain. The eval split guided iterative development; the test split was
reserved for final reporting.

## B.1.3 Design Rationale

We separate the three tracks for the following reasons:

1. 

Retrieval track. Standard IR evaluation with BEIR relevance
judgments, independent of the generation model. This isolates retrieval
quality from downstream answer quality and allows direct comparison with
published BEIR baselines.

2. 

E2E generation track. Tests the full pipeline, including
answer generation. Gold passages are present in the corpus so that the
retriever can find them; failure to produce a correct answer
therefore reflects pipeline limitations rather than corpus gaps.

3. 

Hallucination track. Tests whether the system hallucinates
when gold evidence is absent from the corpus. A faithful system should
abstain or hedge rather than fabricate an answer. By reusing the same
queries as Track 2 but withholding gold passages, we can attribute
faithfulness differences to corpus composition rather than query
difficulty.

Tracks 2 and 3.
