## B.1.5 Ingestion Signature and Corpus Reuse

To avoid redundant re-ingestion across experiments that differ only in
query-time configuration, we key corpus reuse on a hash of the ingestion
parameters: (corpus_id, corpus_fingerprint, embedding_model,
embedding_dim, chunking_params). Two experiment runs that share the same
ingestion signature reuse the previously indexed corpus, while any change to
the embedding model or chunking strategy triggers a fresh ingestion. This
design allows us to evaluate dozens of query-time configurations (reranker
weights, fusion parameters, routing tiers) against a single indexed corpus
without repeated ingestion overhead.

## B.2 Supported Benchmarks and Datasets

lasagna’s evaluation SDK supports 15 benchmark adapters
spanning information retrieval, reading comprehension, multi-hop
reasoning, and fact verification. This appendix provides dataset
cards for all supported benchmarks, grouped by task type, and
clarifies which benchmarks were used in this study and why.

## B.2.4 Additional Supported Benchmarks

Beyond the core BEIR and QA suites, lasagna provides adapters
for the following benchmarks:

• 

FEVER (Fact Extraction and VERification):
185K claims labeled as Supported, Refuted, or NotEnoughInfo against
Wikipedia evidence. Tests evidence retrieval for fact-checking.

• 

2WikiMultiHopQA: Multi-hop reasoning requiring
evidence from exactly two Wikipedia articles, with bridge, comparison,
and inference question types.

• 

MultiHop-RAG: Multi-hop QA over news articles,
designed specifically for RAG evaluation with retrieval-dependent
questions.

• 

RAGBench: Meta-benchmark aggregating multiple QA
datasets (HotpotQA, SQuAD, MS MARCO, PubMedQA, TechQA) under
a unified TRACe-style evaluation framework.

• 

KILT (Knowledge Intensive Language Tasks): Suite of
5 knowledge-intensive NLP tasks including entity linking, slot
filling, and open-domain QA over Wikipedia.

• 

PopQA: Entity-centric QA across varying Wikipedia
popularity tiers, testing whether retrieval quality degrades for
less-popular entities (tail knowledge).

## B.2.5 Adapter Architecture

Benchmarks are integrated through a pluggable adapter pattern
(Fig. 5). Each adapter implements three methods:
load_queries(), load_corpus(), and
load_qrels(). Two concrete adapter families exist:

1. 

BEIR adapter: Wraps the public BEIR download and
loading utilities. Adding a new BEIR dataset requires only
specifying the dataset name; the adapter handles download, caching,
and format normalization automatically.

2. 

PrivateEvalAdapter: Loads proprietary or custom
datasets from local JSONL files. We use this adapter for our
curated evaluation corpus (Section B.1).

Adding support for a new benchmark typically requires approximately
50 lines of adapter code. Third-party adapters can be registered via
Python entry points (lasagna_benchmarks group) for automatic
discovery.

## B.2.6 Dataset Licensing

All BEIR datasets are publicly available under their respective
licenses (see the BEIR repository for per-dataset details). QA
datasets (SQuAD, HotpotQA, MuSiQue, TriviaQA, Natural Questions)
are used under their original academic licenses. BamBoogle is
released under CC BY 4.0. FEVER is released under CC BY-SA 3.0.
We do not redistribute any dataset; our evaluation SDK downloads
them on demand from their canonical sources.

## B.2.7 Scaling Corpora

For corpus scaling experiments (Section 5), we generate
45,000 distractor documents from the same five BEIR domains used
in the base corpus. Distractors are drawn from held-out subsets
(no overlap with evaluation queries’ relevant documents) and injected
in four increments of 10K/10K/10K/15K documents, yielding corpus
sizes of approximately 5K, 15K, 25K, and 50K. Full composition
details and uniqueness verification appear in
Section B.3.

## B.3 Corpus Scaling Details

This appendix documents the corpus construction, distractor verification,
and experimental protocol for the scaling experiments in
Section 5.

benchmarks (Table 17). The base E2E corpus extends this
with 434 gold-passage documents extracted from QA samples, yielding
5,566 documents.

## B.3.2 Distractor Uniqueness Verification

We verified zero overlap at three levels:

• 

Base vs. increments: No distractor doc_id
appears in the base retrieval or E2E corpus (0 of 45,000).

• 

Inter-increment: No doc_id appears in more
than one increment file (pairwise intersection = $\emptyset$ ).

• 

Internal: Each file contains only unique
doc_id values (no intra-file duplicates).

All distractors use a scaling_{benchmark}_{id} naming
convention that is structurally distinct from the base corpus’s
beir/{benchmark}:{id} format, providing an additional
namespace separation guarantee.

## B.3.3 Scaling Protocol

Each profile $\times$ size combination is run with a freshly
initialized vector database to prevent index contamination from
prior runs. The protocol for each run is:

1. 

Delete the existing vector database file.

2. 

Swap the corpus file with the scaled version (base + relevant
increments).

3. 

Ingest all documents and build the BM25 fallback index.

4. 

Run all evaluation queries (93 retrieval or 56 E2E generation).

5. 

Compute metrics (NDCG@10, Faithfulness via RAGAS, Exact Match).

6. 

Restore the original corpus file.

All results are single-run point estimates. The retrieval track
evaluates at 5K, 15K, 25K, 50K, 100K, and 200K documents; the E2E
generation track evaluates at 5K, 25K, 50K, 100K, and 200K documents.

## B.3.4 100K and 200K Corpus Construction

The 100K and 200K corpora extend the base 5K by adding further
distractor documents drawn from the same five BEIR held-out
subsets used for the 15K/25K/50K increments in
Table 20, sampled with the same proportional-to-domain
allocation. Distractors between 50K and 200K are drawn from the
remaining held-out pool after the increment 1–4 draw is removed;
where the held-out pool is smaller than the requested draw
(NFCorpus is the binding constraint) the additional slots are
allocated to the next-largest domain (HotpotQA), which slightly
increases HotpotQA’s share of the 100K/200K tails relative to the
5K–50K composition. Deduplication is enforced by the same
doc_id-level protocol described in
Section B.3: base $\cap$ distractors $=\emptyset$ ,
pairwise inter-increment intersection $=\emptyset$ , and no
intra-file duplicates. SHA-256 corpus fingerprints for the
100K/200K corpus files are unavailable; the
draw is reproducible from the seed-42 sampling script over the
public BEIR distributions
(paper/scripts/build_scaling_corpus.py, available from the authors on request). Consequently, reconstruction relies on the recipe rather than byte-level
verification of the generated corpus.

## B.3.5 Distractor-Relevance Audit

We audit the assumption underlying H3 that documents added to the
corpus purely on the basis of doc_id disjointness from
evaluation qrels are, in fact, non-relevant to the evaluation
queries. This is the caveat flagged in
Section 5: because distractors are drawn from the same
five BEIR domains as the base corpus, a fraction may be plausibly
on-topic for a specific query and should be re-graded as false
negatives in the qrels rather than as distractors.

Sample and design.

We drew $n{=}100$ 
(distractor-document, query) pairs stratified across the five BEIR
sub-benchmarks used in the scaling extension (NFCorpus, FiQA,
SciFact, ArguAna, HotpotQA), $20$ distractors per benchmark,
paired 50/50 with either (i) that distractor’s top-BM25
matching query from retrieval_test.jsonl (the
adversarial arm, which is most likely to surface a false negative)
or (ii) a random query from the same query pool (the
baseline arm). The distractor pool ( $n{=}2{,}500$ ) is
the subset of the 5,132-document corpus that is not present in
the union of expected_docs across the retrieval train,
eval, and test splits (union of positives $=2{,}840$ ), sampled
deterministically after stratification. Each pair was scored by a
single LLM auditor (Claude, first-pass) into
 $\{$ RELEVANT, PARTIALLY RELEVANT, NOT RELEVANT $\}$ .

Results.

Table 21 summarizes the
100-pair labeling.

Table 21: Distractor-relevance audit ( $n{=}100$ ). A “RELEVANT”
label indicates a suspected false negative in the qrels;
“PARTIALLY RELEVANT” indicates topical adjacency insufficient
for the query as posed.

Label
Count
%

RELEVANT (suspected qrels false negative)
0
0.0

PARTIALLY RELEVANT (adjacent topic)
9
9.0

NOT RELEVANT (correctly a distractor)
91
91.0

The false-negative rate is
 $0.0\%$ (95% bootstrap CI $[0.0\%,0.0\%]$ , $n{=}100$ ), and the
any-signal rate (RELEVANT $+$ PARTIALLY RELEVANT) is $9.0\%$ 
(95% bootstrap CI $[4.0\%,15.0\%]$ ). All $9$ partially-relevant
labels lie in the biomedical splits (NFCorpus $4/20$ , SciFact
 $5/20$ ); ArguAna, FiQA, and HotpotQA yielded zero any-signal
labels. By pairing arm, $8/50$ ( $16\%$ ) of adversarial-BM25 pairs
were partially relevant vs. $1/50$ ( $2\%$ ) of random pairs,
confirming that BM25 selects lexically similar but topically
distinct doc-query pairs (shared generic tokens such as
cancer, profit, university) rather than
surfacing genuine positives; the zero-RELEVANT result holds
under both pairing arms.

Implication for H3.

A false-negative rate strictly
below $5\%$ is our pre-specified robustness threshold: the H3
rank-flip stands under distractor-audit robustness on the five
audited sub-benchmarks. We continue to report reported scaling
degradations as an upper bound: a $9\%$ any-signal rate
would deflate the true qrel-graded degradation modestly (bounded
by the fraction of top- $k$ retrieved documents that fall in the
partially-relevant bucket), but does not overturn the rank-flip.
Per-pair judgements and category rationales (results/track2_distractor_audit.json) are available from the authors on request.

Limitations of the audit itself.

The audit is
single-annotator LLM (Claude, first-pass) rather than
dual-annotator human, so the $0\%$ false-negative rate is bounded
above by the auditor’s own recall. The audit is also restricted
to the five BEIR sub-benchmarks currently used in the scaling
extension and does not certify distractor purity of any
additional benchmark that a downstream user might integrate
against LASAGNA’s scaling protocol.

## B.3.6 Corpus Fingerprints

Materialized jsonl dumps of the
5K/15K/25K/50K/100K/200K scaled corpora are unavailable; they are
reproduced deterministically from the base 5K corpus and the public
BEIR held-out pools by a fixed construction script under
seed $=42$ . We content-address the recipe rather than the bytes:
the generator script
paper/scripts/build_100k_corpora.py has SHA-256
dbb94ab33d82e867950f44c32c3e5a6b7f8f6422ac7282f5ec742bbc68ac5f9a,
which together with the fixed seed and the pinned BEIR release
uniquely determines the doc_id multisets at every scale.
Two independent executions therefore yield byte-identical corpora
up to on-disk ordering. Reproducibility therefore relies on the
content-addressed construction recipe rather than distributed corpus
bytes.

## B.3.7 Embedding Models by Profile

The speed profile uses all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384-dim); all other
profiles use BAAI/bge-m3 (1024-dim). Because the embedding dimensions
differ, the speed profile requires a separate vector collection and
cannot share ingested data with the other three profiles. Each speed
run uses a fresh database with its own 384-dim collection.

Figure 6: E2E scaling curves. (a) Retrieval quality
(NDCG@10) within the generation pipeline. (b) Generation
faithfulness (RAGAS). balanced overtakes standard
on both axes at 50K. Dashed annotations show total $\Delta$ from

Alternative mechanisms for the NDCG–faithfulness
divergence.

Two mechanisms besides information coverage may contribute to the
observed faithfulness improvements at scale. First, the generator’s
instruction-following behavior may cause it to produce safer, less
specific answers when retrieved context is noisier, trading answer
precision for faithfulness. Second, RAGAS faithfulness measures
answer–context consistency, not answer–truth consistency: if both
answer and context shift toward vaguer content, faithfulness can
increase trivially. Distinguishing these from the information-coverage
explanation requires human evaluation of answer specificity at each
corpus size, which we leave to future work.

## B.3.9 Scaling Law Fits

We fit log-linear models $\text{NDCG@10}=a\cdot\log_{10}(N)+b$ 
to the six-point scaling data; Table 23 reports
goodness-of-fit and 95% prediction intervals for extrapolations to
500K–1M.

Table 23: Log-linear degradation fits (6 data points, 5K–200K).
 $R^{2}$ measures goodness of fit; $p$ tests whether the slope
differs from zero. Projections use 95% prediction intervals.

Profile
Slope/dec
 $R^{2}$ 
 $p$ 
95% CI (slope)
Proj. 500K
Proj. 1M

 $[-0.168,-0.062]$

0.004
 $[-0.082,-0.029]$

0.296
0.265
 $[-0.061,+0.022]$ 
0.431 $\pm$ 0.075
0.425 $\pm$ 0.084

speed and standard show strong log-linear fits
( $R^{2}\geq 0.89$ ) with slopes significantly different from zero
( $p<0.01$ ), supporting the classic power-law scaling picture.

Deviation from the pre-registered fit form.

The pre-registration specified a power-law fit,
 $\text{NDCG@10}(N)=a\cdot N^{b}$ ; the fit we executed is
log-linear in $\log_{10}(N)$ ,
 $\text{NDCG@10}(N)=a\cdot\log_{10}(N)+b$ . These are not the
same functional form (the pre-registered power law is linear in
 $\log\text{NDCG}$ vs $\log N$ ; the executed fit is linear in NDCG vs
 $\log N$ ). We report the log-linear fit because NDCG@10 is bounded
in $[0,1]$ and the observed decay is close-to-linear on that scale
across our measured range, whereas a power law is more appropriate
when the dependent variable spans multiple orders of magnitude.
A side-by-side power-law vs. log-linear comparison on the same
scaling data is reported in Section D.4; both fits
agree on the sign of the rank ordering and on the direction of the
slope difference between profiles, so the qualitative conclusion is
robust to the choice of parameterization. The qualitative headline conclusion
(balanced overtakes standard between 15K and 25K
and the gap firms at 200K) is a rank claim on measured cells and
does not depend on which fit is used; the numerical slope estimates
(Table 23) and the 500K/1M projections
(Table 49) do, and should be re-fit under a
power-law parameterization before being used quantitatively.
balanced and max-reliable show weaker fits
( $R^{2}=0.40,0.30$ ) with slopes not statistically
distinguishable from zero ( $p=0.175,0.265$ ); these profiles
are so scale-robust that their variation is dominated by seed noise
rather than corpus-size degradation, itself a positive finding for
scale robustness, though it also means the projected 1M intervals
for those two profiles are necessarily wide. Latency scaling appears in Section E.2;
extrapolation to 1M with full caveats in
Section E.3.

lookups (NFCorpus), opinion-seeking financial questions (FiQA),
scientific claim verification (SciFact), counterargument retrieval
(ArguAna), and multi-hop reasoning chains (HotpotQA). The generation
queries require compositional reasoning (MuSiQue, HotpotQA) or reading
comprehension with unanswerable question detection (SQuAD v2).

## Appendix C Metrics and Judge Validation

## C.1 Evaluation Metrics

This appendix provides formal definitions for every evaluation metric
exposed by lasagna’s evaluation SDK, grouped into retrieval
metrics (computed before generation), end-to-end answer metrics, and
LLM-judged metrics. The main paper reports NDCG@10, MRR, Recall@100,
EM, Token F1, and RAGAS faithfulness; the remaining metrics defined
below (answer relevance, pairwise LLM-as-judge preference, MAP, and
RAGAS context recall) are supported by the SDK but not reported
in this study, answer relevance was redundant with faithfulness
under our concise-answer prompting, pairwise preference was
superseded by the factorial metrics, MAP tracked NDCG@10 within noise,
and RAGAS context recall was systematically unreliable with the
GPT-5.1 judge and is excluded from all analyses.
Cross-judge agreement with Claude Sonnet 4.5 is documented under
Section C.1.4.

## C.1.1 Retrieval Metrics

NDCG@ $k$ (Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain).

For a query $q$ with relevance labels $\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}\}$ over the
top- $k$ retrieved passages, the discounted cumulative gain is

 $\mathrm{DCG}@k=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{2^{r_{i}}-1}{\log_{2}(i+1)}\,,$ 

(2)

and NDCG@ $k$ normalizes by the ideal ordering:

 $\mathrm{NDCG}@k=\frac{\mathrm{DCG}@k}{\mathrm{IDCG}@k}\,,$ 

(3)

where $\mathrm{IDCG}@k$ is Eq. 2 computed over the
relevance-sorted ranking. We use $k{=}10$ for all retrieval
experiments. When multiple chunks originate from the same parent
document, we de-duplicate by document ID before computing DCG to
avoid inflating the score (see the NDCG $>1.0$ correction described
in Section 5).

MRR (Mean Reciprocal Rank).

For a set of $Q$ queries, each with rank $r_{q}$ of the first relevant
passage:

 $\mathrm{MRR}=\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\frac{1}{r_{q}}\,.$ 

(4)

If no relevant passage appears in the top- $k$ results, $1/r_{q}=0$ 
for that query.

Recall@ $k$ .

The fraction of all relevant passages for query $q$ that appear in the
top- $k$ retrieved set:

 $\mathrm{Recall}@k=\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{q}\cap\mathcal{T}_{q}^{k}|}{|\mathcal{R}_{q}|}\,,$ 

(5)

where $\mathcal{R}_{q}$ is the set of relevant passages and
 $\mathcal{T}_{q}^{k}$ is the top- $k$ retrieved set. We report
Recall@100, which measures the retriever’s ability to surface relevant
material before reranking.

Precision@ $k$ (P@ $k$ ).

The fraction of the top- $k$ retrieved passages that are relevant:

 $\mathrm{P}@k=\frac{|\mathcal{R}_{q}\cap\mathcal{T}_{q}^{k}|}{k}\,.$ 

(6)

Complementary to Recall@ $k$ and reported in the evaluation SDK but
not in the main paper (tracks NDCG@10 within noise at the $k{=}10$ 
cutoff we use elsewhere).

MAP (Mean Average Precision).

The mean over queries of average precision:

 $\mathrm{MAP}=\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}_{q}|}\sum_{k=1}^{|\mathcal{T}_{q}|}\mathrm{P}@k(q)\cdot\mathbf{1}[\text{doc at rank }k\text{ is relevant}]\,.$ 

(7)

Supported by lasagna’s evaluation SDK and reported during
development but not in the main paper; in our runs it tracked
NDCG@10 within noise across all 167 configurations.

## C.1.2 End-to-End Answer Metrics

Exact Match (EM).

A binary indicator that equals 1 if the predicted answer string,
after lowercasing and stripping articles/punctuation, matches any
acceptable gold answer string:

 $\mathrm{EM}(a,\hat{a})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathrm{normalize}(\hat{a})\in\{\mathrm{normalize}(a_{j})\}_{j=1}^{J}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.$ 

(8)

Token F1.

The harmonic mean of token-level precision and recall between the
predicted and gold answer, computed over whitespace-tokenized,
lowercased strings:

 $F_{1}=\frac{2\cdot P\cdot R}{P+R}\,,\qquad P=\frac{|\hat{T}\cap T^{*}|}{|\hat{T}|}\,,\qquad R=\frac{|\hat{T}\cap T^{*}|}{|T^{*}|}\,,$ 

(9)

where $\hat{T}$ and $T^{*}$ are the token multisets of the predicted and
gold answers, respectively. When multiple gold answers exist, we take
the maximum $F_{1}$ over all references.

## C.1.3 LLM-Judged Metrics

All LLM-judged metrics use RAGAS v0.4 (Es et al., 2024) with
GPT-5.1 as the judge model at temperature $T{=}0$ , greedy
decoding, and deterministic seed. This applies uniformly to all
experiments: profile comparisons, scaling experiments, and the
122-configuration faithfulness factorial. lasagna wraps
the RAGAS framework so the same judge configuration is reused
across faithfulness, answer relevance, and context recall, and so
every LLM call is deterministic under the fixed seed.

Faithfulness.

Faithfulness measures the fraction of claims in the generated answer
that are supported by the retrieved context. RAGAS decomposes the
answer into atomic claims $\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{M}\}$ using the judge LLM,
then classifies each claim as supported or
not supported given the context:

 $\displaystyle\mathrm{Faithfulness}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{1}\bigl[$ 
 $\displaystyle\text{claim }c_{m}$ 

(10)

 $\displaystyle\text{ is supported by context}\bigr].$ 

A faithfulness score of 1.0 indicates that every atomic claim in the
answer is grounded in the retrieved passages. We use concise answer
prompts (one sentence) to reduce the number of atomic claims per
sample, which both accelerates evaluation and reduces variance from
claim decomposition.

Answer Relevance.

Answer relevance estimates how well the generated answer addresses the
original question. RAGAS generates $N{=}3$ synthetic questions from
the answer, computes the cosine similarity between each synthetic
question embedding and the original question embedding, and averages:

 $\mathrm{Relevance}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\cos\bigl(\mathbf{e}(q),\;\mathbf{e}(\tilde{q}_{n})\bigr)\,,$ 

(11)

where $\mathbf{e}(\cdot)$ is the embedding function and
 $\tilde{q}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{q}_{N}$ are the synthetic questions.

LLM-as-Judge (Pairwise Preference).

For Pareto comparisons (Section 5), we additionally use a
pairwise LLM judge that receives the question, gold answer, and two
candidate answers (A and B), then outputs a preference
 $\in\{\text{A},\text{B},\text{Tie}\}$ . The judge prompt asks for a
brief rationale before the verdict. We report win rate as the
fraction of queries where a configuration is preferred over the
baseline.

## C.1.4 Judge Bias and Mitigations

LLM judges exhibit well-documented biases that can distort
evaluation (Zheng et al., 2023). Our protocol mitigates three
of these biases, described below.

Position bias.

LLM judges tend to prefer the answer presented first in the
prompt (Zheng et al., 2023). For all pairwise comparisons, we run
each pair twice with swapped presentation order (A-B and B-A) and
record a win only when the preference is consistent across both
orderings. Inconsistent pairs are counted as ties.

Self-preference bias.

Models may prefer outputs from the same model family. Because both
answer generation (GPT-5.1) and judging (GPT-5.1) use the same
model, self-preference bias is a concern. We mitigate this in two
ways: (i) the judge evaluates answer pairs from different
pipeline configurations, not from different base models, so
both candidates are GPT-5.1 outputs and self-preference does not
favor either candidate; (ii) we validate a 50-query subsample using
Claude Sonnet 4.5 as an alternative judge to measure inter-judge agreement.
We ran 80 matched samples (40 per configuration) through both judges.
Kendall’s $\tau_{b}=0.38$ ( $p<0.001$ ), Spearman $\rho=0.42$ 
( $p<0.001$ ), and 66% of per-sample faithfulness scores agree within
0.1 absolute points. GPT-5.1 assigns slightly higher mean faithfulness
(0.879 vs. 0.860 for Claude Sonnet 4.5). This moderate, statistically
significant agreement indicates that per-sample faithfulness scores
are largely consistent across judge models on the two configurations
sampled, though absolute scores show provider-specific calibration
differences; see Section C.5 for a separate pairwise-BTL
protocol study showing that fine-grained configuration-level rankings
are protocol-dependent while profile-tier separations are preserved.

Determinism.

We fix the judge model to GPT-5.1 with temperature $T{=}0$ and
a fixed random seed across all experiments. All RAGAS evaluations use
batch_size=4 to avoid rate-limit-induced timeouts, and we use
paired statistical tests (paired
bootstrap or Wilcoxon signed-rank, paired by query) so that any
residual judge noise cancels across configurations being compared.

## C.2 Generation Prompts and Faithfulness Regimes

Faithfulness scores in this paper come from two distinct generation
regimes that use different prompt templates. Both are scored by the
same RAGAS v0.4 / GPT-5.1 judge with identical decoding
(Section C.1.4); the numerical gap between them is a
prompt effect, not a scoring inconsistency. We disclose both
templates here so that faithfulness comparisons within a regime
(pre-registered) are separable from cross-regime comparisons
(descriptive only).

Concise-regen prompt (scaling and factorial tracks).

Used by the 122-configuration faithfulness ANOVA (Fig. 3)
and the H2 cross-corpus corroboration. Prioritizes answer
atomicity to reduce RAGAS claim-decomposition variance:

Answer in ONE concise sentence based only on the context.

Do not explain or elaborate.

Question: {question}

Context: {contexts}

Answer:

Verbose-agentic prompt (deployment logs, agentic pipeline).

Used in Tables 65 and 46 and by the agentic
routing tier when it returns its own composed answer. Encourages the
model to cite retrieved evidence inline:

You are a helpful assistant. Using the retrieved context below,

answer the question. Cite the supporting passage for each claim.

If the context is insufficient, say so explicitly.

Question: {question}

Context: {contexts}

Answer:

Regime–metric mapping.

Table 26 maps the headline cross-regime faithfulness
numbers to the regime that produced them; appendix tables state their
regime inline in the surrounding prose.

The regen and agentic columns are not directly comparable:
verbose answers produce more atomic claims per unit of retrieved
evidence, which systematically inflates RAGAS faithfulness by
 $+0.15$ to $+0.18$ (Table 27).

Implication for cross-table reading.

Different tables report faithfulness under different answer-generation
prompts (Table 26); values are not directly

computed within a single regime (pre-registered $\eta^{2}$ , scaling
slopes) are unaffected.

Prompt-regime sensitivity of the joint-HV winner (Table 2).

As a sensitivity check on the joint hypervolume comparison in
Table 2, we recompute HV under two counterfactual regime
alignments using the paired-sample gap from Table 27.
Aligning all four profiles to the concise-regen prompt (subtracting
each agentic profile’s paired regime gap) drops balanced’s
HV to $0.37$ and max-reliable’s to $0.32$ , versus
standard’s unchanged $0.52$ ; aligning all four to the
verbose-agentic prompt raises standard’s HV to $0.64$ 
(both at $L_{\mathrm{ref}}{=}30$  s). standard remains the
joint-HV leader under either alignment, and the within-agentic-class
comparison (balanced vs max-reliable) uses a
common prompt regime by construction and is unaffected.

Agentic-prompt scope.

The CRAG grader prompt, Self-RAG reflection prompts, and
Speculative-RAG draft-and-verify prompts are held fixed at the
versions frozen at design time (recorded alongside the manifest at the paths referenced in Table 58 and available from the authors on request). Observed
agentic-vs-standard deltas throughout this paper are for these
specific prompt configurations and cost model; alternative prompt
engineering choices (e.g., few-shot exemplars in the grader
prompt, different Self-RAG reflection-token sets, or different
Speculative-RAG draft budgets) may shift the ordering of agentic
gates relative to no-gate. Section I.2 documents the
practical decision rule that de-couples the choice of agentic
gate from the choice of agentic prompt, so the
profile recommendations survive prompt-configuration changes even
when specific per-gate deltas do not.

## C.3 Length-Controlled Faithfulness Rerun

A natural concern is that faithfulness gains under our
concise-answer prompting may partly reflect brevity, shorter answers
make fewer atomic claims and thus fewer opportunities for the RAGAS
judge to flag a claim as unsupported, rather than genuinely superior
grounding. We ran a targeted length-controlled experiment to
quantify this effect.

Design.

We drew a stratified sample of 50 questions across three benchmarks
(SQuAD v2: 22, MuSiQue: 18, HotpotQA: 10) and re-generated answers
under three prompt regimes derived from a common instruction skeleton
that varies only length guidance: (A) concise (“answer in one
short sentence”), (B) neutral (“answer based on the
context”), and (C) verbose (“answer in detail with supporting
facts”). Retrieved contexts, decoding parameters, and the generator
LLM are held constant. All $50\times 3=150$ answer–context pairs
are re-scored under the canonical GPT-5.1 faithfulness judge. Raw data (paper/data/length_controlled_faith.jsonl) and the analysis script (paper/scripts/m6_length_analysis.py) are available from the authors on request.

Table 28: Length-controlled faithfulness ( $n{=}50$ per regime,
GPT-5.1 judge). Within-regime Pearson correlation is between answer
length and the sample’s faithfulness score. Residualized means are
computed after regressing faithfulness on log answer-token count and
adding back the grand mean.

Regime
Mean length (tok)
Raw faithfulness
Within-regime $r$ 
Residualized faithfulness

Concise (A)

0.714

Neutral (B)

0.862
 $-0.064$

0.644
 $-0.459$ 
0.824

Length is a large, significant driver.

Pooling all 150 judgements, Pearson $r(\text{tokens},\text{faith})=-0.398$ ( $p=4.6\times 10^{-7}$ ). A fixed-effects OLS
 $\text{faith}\sim\text{tokens}+\text{variant}+\text{benchmark}$ 
gives $\beta_{\text{len}}=-1.26\times 10^{-3}$ per token
(SE $3.28\times 10^{-4}$ , $p=1.9\times 10^{-4}$ , $R^{2}=0.181$ ):
every additional 100 answer tokens costs $0.126$ 
faithfulness points on average. A Friedman test across the three
regimes on the paired 50-sample basis rejects equality of variant
distributions ( $\chi^{2}=25.2$ , $p=3.3\times 10^{-6}$ ).

The concise-over-verbose gap reverses under length control.

Raw means order as neutral $>$ concise $>$ verbose (a
concise ${-}$ verbose gap of $+0.179$ ). After residualizing on log
answer length the ordering flips: verbose (0.824) $>$ neutral
(0.791) $>$ concise (0.714), a residualized gap of $-0.110$ . In other
words, verbose answers make more grounded claims per unit of
attempted content; the apparent concise advantage on raw
faithfulness is fully accounted for by the mechanical fact that
shorter answers contain fewer claims to verify.

Per-benchmark decomposition.

The length effect is strongest on SQuAD v2 ( $r{=}-0.56$ ,
 $p{<}10^{-6}$ ; verbose mean drops to 0.539), moderate on MuSiQue
( $r{=}-0.26$ , $p{=}0.057$ ), and weak on HotpotQA ( $r{=}-0.25$ ,
 $p{=}0.17$ , $n{=}30$ ). This is consistent with SQuAD’s short
span-extraction reference style: verbose answers deviate most from
the corpus-anchored evidence surface and pick up more unsupported
elaboration.

What this changes and what it does not.

A portion of the raw faithfulness advantage
reported for concise-regen configurations reflects brevity rather
than superior grounding, so we report a length-residualized
column alongside raw faithfulness for any cross-regime comparison
(see the length-controlled entry in Table 58). Within-regime
comparisons (the 122-configuration ANOVA and the scaling slopes)
hold prompt length constant by construction and are therefore
unaffected: the
regen-vs-agentic gap in Table 27 is now
best read as a joint prompt-and-length effect, not a pure prompt
effect, but its statistical significance and direction survive
length control on the paired 100K sample ( $\Delta=+0.109$ 
residualized vs. $+0.161$ raw).

## C.4 Human Evaluation of Faithfulness

The paper validates the RAGAS/GPT-5.1 faithfulness metric with
three independent judge-validation studies that should not be
conflated:

1. 

Human dyad study (this appendix, $n=50$ ): two human
annotators vs. RAGAS on 50 QA triples, yielding Pearson $r=0.83$ 
against RAGAS and $r=0.96$ between annotators; Cohen’s $\kappa$ 
at the 0.5 midpoint $=0.92$ .

2. 

Cross-LLM study (Section C.1.4, $n=80$ ): 40
matched configuration answers per profile scored by GPT-5.1
and Claude Sonnet 4.5, yielding Kendall’s $\tau_{b}=0.38$ 
with 66% pairwise agreement within $\pm 0.10$ .

3. 

Pairwise Bradley–Terry study
(Section C.5, $n{=}123$ pairs, $246$ judgments):
two independent LLM annotators fit a BTL posterior over 12
D5 $\times$ D8 configurations, yielding Cohen’s $\kappa=0.79$ 
(substantial inter-annotator agreement) and a Spearman
 $\rho=-0.36$ between BTL and RAGAS configuration ranks; the
fine-grained configuration ordering is protocol-dependent, but
profile-tier separation is preserved.

These three studies use disjoint samples and answer different
questions (per-answer human calibration, cross-model robustness, and
pairwise configuration-ranking) and are therefore not pooled. Raw
per-sample scores for all three studies are available from the authors on request:
paper/data/human_eval_annotated.json (human dyad),
paper/data/judge_cross_validation_80.json (cross-LLM), and
paper/data/human_eval_btl_posteriors.json (with per-item pairwise annotations in
paper/data/human_eval_pairwise_btl_items_A1.csv and
paper/data/human_eval_pairwise_btl_items_A2.csv).

Scope of the human validation.

The human validation ( $n{=}50$ examples across SQuAD v2, MuSiQue and
HotpotQA) establishes judge-level Pearson $r{=}0.83$ against RAGAS.
This sample is UNDER-POWERED to validate configuration-ranking-level
correlation across the 122 pipeline configurations in the faithfulness
factorial: at $n{=}50$ the standard error on a Pearson correlation is
 $\approx 0.14$ and the sample provides on average
 $50/122\approx 0.4$ observations per configuration, insufficient to
certify a Kendall’s- $\tau_{b}$ between per-configuration human means and
per-configuration RAGAS means. We therefore use this study only
to establish judge validity at the per-answer level, and do not treat
it as certifying ranking-level accuracy across configurations.
The two cross-LLM sensitivity studies described elsewhere
(Section C.1.4 $n{=}80$ ; Section F.8
 $n{=}220$ ) supplement this human study, but do not substitute for it;
they measure robustness across judge models rather than validity
against human judgement, and remain LLM-scored throughout.
The study is also long-form-QA-only (three benchmarks; no retrieval
metrics, no scaling cells, no cross-profile coverage), so
extrapolation to retrieval-track claims (NDCG@10) or unseen profiles
is not licensed by this evidence.

To validate the LLM-judged faithfulness metric, two domain experts
with RAG evaluation experience independently annotated 50
question–answer–context triples sampled from the generation
evaluation set (17 SQuAD v2, 18 MuSiQue, 15 HotpotQA). Each
annotator scored faithfulness on a continuous 0–1 scale using the
same rubric as the RAGAS judge: 1.0 if every claim is supported by
the context, 0.0 if no claims are supported. Annotators worked
independently without seeing each other’s scores or the RAGAS output.

Annotation instructions.

The following instructions were provided verbatim to both annotators:

Annotation instructions. You will receive a question, a generated
answer, and the retrieved context used to generate that answer. Score
the answer’s faithfulness on a continuous scale from 0 to 1. A score
of 1 means that every factual claim in the answer is supported by the
context. A score of 0 means that none of the factual claims are
supported. Evaluate support from the provided context only; do not
use outside knowledge. Work independently and do not consult the
other annotator or the automated RAGAS score.

Table 29 reports inter-rater reliability across
all three raters (two human annotators and the GPT-5.1 RAGAS judge).

Table 29: Inter-rater reliability: two human annotators and GPT-5.1
RAGAS judge on 50 faithfulness evaluations.

Average pairwise Pearson $r$ : 0.853

Human inter-rater reliability is high: Pearson $r=0.96$ ,
Cohen’s $\kappa=0.93$ (binarized), and 96% of scores agree within
0.2 absolute points. Both human annotators agree strongly with
the RAGAS judge (Pearson $r=0.83$ and $0.77$ ), with the average
pairwise Pearson across all three raters reaching $r=0.85$ .
The RAGAS judge shows a slight positive bias ( $+0.019$ over Human 1,
 $+0.063$ over Human 2), consistent with known LLM self-preference
effects (Zheng et al., 2023).

The high human–human agreement ( $r=0.96$ ) establishes that
per-answer faithfulness scoring is reproducible across annotators,
while the strong human–RAGAS agreement ( $r>0.77$ ) confirms that
judge substitution preserves per-answer scores and the effect-size /
profile-tier structure that H1–H3 rest on. Fine-grained
configuration-to-configuration rankings within a profile tier are
protocol-dependent (Spearman $\rho=-0.36$ between BTL and RAGAS on
the 12 D5 $\times$ D8 configurations covered by the BTL sample; see Section C.5);
accordingly the paper’s inferential claims are scoped to the tier
level, and within-tier configuration comparisons are reported as
descriptive.

## C.5 Pairwise Faithfulness Validation via Bradley–Terry

The $n{=}50$ human study of Section C.4 establishes judge
validity at the per-answer level but is under-powered to certify
configuration-ranking accuracy across the D5 $\times$ D8 factorial. To
probe whether the point-wise RAGAS faithfulness judge preserves
ordering at fine-grained configuration granularity, we ran the third
validation study of Section C.4 using a pairwise
Bradley–Terry (BTL) LLM annotation protocol on 123 blinded
configuration-pairs drawn from the
D5 $\times$ D8 grid. Pairs were biased toward small RAGAS deltas
(top- $K$ nearest per shared sample) to concentrate the study on the
sub-noise regime the point-wise judge is least confident about. The
RAGAS anchor scores against which the BTL posteriors are correlated
in Table 30 (Spearman $\rho=-0.364$ ; Pearson
 $r=-0.421$ ) are computed under the concise-regen prompt
regime (Sections C.2 and 26), matching the
122-configuration faithfulness ANOVA (Sections D.2 and 3)
from which the D5 $\times$ D8 slice is drawn. Two
independent LLM annotators produced 246 pairwise judgments; the full annotation brief (paper/data/human_eval_annotator_instructions.md) and raw per-item annotations (paper/data/human_eval_pairwise_btl_items_A1.csv and paper/data/human_eval_pairwise_btl_items_A2.csv) are available from the authors on request.

Table 30 reports coverage and inter-protocol
reliability; Table 31 reports the BTL posteriors
over the 12 configurations covered by the sample.

 $[-0.45,\ +0.00]$

 $-0.574$

 $-0.744$ 
 $[-1.09,\ -0.21]$ 

Bottom line.

Three findings emerge, each with distinct
implications for the paper’s headline claims (protocol reliability is
separately established at the end of this subsection: Cohen’s $\kappa=0.79$ between annotators, $92.7\%$ raw agreement across $246$ 
judgments, so the $79\%$ tie rate reflects true configuration
indistinguishability rather than annotator noise).

(i) Fine-grain configuration deltas on the small-delta subsample
are largely below LLM discriminability. With $79\%$ of pairwise
judgments returned as ties and only $1$ of $12$ covered configurations
achieving a bootstrap CI fully separated from zero, the pairwise
protocol establishes the LLM-judge noise floor on the hardest-to-
distinguish pairs it was designed to probe (pairs deliberately biased
toward small RAGAS deltas per the sample construction above). This
does not license the broader claim that all within-tier
configuration differences on faithfulness are sub-noise: the
D5 $\times$ D8 cell-departure signal of $3$ – $5$ points reported in
Table 42 is above the noise floor characterized
here for a majority of cells whose pair-CIs were not included in this
subsample. Within-tier configuration ranks at the small-delta end of
the design are not robustly ordered by either judge protocol; larger-
cell-delta and tier-level claims are outside the subsample and
unaffected.

(ii) The D8 agentic-gate choice is the one substantive within-tier
signal. Speculative-RAG-gated configurations occupy $3$ of the top- $5$ 
BTL positions and CRAG-gated configurations occupy the $3$ bottom
positions; this is the only cross-cell pattern in the posterior that
survives the tie-heavy noise floor and is consistent with the
within-agentic D8 ordering (Speculative-RAG $>$ Self-RAG $>$ 
CRAG) reported for the generation track in Section D.2
(Speculative-RAG $0.811$ vs. CRAG $0.692$ ). The BTL sample does not
include D8 $=$ none configurations, so the H1 verdict’s
no-gate-vs-agentic marginal claim is not directly testable by this
posterior.

(iii) Fine-grained configuration ranks are protocol-dependent,
but tier-level separations are not. The Spearman $\rho=-0.36$ 
between BTL and RAGAS ranks on the same $12$ configurations indicates
that judge-protocol choice matters at fine-grained configuration
granularity. H2 and H3 are stated at profile-tier granularity
(speed / balanced / max-reliable), where both protocols preserve the
same three-tier separation, so those verdicts remain protocol-robust.
H1 is stated at the interaction/cell level and at the D8 marginal;
the present BTL sample corroborates the within-agentic D8 ordering
(Speculative-RAG $>$ CRAG, item (ii) above) but cannot independently
probe H1’s no-gate-vs-agentic marginal because all $12$ sampled cells
have D8 $\in$ {crag, self_rag_quality_gates, speculative_rag}.
This is a scope gap of the BTL validation for H1, not evidence for or
against H1. The protocol dependence is confined to
within-tier configuration comparisons and is documented alongside the
existing judge-noise footnote of Table 29.

The high inter-annotator agreement ( $\kappa=0.79$ ) further establishes
that the pairwise-BTL protocol is itself internally reliable, so the
tier-level protocol agreement is not an artifact of intra-protocol
noise. Full posteriors, per-annotator distributions, and bootstrap draws (paper/data/human_eval_btl_posteriors.json) are available from the authors on request.

## Appendix D Detailed Statistical Analyses

## D.1 Full Ablation Results

Table 32 reports the marginal effect of each feature on
NDCG@10, measured by toggling one feature at a time against the standard
baseline (dense retrieval, hybrid $\alpha{=}0.5$ , MMR enabled, no
reranking, no query enhancement). All deltas are computed on the held-out
test split averaged across five BEIR domains. Results are exploratory
because these single-feature ablations do not account for interaction
effects (Section 5).

Key observations.

Three features provide robust improvements with modest latency cost:
cross-encoder reranking ( $+0.084$ ), disabling MMR ( $+0.046$ ), and
document awareness with 100-token prefix ( $+0.062$ ). Query enhancement
methods (expansion, fusion, decomposition) add 2.4–2.6 seconds of latency
for marginal or negative retrieval gains, suggesting that LLM-based query
rewriting is not cost-effective on domain-focused corpora at this scale.
Agentic RAG is the only feature that improves faithfulness substantially
( $+0.05$ ) while simultaneously reducing NDCG@10 ( $-0.015$ ), illustrating
the retrieval–generation tradeoff discussed in Section 5.

## D.2 Faithfulness Factorial ANOVA

This appendix reports the complete results of the 122-configuration
faithfulness ANOVA. Each configuration was evaluated on 56 end-to-end
generation queries (SQuAD v2, MuSiQue, HotpotQA) with RAGAS
faithfulness scoring via GPT-5.1 judge, totalling
6,832 individual query evaluations. All faithfulness values in this
subsection use a common answer prompt (Section C.2) and are
not directly comparable to faithfulness reported in
Tables 65 and 46, which use a different
answer prompt (Table 26).

Main effects.

Three dimensions explain significant faithfulness variance.
Query augmentation (D5) and agentic gating (D8) each explain
 ${\sim}$ 22% of variance ( $p<0.001$ ), while compression (D7)
explains 11%. Embedding model, chunking strategy, enrichment,
and retrieval mode each contribute $\eta^{2}<0.04$ and are not
significant ( $p>0.18$ ). This stands in sharp contrast with
the retrieval ANOVA (Section 5), where embedding
and enrichment are the dominant factors, confirming that retrieval
and generation quality are governed by different pipeline stages.

Key faithfulness main-effect findings.

• 

Decomposition-based query augmentation reduces faithfulness
(mean $=0.645$ ) relative to no augmentation ( $0.778$ ) and
step-back prompting ( $0.781$ ), likely because multi-part queries
dilute the retrieval focus, yielding less relevant contexts.

• 

Speculative-RAG gating achieves the highest faithfulness
( $0.811$ ), while CRAG ( $0.692$ ) and no gating ( $0.691$ ) perform
worst. Self-RAG ( $0.760$ ) falls between.

• 

LLMLingua compression hurts faithfulness ( $0.702$ vs. $0.775$ without), consistent with information loss during
context compression reducing answer grounding.

Two-way interactions.

Table 33 reports all 28 two-way interactions
sorted by partial $\eta^{2}$ . Only
Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate is highly significant
( $\eta^{2}=0.191$ , Type III SS, $F=5.47$ , $p<0.001$ ).
The second-strongest faithfulness interaction is Enrichment
 $\times$ Reranking ( $\eta^{2}=0.105$ , raw $p=0.044$ ), which reaches
raw significance but not the Holm-corrected family-wise threshold.
We attribute the interaction to a coupling between the two stages:
enrichment (contextual prefixes, RAPTOR summaries) changes the
surface form of each chunk so that the reranker scores a candidate
whose distribution differs from its untyped training distribution,
while the reranker simultaneously reorders chunks in a way that can
disturb the document-level coherence enrichment was meant to
preserve for the answer LLM; whether the joint effect helps or
hurts faithfulness depends on which of these two channels dominates
for a given configuration.

Table 33: Faithfulness interaction table (122 configs, 56 queries each).
Type I SS shown for the full 28-pair scan; the key interaction
(QueryAug $\times$ AgenticGate) was verified with Type III SS
( $\eta^{2}=0.191$ , $F=5.47$ , $p<0.001$ ).
∗ Holm $p<0.05$ ; † raw $p<0.05$ ;
‡ Type I value; Type III gives $\eta^{2}=0.191$ , $p<0.001$ .
Regime: concise-regen (Section C.2).

Design limitations.

The 122-configuration faithfulness ANOVA covers 3 of 4 embedding
models (MiniLM-L6 excluded due to API timeouts during RAPTOR
ingestion) and 3 of 4 enrichment levels (GraphRAG excluded).
All 8 dimensions retain at least 22 observations per factor level,
and all main effects and two-way interactions are estimable.
The unbalanced design requires caution in interpreting aggregate
 $\eta^{2}$ values; individual interaction $p$ -values are more
reliable indicators.

Per-configuration metric decoupling.

Figure 7 plots retrieval-track NDCG@10 against
e2e RAGAS faithfulness for the $113$ configurations evaluated on
both factorial tracks (joined by config_id). The two
metrics are statistically uncorrelated (Pearson $r=-0.01$ ): $5\%$ 
of configurations achieve faithfulness $>0.80$ at NDCG@10 $<0.45$ ,
and $6\%$ show the inverse pattern (NDCG@10 $>0.49$ at faithfulness
 $<0.65$ ). A configuration optimized on either metric alone offers
no guarantee on the other, motivating the dual-axis evaluation used
throughout this paper.

Simpson-style per-benchmark stratification.

The pooled retrieval-faithfulness $r=-0.01$ could hide within-family
patterns (Simpson-style aggregation), so we stratify by the
faithfulness benchmark and pair each configuration’s overall retrieval
NDCG@10 with its per-benchmark faithfulness ( $N{=}113$ configurations
per stratum): HotpotQA $r=-0.03$ ( $p=0.72$ ), MuSiQue $r=-0.05$ 
( $p=0.63$ ), SQuAD v2 $r=+0.10$ ( $p=0.30$ ). No stratum reaches
 $|r|>0.10$ or $p<0.30$ , and the pooled coefficient lies inside
the within-family range with no sign reversal, so the pooled statistic
attenuates rather than misleads: retrieval quality and faithfulness
are decoupled within each task family, not merely across them.

Figure 7: Retrieval-track NDCG@10 (93 BEIR queries) vs e2e RAGAS
faithfulness (56 QA queries) for the $113$ factorial configurations
evaluated on both tracks. Pearson $r=-0.01$ across the joined
set. Shaded regions mark off-diagonal quadrants: red holds $7/113$ 
configurations with strong retrieval but weak faithfulness, yellow
holds $6/113$ with the inverse pattern, and green holds $17/113$ 
that clear both thresholds simultaneously. The near-zero
correlation indicates that retrieval quality and faithfulness are
governed by different pipeline stages, consistent with the disjoint
factor-pair dominance reported in Fig. 3.

LASSO robustness check.

To verify that the sequential one-at-a-time ANOVA does not produce
spurious interactions, we fit a joint LASSO-penalized regression
with all 192 main-effect and interaction features simultaneously
(5-fold CV repeated 3 $\times$ , $\alpha=0.006$ ). The LASSO
achieves $R^{2}=0.77$ and selects 34 non-zero features, of which
31 are interaction terms. Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate
is the strongest surviving interaction pair (4 terms selected,
total $|\beta|=0.074$ ), confirming the sequential ANOVA finding.
The specific decomposition $\times$ Self-RAG interaction ( $\beta=-0.034$ )
is the single largest coefficient, consistent with the mechanistic
explanation that multi-part queries overwhelm CRAG/Self-RAG gating.
For retrieval, LASSO ( $R^{2}=0.84$ , 81 features selected) confirms
Embedding $\times$ Enrichment as the strongest interaction (total
 $|\beta|=0.016$ ) and Embedding $\times$ Reranking as the third
strongest ( $|\beta|=0.012$ ), matching the ANOVA results.

Judge consistency.

All faithfulness evaluations (both the factorial ANOVA and the
profile comparisons) use the same GPT-5.1 judge model with
identical RAGAS v0.4 configuration ( $T{=}0$ , deterministic seed,
batch_size ${}=4$ ). We additionally validated inter-judge agreement
by running 80 matched evaluations with Claude Sonnet 4.5 as an
alternative judge (Kendall $\tau_{b}=0.38$ , $p<0.001$ ; 66% of
scores agree within 0.1; details in Section C.1).

## D.3 H1 Reproducibility and Joint-Interaction Correction

The initial H1 analysis reported a proxy for the pre-registered H1
statistic. The preregistration
(paper/preregistration/preregistration_body.tex L357–358;
OSF §189) specifies partial $\eta^{2}$ for the joint set of all
28 two-way interactions in a mains-plus-28-interactions model, tested
against $F(209,\cdot)$ and thresholded at
 $\eta^{2}=\text{SS}_{\text{int}}/(\text{SS}_{\text{int}}+\text{SS}_{\text{resid}})\geq 0.20$ .
That analysis fit a reduced model containing mains plus
only one interaction (Query Aug $\times$ Agentic Gate) and
reported the resulting $\eta^{2}=0.191$ , $F(12,\,279)=5.47$ as the H1
decision statistic. The per-sample RAGAS faithfulness table used to
produce that fit ( $n\approx 292$ observations at df ${}_{\text{den}}=279$ )
is unavailable, motivating the reconstruction below.

Reconstruction.

We reconstructed the per-sample table
locally from 117 .checkpoints/*.json experiment artifacts
under the research/results/factorial_experiments/ artifact tree,
extracting 4,948 per-sample rows (4,820 after deduplication on
(config_id, sample_id)). This recovers 113 of the 122 recorded configurations $\times$ 3 QA benchmarks (hotpotqa,
musique, squad_v2) $=339$ long-form
(config $\times$ benchmark) cells; the remaining nine configurations
are absent from the local checkpoint pool. The per-config
faithfulness field in the reconstruction matches the recorded factorial_gen_collected.json within mean
 $|\Delta|=1.1\!\times\!10^{-4}$ across all 113 shared configurations,
confirming the reconstruction is faithful.

Sanity refit (D5 $\times$ D8 alone).

Fitting
faithfulness ~ C(D5)*C(D8) + mains on the
reconstructed 339-row table gives
Type-III $\eta^{2}_{\text{partial}}=0.201$ , $F(12,\,306)=6.40$ ,
 $p=3.8\!\times\!10^{-10}$ . This reproduces the previously reported $\eta^{2}=0.191$ within $\Delta=0.010$ ; the residual gap is
attributable to the nine missing configurations. We also confirm the
partial- $\eta^{2}$ definition is
 $\text{SS}_{\text{int}}/(\text{SS}_{\text{int}}+\text{SS}_{\text{resid}})$ 
rather than $\text{SS}_{\text{int}}/\text{SS}_{\text{total}}$ (the
latter yields 0.136 on the same fit, far from either value).

Pre-registered joint refit (two-track sensitivity).

An earlier H1 analysis reported
 $\eta^{2}_{\text{joint}}=0.546$ obtained by additively summing
per-pair Type-II SS across 28 rank-deficient interactions. This
double-counts shared variance (diagnostic: df sum $172{+}226{=}398$ 
exceeds $n{-}1{=}338$ , arithmetically impossible). We correct by
computing SS ${}_{\text{int,joint}}$ as a nested-SSR difference:
SS ${}_{\text{int}}=$ SSR ${}_{\text{reduced (mains only)}}-$ 
SSR ${}_{\text{full (mains + 28 pairs)}}$ , with df ${}_{\text{int}}=92$ 
(rank ${}_{\text{full}}-$ rank ${}_{\text{reduced}}$ , using min-norm
np.linalg.lstsq to avoid the smf.ols/pinv
rank-inflation observed with rcond $=10^{-15}$ ).
We report both granularities:

• 

Per-sample (primary, $n{=}4{,}820$ raw rows):
SS ${}_{\text{int}}{=}26.95$ (df=92), SSR ${}_{\text{full}}{=}652.97$ (df=4707),
 $\eta^{2}=\mathbf{0.040}$ , $F(92,\,4707){=}2.11$ , $p{=}4.8\!\times\!10^{-9}$ .
Config-clustered bootstrap (500 reps, seed 42): 95% CI $[0.022,\,0.042]$ ,
 $P(\eta^{2}{\geq}0.20){=}0$ . BIC $\Delta_{\text{full}-\text{red}}{=}+619.6$ ;
group-CV (5-fold on config_id) $R^{2}_{\text{full}}{=}{-}0.008$ 
vs $R^{2}_{\text{red}}{=}{+}0.024$ (interaction expansion hurts generalization).
Regen-only subset: $\eta^{2}{=}0.008$ ( $p{=}0.997$ ).
Agentic-only subset: $\eta^{2}{=}0.022$ ( $p{=}0.070$ ).

• 

Aggregated (sensitivity, 339 config $\times$ benchmark cells):
SS ${}_{\text{int}}{=}1.89$ (df=92), SSR ${}_{\text{full}}{=}2.53$ (df=226),
 $\eta^{2}=0.428$ , $F(92,\,226){=}1.84$ , $p{=}1.4\!\times\!10^{-4}$ .
Clustered bootstrap 95% CI $[0.28,\,0.46]$ , $P(\eta^{2}{\geq}0.20){=}1.00$ .
Caveat: aggregation over $\sim 14$ samples/cell collapses within-cell
variance into the interaction pocket, mechanically inflating both
SS ${}_{\text{int}}$ and $F$ ; BIC $\Delta{=}+347$ still favors mains-only.

Verdict. H1 is not confirmed at the pre-registered
 $\eta^{2}\!\geq\!0.20$ joint threshold. We
withdraw the per-sample specification as pseudo-replication
(within-config RAGAS samples are not independent trials of
component-composition effects); the aggregated fit ( $\eta^{2}{=}0.428$ )
is retained as sensitivity only (BIC $\Delta{=}+347$ favors
mains-only). Additivity is nonetheless affirmatively rejected at the
 $5\%$ floor in both tracks ( $p{\ll}0.001$ ). Under the pre-registered
per-pair Bonferroni procedure, reportable as an
independent inferential path rather than as a substitute for the joint
test, D5 $\times$ D8 clears both criteria ( $\eta^{2}{=}0.201$ ,
 $p{=}3.8\!\times\!10^{-10}$ ) and is retained as a pre-registered
large-effect follow-up finding.

Per-pair breakdown.

Under the joint fit, the strongest
individual pairs are D5 $\times$ D8 (Query Aug $\times$ Agentic Gate,
partial $\eta^{2}=0.201$ ), D5 $\times$ D6 (Query Aug $\times$ Reranking,
 $0.127$ ), D2 $\times$ D8 (Chunking $\times$ Agentic, $0.098$ ), and
D3 $\times$ D5 (Enrichment $\times$ Query Aug, $0.091$ ); the remaining
24 pairs each contribute $\eta^{2}<0.07$ . The joint effect is
therefore driven primarily by four interaction pairs but is not
localized to any single one, consistent with the LASAGNA thesis that
component interactions are distributed rather than concentrated.

Reproducibility artifacts.

The following artifacts are available from the authors on request:
paper/scripts/reconstruct_faithfulness_longform.py
(checkpoint aggregation),
paper/scripts/h1_joint_longform.py (single-pair + joint
refit),
paper/scripts/h1_joint_longform_bootstrap.py (CIs),
paper/data/faithfulness_longform_raw.jsonl
(4,820 per-sample rows),
paper/data/faithfulness_longform_by_benchmark.csv
(339 aggregated cells), and
paper/data/faithfulness_type3.json (joint numbers primary,
single-pair as reduced_model sensitivity). The nine missing
configurations from the original 122-config run are unavailable and
excluded from the reconstruction.

Degrees-of-freedom audit.

A natural question is how a 232-column joint model
can be estimated from 167 or 122 configuration observations.
Table 34 answers by tabulating $(\text{df}_{\text{int}},\text{df}_{\text{resid}})$ , their sum, and $n{-}1$ for every specification
we fit. Row 4 is the erroneous additive Type-II SS aggregation:
 $172{+}226{=}398>338{=}n{-}1$ is arithmetically impossible under a
single OLS fit, revealing the double-counting of shared variance.
Rows 1–3 and 5 satisfy $\text{df}_{\text{int}}{+}\text{df}_{\text{resid}}{=}n{-}1$ 
exactly (up to the mains block absorbed into the intercept), confirming
each fit is identified. The pre-registered 232-column joint at
per-config $n{=}113$ (row 3) is rank-deficient, this is why we escalate
to per-sample $n{=}4{,}820$ (row 5) for the primary H1 verdict.

Table 34: Identifiability audit for every H1 model fit. “df ${}_{\text{int}}$ ”
counts free parameters carrying interaction SS after Type-II SSR
differencing; “df ${}_{\text{resid}}$ ” is residual df at fit convergence.
Row 4 (gray) is the erroneous additive-SS aggregation identified by
the degrees-of-freedom audit.

Hierarchical reanalysis.

Our primary ANOVA operates on configuration
aggregates; we also verify that the D5 $\times$ D8 verdict survives a
model that treats query variability as a random effect. We refit the
per-sample data (n=2,425 rows with faithfulness scores
present, 43 unique queries, 57 configs) with two more conservative
specifications sharing the same fixed part
(D1–D8 mains plus the D5 $\times$ D8 interaction block):
(i) a MixedLM with a query random intercept, and
(ii) an OLS with cluster-robust standard errors clustered on
sample_id. The MixedLM yields
 $\chi^{2}(4){=}4.27$ , $p{=}0.371$ for the joint D5 $\times$ D8 Wald test,
ICC ${=}0.284$ (query intercept), marginal $R^{2}{=}0.224$ , conditional
 $R^{2}{=}0.445$ ; the cluster-robust OLS yields
 $F(4,2402){=}1.62$ , $p{=}0.168$ on the same block. The largest
individual interaction coefficient (D5=decomposition $\times$ 
D8=Speculative-RAG, $\hat{\beta}{\approx}0.09$ ) is common to both fits.
The naive aggregated $F(12,306){=}6.40$ , $p{=}3.8\!\times\!10^{-10}$ 
was inflated by treating the 43 queries as 306 residual df; once the
 $28.4\%$ of variance absorbed by the query intercept is modeled, the
D5 $\times$ D8 confirmation does not survive the hierarchical
analysis at Bonferroni or nominal $\alpha{=}0.05$ . We accordingly
downgrade the per-pair D5 $\times$ D8 result to the same
not confirmed status that the joint 28-two-way form already
carries, and treat the cell-level composition patterns in
Table 42 as descriptive rather than
preregistered evidence.
Reproducer, available from the authors on request: paper/scripts/m7_mixed_effects.py,
outputs paper/data/m7_mixed_effects.json.

## D.4 Power-law refit of scaling curves

The pre-registration specified a power-law fit
 $\mathrm{NDCG}(N)=a\cdot N^{-b}$ ; the main paper reports a
log-linear fit $\mathrm{NDCG}(N)=a+b\cdot\log_{10}(N)$ . We
refit both models on the same retrieval-NDCG@10 scaling points
(5K, 15K, 25K, 50K, 100K, 200K) for sota_standard and
sota_balanced using the identical data source
(paper/data/scaling_bootstrap_cis.json +
paper/data/scaling_100k_200k.json) and compare the fits
side by side (Tables 35 and 8).

Figure 8: Retrieval NDCG@10 vs. corpus size (log scale) under

conclusion does not depend on the fitting choice.

Across both fits, standard degrades with a magnitude of
 $|b|\approx 0.05$ and balanced with $|b|\approx 0.014$ :
the log-linear slope and the power-law exponent match to the
second decimal on both profiles, and the $R^{2}$ values agree to
within 0.02. The rank-flip finding (balanced overtakes
standard at large $N$ ) is therefore robust to the choice
of scaling-law functional form.

## D.5 P-value reconciliation across statistical tests

The main paper cites several p-values computed under different test
families (Type III ANOVA on the D5 $\times$ D8 grid, paired- $t$ and
Welch’s $t$ on scaling crossover, Wilcoxon signed-rank on H2
hypervolume, mixed-effects LRT). To make the multiple-comparisons
structure explicit, Table 36
enumerates every headline p-value in the main paper together with
its test family, null hypothesis, sample size (queries $\times$ 
seeds), effect size, and section reference.

Section E.1

Multiple-comparisons posture.

We apply Holm–Bonferroni within each test family (H1
per-pair uses the pre-registered per-pair Bonferroni $\alpha/28$ ;
H3 scaling crossover uses Holm across 4 corpus sizes; H2 is a
single Wilcoxon test with no family). A global correction across
all families in Table 36 would be
inappropriate because the tested hypotheses are logically
independent (interaction structure vs. scaling crossover vs. Pareto dominance) and would over-inflate Type II error on the
pre-registered H1 test. The two different p-values on the same H3
crossover (paired $t$ $p{=}0.003$ ; Welch’s $t$ $p{=}0.004$ ) reflect
different assumptions about within-seed correlation, not
different data; both survive within-family Holm adjustment.

## D.6 Scope of H3 uncertainty across corpus realizations

The reported H3 p-value is conditional on a single corpus realization.
The three execution seeds (42/43/44) resample retrieval and generation
stochasticity (HyDE draws, embedding-time nondeterminism, RAGAS judge
sampling) but hold corpus composition fixed: our 100K/200K distractor
set is a single deterministic realization built by
paper/scripts/build_100k_corpora.py (SHA-256
dbb94ab3…ac5f9a; see Section B.3.6),
in which HotpotQA documents are streamed in the BEIR adapter’s native
order, MD5-deduplicated, filtered against eval-relevant
doc_ids, and assigned scaling_{i:06d} IDs in
encounter order. The H3 confidence interval therefore does not
marginalize over the family of plausible 200K HotpotQA subsets a
reader could have drawn.

Two lines of already-collected evidence bound this from above without
a re-ingest:

1. 

Distractor-relevance audit
(Section B.3.5): an LLM-adjudicated stratified
audit of $n{=}100$ (query, distractor) pairs puts the qrel
contamination rate at $0/100$ relevant (95% BCa
 $[0.0,0.0]\%$ ) with $9/100$ partially relevant (95%
BCa $[4.0,15.0]\%$ ). The partial-relevance signal concentrates
in biomedical domains that are absent from scaling increments
5–6, so the specific 100K/200K realization is not adversarially
favorable to either profile.

2. 

Query-level paired bootstrap
(paper/data/scaling_bootstrap_cis.json, $B{=}10{,}000$ 
resamples over per-query NDCG@10 at fixed corpus): the profile
crossover between sota_standard and
sota_balanced at the 100K $\!\to\!$ 200K boundary
preserves its sign in every resample, ruling out that the
rank-flip is a query-sampling artifact orthogonal to seed
variance.

A fully-marginalized corpus-realization CI would require $B\!\geq\!3$ 
independently-shuffled 200K HotpotQA subsets, each re-ingested into
Qdrant per profile ( $\sim$ 4–6 h/ingest), and a two-way
random-effects ANOVA on faithfulness with corpus and seed as random
factors. We defer this to future work; the two bounds above cap the
corpus-realization contribution to the rank-flip below the audit noise
scale.

## D.7 H2 corpus scope and cross-corpus generalization study

The H2 headline corpus.

The H2 headline test in Table 2 is executed on a
SQuAD v2 / MuSiQue / HotpotQA mixture ( $43$ generation / $56$ 
retrieval queries per seed, three seeded runs). This mixture was
selected as the practical H2 slice after the MS MARCO and CUAD
substitutions (Appendix A) eliminated two
pre-registered benchmarks, and it satisfies the three properties
that make the H2 agentic-vs-retrieval contrast meaningful within our
compute envelope:

1. 

Multi-hop coverage.
HotpotQA (2-hop) and MuSiQue ( $\geq$ 3-hop) supply the
multi-document reasoning regime in which agentic aggressive
refinement is hypothesized to help and in which a single-shot
retriever is hypothesized to fail; SQuAD v2 supplies the
single-hop control needed to prevent the H2 contrast from
collapsing on multi-hop content alone.

2. 

Judge-noise control.
All three benchmarks have short, factual gold strings that
minimize RAGAS faithfulness-judge noise on the concise-regen
prompt regime declared in Table 26, so observed
NDCG $\times$ Faith deltas attribute to retrieval-composition
changes rather than to answer-style drift.

3. 

Pretraining-overlap control.
None of the three benchmarks appears verbatim in the reported
pretraining corpus of the generator or judge to a degree that
would let the faithfulness metric reward memorized answers.

Cross-corpus generalization study (deferred).

To extend H2 from this single mixture to genuinely unrelated
benchmarks, the following study grid is fully defined against
corpora already ingested in our Qdrant deployment:

• 

Corpora: TriviaQA (single-hop retrieval-heavy;
ingested for Fig. 3 and Table 22) and
Natural Questions (single-hop web queries; ingested for the
scaling sweep). No new ingestion required.

• 

Config grid: balanced vs. max-reliable $\times$ 3 seeds each per corpus,
12 runs total.

• 

Query slice: 300 queries per corpus, stratified by
hop-count to prevent corpus difficulty from confounding the
agentic-vs-retrieval contrast.

• 

Analysis: per-corpus NDCG $\times$ Faith and latency
deltas ( $\Delta=\text{Balanced}-\text{Max-Reliable}$ ) with
95% CIs; pooled random-effects meta-analysis
(DerSimonian–Laird) yielding a marginal cross-corpus estimate
with a between-corpus $\tau^{2}$ heterogeneity term.

• 

Cost: ${\sim}15$ GPU-hours (single overnight run).

This pre-registered grid was not executed in this study. A separate,
post-hoc replication of the H2 pattern at 100K and 200K corpus
scale, using scaling-experiment data already collected on the same
SQuAD v2 / MuSiQue / HotpotQA benchmark family under two profile
realizations on the H2 axis, is reported in
Section D.8. That replication tests
robustness to corpus SIZE (5K $\rightarrow$ 100K $\rightarrow$ 
200K) rather than to unrelated benchmark FAMILIES; the two are
complementary but not substitutes.

## D.8 Cross-corpus H2 pattern replication (post-hoc)

Section D.7 describes a pre-registered multi-corpus H2
grid extending H2 to unrelated benchmarks (TriviaQA, NQ); that
extension was not executed. This subsection reports a separate,
post-hoc replication that uses scaling-experiment data already
collected on the same benchmark family as the H2 headline test
(HotpotQA, MuSiQue, SQuAD v2), evaluated at 100K and 200K corpus
sizes with three seeds per (profile, corpus, size) cell. The
replication tests robustness of the balanced $>$ 
max-reliable pattern to corpus SIZE rather than to
benchmark family; it does not substitute for the deferred
cross-family grid and does not modify the H2 verdict of
Table 2 (which remains a single-slice test at the base
corpus). Its role is descriptive: to establish whether the
compositional pattern motivating H2, a lighter retrieval-focused
profile providing similar faithfulness with lower response time,
recurs at 100K and 200K scale on
the same benchmark family.

Profile realizations under test.

Both realizations sit on the H2 compositional axis and share the
embedding model, chunker, and generation LLM used in the H2 headline
run. The balanced-tier realization uses hybrid retrieval
( $\alpha{=}0.3$ ), top- $k{=}20$ retrieval breadth, extractive context
compression at $0.5$ ratio, and first-100-token document-awareness,
with CRAG, RAG-fusion, and query expansion disabled. Its mean
per-sample latency across the three benchmarks is $6.4$  s at 100K
and $8.3$  s at 200K. The max-reliable-tier realization
uses hybrid retrieval ( $\alpha{=}0.5$ ), a neural reranker
(mxbai-rerank-large-v1), HyDE query expansion at three draft
documents, extractive compression, and first-100-token
document-awareness. Its mean per-sample latency is $69.3$  s at 100K
and $134.3$  s at 200K. Both realizations sit on the compositional
axis probed by H2 (heavier query-side augmentation vs. broader
retrieval with light post-processing); they are two operating points
on that axis and are not identical to the profile pair in
Tables 2 and I.1, which is why we report this as
pattern replication rather than direct replication of the headline
test. Specifically, the balanced-tier realization used
here differs from the balanced profile specified in
Section I.1 in three ways (hybrid $\alpha{=}0.3$ vs. $0.5$ ,
HyDE disabled vs. three-variant, no neural reranker vs. mxbai-rerank-large-v1), and the max-reliable-tier
realization used here omits the agentic gate that
Section I.1 enables for the headline max-reliable
profile; per-sample latency comparisons below should therefore not be
read against the p95 numbers in Section I.1.

Protocol.

Faithfulness is scored by RAGAS with the GPT-5.1 judge used
throughout the paper (Section C.1.4). Answer generation
uses the pipeline’s deployment-native answer configuration
(Section C.2). Token-level $F_{1}$ is
computed against the benchmark gold answers. Per-sample latency is
recorded in the runner. For each (metric, benchmark, size) cell we
run a paired Wilcoxon signed-rank (two-sided) on the per-sample
delta $\Delta=\text{Balanced}-\text{Max-Reliable}$ , and apply
Holm-Bonferroni correction across the six cells within each metric
at $\alpha{=}0.05$ . Full per-sample data (paper/data/h2_cross_corroboration.json) and the analysis script (paper/scripts/h2_cross_corroboration.py) are available from the authors on request.

Table 37: Cross-corpus H2 replication, quality metrics. Per-cell
paired Wilcoxon signed-rank (two-sided) against $\Delta{=}0$ ; Holm
correction across the six cells within each metric.

Result.

Across $12$ (metric, benchmark, size) quality cells and $336$ 
paired-sample response-time comparisons, the balanced-tier
realization provides comparable measured quality with a consistent
response-time advantage over the max-reliable-tier
realization. Faithfulness deltas
are statistically indistinguishable in either direction across all
six benchmark-size cells (Holm-min $p=0.750$ ;
Table 37), as are token- $F_{1}$ 
deltas (Holm-min $p=0.398$ ). The lighter realization is faster on
every sample tested ( $336/336$ ; Table 38),
with a speedup ratio that grows from $10.0$ – $12.7\times$ at 100K
to $14.6$ – $20.0\times$ at 200K.
The direction of the latency advantage is stable across benchmarks
and both corpus sizes.

Scope of the corroboration.

This replication uses profile realizations that sit on the H2
compositional axis but are not identical to the pair in
Table 2. It is therefore not a direct replication of the
H2 headline test; the pre-registered multi-corpus comparison in
Section D.7 remains unexecuted. The compositional
H2 pattern (a lighter retrieval-focused profile providing similar
faithfulness with lower response time than a heavier configuration)
recurs at two corpus scales on the same
SQuAD v2 / MuSiQue / HotpotQA benchmark family as the H2 headline.
Because faithfulness gaps are non-significant in both directions
and the latency advantage holds on every sample, we treat the
observation as descriptive corroboration of the H2 pattern rather
than a new significance claim.

Joint HV under other fixed response-time references.

The main-paper HV in Table 2 uses a fixed
 $L_{\mathrm{ref}}{=}30$  s response-time reference.
Table 39 reports the joint HV under five fixed
references from real-time chat to a long reference, with NDCG@10,
faithfulness, and latency taken from Table 2 and HV
computed as $\mathrm{NDCG@10}\times\mathrm{Faith}\times\max(0,1-\mathrm{latency}/L_{\mathrm{ref}})$ ; per-profile HV values (paper/data/h2_hv_3d_sensitivity.json) are available from the authors on request.
standard has the highest joint HV at the $10$  s, $30$  s,
and $45$  s references; the ordering crosses over at
 $L_{\mathrm{ref}}{\approx}46$  s, above which balanced’s
faithfulness advantage dominates the extra $7$  s of response
time (balanced has the highest HV from $60$  s onward,
e.g., $0.609$ vs standard’s $0.579$ at $60$  s). Within
the agentic-route class, balanced $>$ max-reliable
at every reference that admits both. Under the $10$ -second
reference, both balanced and max-reliable have
mean response times exceeding the reference and score $0$ .
The deviation from the pre-registered $3$ D cost axis is documented
in Appendix A.

## D.9 Focused Confirmatory Experiment (E1): Pre-analysis Design

The exploratory factorial identified Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate as
the only interaction to survive Bonferroni on faithfulness with a Cohen-large
effect ( $\eta^{2}=0.201$ , aggregated). Two limitations of that analysis motivated
a focused confirmatory study: (i) the 232-parameter joint model is
under-identified against the executed 122 usable generation configurations; and
(ii) the same query set was applied across configurations, so hierarchical
inference at the query level was more appropriate than flat OLS.

The pre-analysis plan defined a focused, hierarchically analyzed study
(henceforth E1) isolating the Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate
subspace.

Design.

The pre-analysis design specified $5\times 4=20$ 
configurations (Query Augmentation
levels $\{$ none, HyDE, query-expansion, query-decomposition, RAG-Fusion $\}$ 
crossed with Agentic Gate levels $\{$ none, CRAG, Self-RAG (quality gates +
refinement), Speculative-RAG $\}$ ),
holding all other six pipeline dimensions constant at the Standard-profile
defaults. It specified the same 200 stratified queries for each configuration
(40 per benchmark from SQuAD v2, MuSiQue, HotpotQA, TriviaQA, FiQA), across
three seeds, for a total of $N{=}12{,}000$ per-sample observations.

Primary test.

The primary test was a hierarchical mixed-effects likelihood-ratio test
of full ( $\mathtt{faithfulness\sim C(D5)+C(D8)+C(D5)\!:\!C(D8)+(1|query\_id)}$ )
vs. reduced ( $\mathtt{faithfulness\sim C(D5)+C(D8)+(1|query\_id)}$ ) at
 $\alpha=0.05$ . At the planned $N{=}12{,}000$ , Cohen’s $f=0.10$ was detectable at
 $>99\%$ power against $\alpha=0.05$ ; the exploratory $\eta^{2}=0.20$ estimate
corresponds to Cohen’s $f=0.50$ , so the planned study was over-powered
against the effect of practical interest.

Preregistration.

The pre-analysis plan, configuration matrix,
query IDs, and analysis code were frozen and hashed under
paper/e1_focused_experiment/preregistration/ (SHA-256 hashes in
MANIFEST.json). Deviations from this plan are reported in
Section D.10.

Sensitivities.

The prespecified sensitivity analyses were:
cluster-robust OLS with query-level clustering; aggregated OLS BIC comparison
of full vs. reduced; grouped 5-fold cross-validation $R^{2}$ ; a $5{\times}4$ 
grid of cell means with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals.

Interpretation policy.

The prespecified policy treated rejection
of $H_{0}$ ( $p<0.05$ ) as confirmation under hierarchical inference. Under
non-rejection, the confirmatory result would supersede the exploratory
 $\eta^{2}=0.20$ estimate. The policy was confirmatory in either direction.

## D.10 E1 Executed Results

We executed the pre-registered $5{\times}4$ focused-confirmatory
experiment described in Section D.9. This subsection
reports the executed design (with transparent deviations from the
pre-analysis plan), the primary hierarchical test, the cell-mean
geometry, the surviving interaction contrasts, and the verdict on
H1’s pre-registered magnitude bar.

Executed design and deviations from the pre-analysis plan.

All 20 cells (Query Augmentation $\in$ {none, HyDE, query-expansion,
query-decomposition, RAG-Fusion} crossed with Agentic Gate $\in$ 
{none, CRAG, Self-RAG quality gates + refinement,
Speculative-RAG}) were executed. Faithfulness was scored by a
single LLM judge (GPT-5.1) under the concise-regen prompt regime
(see Tables 26 and C.2). The executed sample is $n_{\text{runs}}=3$ 
(three-seed confirmatory read, matching the pre-analysis plan in
Section D.9), yielding approximately $2{,}550$ per-sample
faithfulness observations over $43$ unique query sample_ids
 $\times$ $3$ seeds (vs. the pre-registered $N{=}12{,}000$ across
 $200$ queries $\times$ $3$ seeds). We report the query-count
shortfall as an explicit deviation from plan: seed replication meets
the pre-registration, but per-configuration query count is reduced
(rate-limit budget cut, Appendix A), so the
magnitude estimate (partial $\eta^{2}$ ) and per-contrast $p$ -values
should be read under this reduced query sampling. The pre-registered LRT
specification, the analysis code, and the executed cell-mean grid
are recorded under
paper/e1_focused_experiment/results/ with SHA-256 manifests
matching paper/e1_focused_experiment/preregistration/, and are available from the authors on request.

Primary test (hierarchical LRT).

Following the
pre-analysis plan, we compare the full mixed-effects model
 $\mathtt{faithfulness\sim C(D5)+C(D8)+C(D5)\!:\!C(D8)+(1\mid query\_id)}$ 
against the reduced no-interaction model
 $\mathtt{faithfulness\sim C(D5)+C(D8)+(1\mid query\_id)}$ 
(ML fits for the likelihood-ratio test) at $\alpha=0.05$ . The
interaction is retained:

 $\chi^{2}(12)=27.28,\quad p=0.00704,\quad\text{partial }\eta^{2}=0.033.$ 

The pre-registered decision rule is met on significance
( $p<0.05$ ) but not on magnitude (partial
 $\eta^{2}=0.033$ , well below the pre-registered $\eta^{2}\geq 0.20$ 
Cohen-large bar).

Cell-mean geometry.

Table 40
highlights the extreme cells of the $5{\times}4$ grid; the full grid (paper/e1_focused_experiment/results/cell_means.csv) is available from the authors on request. Mean
faithfulness spans $0.539$ (RAG-Fusion $\times$ CRAG, minimum) to
 $0.911$ (none $\times$ Self-RAG quality gates, maximum), a range
of $0.372$ . The minimum sits in the expansion-heavy
 $\times$ active-verifier corner and the maximum sits in the
quiet-query $\times$ moderate-verifier corner. These absolute extremes
motivate the cell-level analysis below, which compares each observed
combination with its additive prediction.

Table 40: E1 executed cell means: mean faithfulness (GPT-5.1 judge,
three-seed read, $n_{\text{runs}}=3$ ) for the extreme cells of the $5{\times}4$ 
Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate grid. Full grid available from the authors on request (paper/e1_focused_experiment/results/cell_means.csv).

Surviving interaction contrasts.

signal is concentrated in cells where one of the two axes is
disabled.
