Verdict on H1’s pre-registered decision rule.

The pre-registered decision rule (Section D.9) treats
significance and magnitude as separable criteria. On the executed
E1 read, the null of additivity is rejected at
 $p=0.007$ , but the observed partial $\eta^{2}=0.033$ is well
below the pre-registered $\eta^{2}\geq 0.20$ Cohen-large bar.
We therefore report E1 as confirmed as directional
(the interaction between Query Augmentation and the Agentic Gate
is statistically robust under hierarchical inference) and
not confirmed at the pre-registered magnitude (the
per-pair interaction on this axis is small, not the Cohen-large
effect implied by the exploratory $\eta^{2}=0.201$ estimate on the
same sub-space). Substantively, this refines H1 rather than
overturning it: composed RAG pipelines do exhibit real interactions
between stages, but on the specific
Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate axis those interactions
are small on top of the additive main effects, and the exploratory
 $\eta^{2}=0.201$ estimate on this sub-space is superseded by the
confirmatory $\eta^{2}=0.033$ .

Caveats specific to this confirmatory read.

Two caveats apply, and are echoed in the main paper’s Limitations
section: (i) query-count shortfall relative to plan
( $43$ unique queries $\times$ $3$ seeds ${\approx}2{,}550$ 
per-sample observations, versus the pre-registered $200$ queries
 $\times$ $3$ seeds ${=}12{,}000$ ), a deviation driven by GPT-5.1
rate-limit budget (Appendix A); seed replication
itself matches plan; (ii) the E1 grid holds all other six pipeline
dimensions at Standard-profile defaults, so generalization to other
stage settings is a scoped rather than universal claim.

Cell-level interaction structure (exploratory).

While the aggregate D5 $\times$ D8 interaction is small on the E1 grid,
cell-level departures from the additive main-effects prediction reveal
specific compositional patterns that isolated single-component
ablations cannot see. Table 42 lists the top
five antagonistic and top five synergistic cells ranked by
 $|$ residual $|$ ; ten of twenty cells deviate from the additive
prediction by ${\geq}0.02$ faithfulness, evenly split (five
antagonistic, five synergistic), with residuals spanning $[-0.050,+0.047]$ . Two structural patterns are visible: (i) Query
Decomposition synergizes with the active agentic gates: its two
largest positive residuals are with CRAG ( $+0.039$ ) and
Speculative-RAG ( $+0.047$ ), but it antagonizes the no-gate condition
( $-0.050$ ), i.e. multi-part queries need an active verifier
downstream to pay off; (ii) Speculative-RAG antagonizes passive or
lightly-augmented queries (residuals of $-0.041$ with both raw
queries and Query Expansion) yet synergizes with structured
decomposition ( $+0.047$ ), i.e. its parallel draft-and-verify
mechanism is worth the latency only when the upstream query already
carries structure. Both patterns are invisible to single-factor
ablations: a practitioner who tunes D5 and D8 independently would
predict the additive main-effects surface and miss up to
 $\pm 5$ -point cell-level swings against that prediction. This is the
compositional evidence that motivates factorial testbeds like
lasagna even when the aggregate interaction term is small.
The seven cells whose D8 level lies in {CRAG, Self-RAG,
Speculative-RAG} were re-scored by the pairwise-BTL follow-up in
Section C.5, which on a subsample deliberately biased
toward small RAGAS deltas returned $79\%$ ties and Spearman $\rho=-0.36$ between BTL and RAGAS configuration ranks; those seven
cell-level directions should therefore be read as protocol-dependent
descriptive patterns rather than judge-invariant orderings. The
five D8 $=$ none cells of the $5{\times}4$ grid, including
the Query-Decomposition $\times$ none residual of $-0.050$ named
above, sit outside the BTL sample and are not certified by BTL
either way. The within-agentic three-level D8 ordering
(Speculative-RAG $>$ Self-RAG $>$ CRAG) that BTL directly probes via
item (ii) of Section C.5 is protocol-robust; the
four-level D8 marginal that includes none
(Section D.2) is not independently corroborated by BTL
because the BTL sample contains no D8 $=$ none configurations.

## D.11 Exploratory Factorial Probes

Prior to the full factorial ANOVA, we conducted six $2{\times}2$ 
factorial probes on the TriviaQA eval split (76 queries) targeting
four key components: reranking, hybrid alpha, MMR, and HyDE.
The interaction is estimated via the difference-in-differences (DiD)
estimator:

 $\widehat{\tau}_{\text{int}}=\bigl(\bar{y}_{11}-\bar{y}_{10}\bigr)-\bigl(\bar{y}_{01}-\bar{y}_{00}\bigr),$ 

(12)

where $\bar{y}_{ij}$ is mean NDCG@10 for factor levels $i,j$ .
Confidence intervals are constructed from a paired bootstrap (10,000
resamples, query-level pairing) with bias-corrected and accelerated
(BCa) adjustment.

confirmatory). DiD estimates the interaction magnitude; 95% CIs from
paired BCa bootstrap over 76 queries. Holm-corrected $p$ -values
account for six comparisons. None of the six probes survives
Holm correction and we therefore report them as descriptive, not
confirmatory; the confirmatory interaction analysis is the 167-config
factorial ANOVA in Section 5.

The strongest probe (Reranking $\times$ Hybrid Alpha,
DiD $=-0.110$ ) suggests that combining reranking with adjusted
hybrid alpha yields 38% less improvement than the sum of individual
effects. HyDE $\times$ Reranking (DiD $=-0.066$ ) suggests that
cross-encoder reranking subsumes much of HyDE’s benefit.

Held-out validation.

We tested the two strongest probes on the held-out BEIR test split
(96 queries across 5 domains). Reranking $\times$ MMR replicated
exactly (test DiD $=-0.058$ ), confirming the reranker’s subsumption
of MMR diversity effects. Reranking $\times$ Hybrid Alpha showed a
sign reversal (test DiD $=+0.027$ ), indicating that this interaction
is query-set dependent and should not be treated as a stable finding.
This instability motivated the full 167-configuration factorial ANOVA
(Section 5), which provides more robust interaction
estimates across the full 8-dimensional design space.

## D.12 Feature Dropout Analysis

To probe interaction effects between features and pipeline profiles, we
conduct a feature dropout analysis: for each of the four composed profiles,
we disable one feature at a time and measure the change in NDCG@10.
Table 44 reports the results. A negative $\Delta$ NDCG indicates
that removing the feature hurts performance (i.e., the feature is
beneficial in that profile); a positive $\Delta$ NDCG indicates that removing
the feature helps (i.e., the feature is harmful in that profile).
This analysis is exploratory.

composed profile. Cells highlighted in red indicate
features that help in one profile but hurt in another (interaction signal).
All values are on the test split. Pooled across profiles, re-enabling MMR
costs NDCG@10 (paired $t=-3.11$ , $p=0.003$ ; 95% CI on the drop
 $[-0.062,\,-0.013]$ ), opposite its typical open-domain benefit.

Interaction signals.

Several features exhibit profile-dependent behavior, providing evidence of
non-zero interactions. HyDE improves NDCG@10 in the Max-Reliable profile
(dropout $\Delta=-0.031$ ) but marginally hurts the balanced profile
(dropout $\Delta=+0.008$ ), suggesting that HyDE’s benefit depends on
whether a reranker is present to filter its occasionally noisy hypothetical
documents. Context reordering helps in Max-Reliable ( $-0.003$ ) but hurts
in balanced ( $+0.015$ ), likely because the reranker already places the most
relevant passages at optimal positions. Query expansion consistently hurts
when enabled ( $+0.018$ on removal), confirming the single-feature ablation
finding (Section D.1). Agentic RAG removal improves NDCG@10 by
 $+0.021$ in Max-Reliable, consistent with the aggressive refinement effect
discussed in Section 5.

Robust features.

Disabling MMR (i.e., re-enabling it) consistently hurts across all four
profiles, confirming that MMR’s diversity penalty is counterproductive on
our domain-focused corpus regardless of other active features. Document
awareness (100-token prefix) also provides stable gains across all profiles
where it is active, with dropout deltas ranging from $-0.041$ to $-0.055$ .

## D.14 The Composition Penalty

This appendix visualizes the central finding of the paper: that
composing individually beneficial RAG components yields smaller
improvements than the sum of their independent effects.

Figure 9: Waterfall chart of individual component gains stacked on the
default_standard baseline (NDCG@10 $=0.412$ ).
The dashed red line shows the sum of independent gains (0.667);
the solid green line shows the actual composed result
(sota_balanced, NDCG@10 $=0.483$ ; 95% CI on the
realized lift $[+0.058,\,+0.084]$ , paired bootstrap over 93
queries). The gap represents a 72% composition penalty: the
composed system captures only 28% of the total independent
improvement. This visualizes the non-additivity that motivates
factorial evaluation.

Interpretation.

The waterfall in Fig. 9 illustrates why single-factor
ablations are misleading for RAG pipeline design. Each bar represents
the marginal gain of one component when evaluated in isolation against
the baseline. A practitioner who selects components based on these
independent deltas would expect a combined NDCG@10 of 0.667
( $=0.412+0.084+0.062+0.046+0.037+0.026$ ). The actual
composed result is 0.483, a shortfall of 0.184 ( $72\%$ of the expected
improvement is lost to interactions).

This penalty arises because several components partially duplicate
each other’s function. For example, both HyDE and cross-encoder
reranking improve query-passage alignment, and when both are active
the marginal gain of each is smaller than when either operates alone.
Similarly, disabling MMR removes a diversity penalty that the reranker
already subsumes, so the independent gain from disabling MMR overlaps
with the gain from adding a reranker.

The factorial probes in Section 5 quantify these
pairwise interactions, and the waterfall shows their aggregate effect
on the composed system.

Exploratory evidence for faithfulness additivity.

We compare marginal faithfulness effects across the $113$ 
configurations with complete generation-side records. Four positive
one-component contrasts contribute $+0.014$ for BGE-large embedding,
 $+0.036$ for hierarchical chunking, $+0.018$ for hybrid-BM25
retrieval, and $+0.126$ for Speculative-RAG gating. Adding these
marginals to a reference score of $0.692$ predicts $0.886$ ( $+28\%$ );
the best observed complete configuration reaches $0.911$ ( $+32\%$ ).
The reference, marginal contrasts, and best configuration do not form
one exactly matched component stack, so their closeness is not a
controlled additivity test. It is an exploratory signal that
faithfulness may compose more nearly additively for some methods than
retrieval does, and motivates testing the exact combinations directly
rather than assuming either additivity or a composition penalty.

Table 47: Exploratory faithfulness comparison. The selected marginal
effects and best observed configuration are not the same exact
component stack; their proximity motivates a matched composition test
but does not establish additivity.

## E.2 Latency Scaling Behavior

This appendix complements the quality-focused scaling analysis in
Section 5 with latency measurements across corpus sizes.

## E.2.1 Retrieval Latency vs. Corpus Size

Figure 10 shows P95 latency as a function of corpus
size for both the retrieval and E2E generation tracks.

Figure 10: P95 latency vs. corpus size. (a) Retrieval track:
standard and balanced grow sub-linearly on a
log–log scale, while speed remains below 300 ms at all
sizes. (b) E2E generation track at 25K and 50K.
Note the log scale on both axes.

Key observations.

Retrieval latency scales sub-linearly with corpus size: a 10 $\times$ 
expansion from 5K to 50K increases standard P95 latency by
approximately 87% (not 10 $\times$ ), reflecting the logarithmic
complexity of approximate nearest-neighbor search in vector databases.
The speed profile maintains P95 $<$ 300 ms across all corpus
sizes, confirming its suitability for latency-critical deployments.
The balanced profile shows better latency scaling than
standard (65% growth vs. 87%), likely because context
compression reduces the downstream reranking burden.

Practical implication.

At 50K documents, practitioners choosing between standard
(P95 $\approx$ 7 s) and balanced (P95 $\approx$ 18 s)
face a latency–faithfulness trade-off: balanced offers
0.93 faithfulness at 2.6 $\times$ the latency of standard
(0.79 faithfulness). The speed profile remains the only
option for sub-second latency requirements, at the cost of
substantially lower retrieval quality.

## E.3 Scaling Projections

This appendix extends the log-linear scaling fits from Section 5
to project retrieval quality at corpus sizes beyond our measured range.

Figure 11: Observed NDCG@10 and log-linear extrapolations for all four SOTA
profiles. Solid lines (5K–200K) are measured; dotted lines with
shaded bands (200K–1M) are 95% prediction intervals from the
per-profile log-linear fit. balanced’s band widens
rapidly because its slope is not distinguishable from zero
( $p=0.175$ ), informative about its scale-robustness, but
inflating projection uncertainty.

Projection methodology.

We fit $\text{NDCG@10}=a\cdot\log_{10}(N)+b$ to all six measured
data points (5K/15K/25K/50K/100K/200K) per profile and extrapolate to
500K and 1M. Prediction intervals are 95%, using the residual
standard deviation and Student- $t$ critical value for $n-2=4$ 
degrees of freedom. Table 49 reports measured
values (5K–200K) alongside projections (500K, 1M∗).

Table 49: NDCG@10 across corpus sizes: measured values (5K–200K; 3-seed
means at 25K–200K, single-seed at 5K/15K) and 500K/1M∗ projections
from log-linear fit with 95% prediction interval.

 $+$ 0.012
 $+$ 0.033
 $+$ 0.021
 $+$ 0.059
 $+$ 0.064
 $+$ 0.077

∗Projected (95% prediction interval); all other columns measured.

Interpretation.

The measured balanced–standard gap is noisy at
intermediate scales (positive at 25K but within noise; negative-trend
at 15K), then firms at 200K where balanced exceeds
standard by $+0.059$ NDCG@10 (Welch’s $t$ , $p=0.004$ ,
 $n=3$ seeds per cell). At the 1M projection the 95% prediction
intervals overlap, balanced’s lower bound ( $0.423$ ) sits
below standard’s upper bound ( $0.448$ ), so the
separation is not statistically distinguishable at 1M and the point
estimate ( $+0.077$ ) should be read only as a directional continuation
of the measured 200K gap. The interpretation that context
compression and document-awareness prefixes act as implicit noise
filters is therefore supported by the measured 200K result and the
monotone trend 50K $\to$ 200K, not by the 1M extrapolation.

Caveats.

Projections assume (i) the log-linear relationship holds beyond 200K,
(ii) the distractor distribution remains representative of real-world
corpus growth, and (iii) the vector backend scales without
architectural changes. Assumption (i) is on empirically solid ground
for speed and standard ( $R^{2}\geq 0.89$ ) but weaker
for balanced and max-reliable, whose slopes are
not distinguishable from zero, their 1M projections reflect
uncertainty about whether the flat trend will hold rather than about
the form of a decay law. We present 500K/1M values as directional
estimates to motivate future large-scale validation, not as confirmed
predictions.

## Appendix F Experiment Inventory

The headline analyses in the main text rest on the 167-configuration
D-optimal fractional factorial (retrieval ANOVA) and the 122-configuration
faithfulness factorial. Beyond that core design, we ran 24
targeted ablation and model-comparison experiments totalling 138
distinct configurations, plus the 5K–200K corpus-scaling sweep
(18 cells $\times$ 1–3 seeds $=$ 72 runs; see
Sections 5 and B.3.9). Combined with the factorial
executions this yields $\sim$ 450 distinct configuration runs
across eight benchmarks. This appendix indexes every named experiment
so readers can trace any headline claim to the underlying measurements.

grouped by what it tests. Columns report the number of distinct
configurations evaluated and the primary benchmark. Full per-profile
scaling results live in Section 5; the 167- and 122-run
factorials are summarized in Section 5 with full
 $\eta^{2}$ decompositions in Sections 5 and D.2.

runs of that experiment (multi-seed replications not double-counted).
The $\sum N=138$ configurations plus 167 retrieval + 122

SOTA 2025–2026
0.3422
 $-0.1115$ 

bge_reranker_v2_gemma
SOTA 2025–2026
0.3366
 $-0.1171$ 

The SOTA-2025–2026 rerankers (Jina-v2, BGE-v2-m3, BGE-v2-gemma)
underperform on our benchmark, which we attribute to
domain-specialization: those models are tuned for web/MTEB-style
queries whereas our evaluation set blends BEIR (nfcorpus, fiqa,
scifact, arguana, hotpotqa) with QA-style prompts that favor the
older MS-MARCO-trained cross-encoders. The LLM-listwise reranker underperforms on our benchmark, with both
window settings dominated by older cross-encoders (window $=10$ :
NDCG@10 $=0.405$ , $2{,}649$  ms; window $=5$ : NDCG@10 $=0.354$ ,
 $5{,}441$  ms). The window-size effect has two compounding
mechanical sources rather than a single anomaly: (i) at
window $=5$ , reranking the top-20 requires four sequential LLM calls
versus two at window $=10$ , doubling wall-clock latency before any
quality consideration;
(ii) each smaller-window call sees fewer candidates and cannot
reason globally about the ranking, a known limitation of LLM
listwise scoring that our results are consistent with rather than
novel evidence for. We tested only two window sizes on a single
93-query benchmark with one judge model, so the directional gap
should be read as confirming known LLM-listwise behavior under our
specific infrastructure rather than as a generalizable scaling claim;
a full sweep over windows $\in\{3,5,8,10,15,20\}$ with multiple
seeds would be needed to characterize the curve shape.

Two findings emerge. First, Matryoshka truncation to 768 dimensions
(dim_768, NDCG@10 $=0.489$ ) slightly exceeds the full
1024-dim embedding ( $0.478$ ), suggesting the final 256 dimensions of
BGE-M3 add more noise than signal for our benchmark. Second, late
chunking provides a substantial uplift for BGE-large
( $0.460\to 0.493$ , $+0.033$ ) but degrades BGE-M3
( $0.459\to 0.361$ , $-0.098$ ). We attribute the BGE-M3 regression to
its multilingual tokenizer producing shorter effective contexts under
late chunking, reducing the signal each chunk carries.

## F.7 Judge Model Sensitivity

 $\pm 0.08$ shift in absolute faithfulness between GPT-5.1 and

## F.8 Third-Judge Robustness Extension

The two-judge sensitivity analysis reported in
Sections F.7 and C.1.4 covered $n{=}80$ 
matched samples across two LLM judges (GPT-5.1, Claude Sonnet 4.5).
To probe whether the observed cross-judge robustness generalizes to
a third, independently-drawn LLM annotator, we ran an extended
sensitivity study. This is an LLM-scored sensitivity
extension, not additional human evaluation; the $n{=}50$ dyadic
human study in Section C.4 remains the sole human-judged
calibration in this paper.

Method.

We drew a $n{=}220$ deduplicated
 $(\texttt{sample\_id},\texttt{generated\_answer})$ QA-triple sample
(seed $=42$ ) from the 253-triple population produced by all completed
lasagna generation runs. A third LLM
annotator (Claude Opus 4.7) scored each triple on the
 $\{0.0,0.25,0.5,0.75,1.0\}$ ordinal scale using the same
faithfulness-scoring instructions as the primary evaluation
(Section C.4), without seeing the RAGAS score.

Agreement with the canonical RAGAS/GPT-5.1 judge.

Pearson $r{=}0.436$ , Spearman $\rho{=}0.403$ , MAE ${=}0.245$ ,
RMSE ${=}0.395$ . Binary agreement at the $0.5$ midpoint is $78.2\%$ 
with Cohen’s $\kappa{=}0.353$ (fair agreement). Per-sample
absolute-difference bands: $50.9\%$ within $\pm 0.10$ , $53.6\%$ 
within $\pm 0.20$ , $68.6\%$ within $\pm 0.30$ . Mean scores are
similar in aggregate (Opus 0.697 vs. RAGAS 0.727), but the third
annotator is stricter on partial-support cases: RAGAS assigns the
maximum score $1.0$ to $126/220$ samples versus $113/220$ for the
third annotator, which redistributes mass into the $0.25$ and $0.5$ 
buckets.

Interpretation.

Fair kappa ( $0.35$ ) combined with
moderate Pearson $r{=}0.44$ demonstrates that the faithfulness
signal survives judge substitution but is not judge-invariant:
this is consistent with the two-judge $n{=}80$ result
( $\tau_{b}{=}0.38$ ) reported in Section C.1.4, and reinforces
the paper’s headline policy of reporting deltas and rankings rather
than absolute faithfulness magnitudes. Because Opus 4.7 was not
seen by either RAGAS or Sonnet 4.5 in training-style overlap that
would inflate agreement, the persistence of the same qualitative
pattern across a third, independent judge is directionally
supportive of the paper’s judge-robustness claim.

Raw per-sample scores, deltas, and decision-reason categories (paper/e1_focused_experiment/results/track1_llm_annotator_scores.json) are available from the authors on request.

## F.9 Generator Substitution: Does the Faithfulness Ordering Hold?

Every faithfulness number elsewhere in this paper uses a single
answer-generating model. This appendix asks whether the profile-level
conclusions survive replacing it.

What varies, and what is held fixed.

For each of the four profiles we reuse the documents that profile had
already retrieved, so retrieval is untouched and no pipeline is re-run.
We hold the answer prompt, the RAGAS judge, and the decoding temperature
( $T{=}0$ ) fixed, and change only the model that writes the answer:
GPT-5.1 (generator A, the generator used throughout the paper) and
Claude Sonnet 4.5 (generator B). The design is $43$ questions
 $\times$ $4$ profiles $\times$ $3$ seeds per arm, giving $510$ paired
comparisons. One common answer prompt is applied to all four profiles
in both arms, so the absolute values below are not commensurable with
Table 2, which reports each profile under its own
deployment-native prompt. The quantity of interest here is the
difference between the two arms, not the difference from that table.

Table 57: Faithfulness under two generators, with retrieved documents,
answer prompt, and judge held fixed. Paired Wilcoxon signed-rank on
per-question scores.

Profile
Generator A
Generator B
Change (paired test)

standard
0.758
0.833
 $+0.075$ ( $p=0.007$ )

balanced
0.765
0.792
 $+0.027$ (not significant)

max-reliable
0.687
0.771
 $+0.083$ ( $p=0.08$ )

speed
0.565
0.749
 $+0.184$ ( $p<0.001$ )

What holds.

Every profile scores higher under generator B
(Table 57), so absolute faithfulness is
generator-dependent. That is the empirical reason this paper compares
profiles by rank rather than treating absolute faithfulness as a fixed
property of a pipeline. The substantive conclusion is unchanged: under
both generators the two heavier agentic profiles gain no faithfulness
advantage over the lighter direct-generation profile, and the bottom two
positions are identical in both arms.

What does not hold.

The fine-grained ordering is not invariant. standard and
balanced exchange ranks between the arms, although they differ
by only $0.007$ under generator A, which is inside the seed-to-seed
variation we observe. With four profiles, rank agreement across arms is
high but not statistically resolvable (Spearman $\rho{=}0.80$ , Pearson
 $r{=}0.83$ ). Two generators is a sensitivity check and not a sweep, and
both models are proprietary.

Machinery and generator capability partly substitute.

The benefit of a stronger generator is markedly uneven. It is largest
for the lightest pipeline ( $+0.184$ ) and smallest for the heaviest
( $+0.027$ , not significant). Pipeline machinery and generator capability
therefore appear partly interchangeable: a heavier component stack buys
less faithfulness when the generator underneath it is already strong.
We treat this as a composition result rather than a robustness check,
which is why it also appears in the main text.

## F.10 Cross-Cutting Findings from the Ablation Suite

Several ablation-level findings warrant flagging, all traceable to
specific rows in the tables above:

• 

HyDE with 1 document beats HyDE with 3 or 5.
hyde_1doc (0.5123 NDCG@10) leads the clean HyDE sweep,
whereas multi-document HyDE degrades slightly
(hyde_5docs 0.495, hyde_3docs_maxsim 0.494).
Multi-doc HyDE adds averaged noise that blunts dense similarity.

• 

SPLADE underperforms BM25 on our domain.
bm25_baseline (0.460) outperforms all SPLADE variants
( $\leq 0.372$ ); the factorial uses BM25 as the sparse branch,
consistent with this result.

• 

Contextual retrieval slightly hurts.
contextual_on (0.451) trails no_contextual
(0.460); the SOTA profiles disable this feature.

• 

Chunking strategy has a 12-point NDCG@10 swing.
In Table 55, the best chunker
(docling_hier_1600_400, 0.475) beats the worst
(semantic_1200, 0.349) by 0.126 absolute, larger than
most single-feature deltas in the factorial.

• 

Agentic gates help faithfulness, not NDCG.
In Table 54 the Agentic RAG Ablation
sub-block shows NDCG@10 effectively constant across 17 agentic
configurations (0.707 across the board) while
Faith ${}_{\textsc{{a}}}$ spans $[0.88,0.93]$ ; agentic iterations move
generation fidelity, not retrieval.

## F.11 Caveats and Scope

• 

Smoke tests excluded. Runs with
“Smoke” in the experiment name are omitted; they use
truncated sample sets and are not comparable to the production
configurations reported here.

• 

242 planned vs. 167 executed. The retrieval
factorial was pre-registered at 242 configurations; the 75-run
shortfall traces to MPS/MTLCompilerService crashes and enterprise-proxy
rate-limit exhaustion during the live-mode sweep. The executed
167 retain 99.3% D-efficiency against the pre-registered design.

• 

Multi-seed replication not double-counted. The
scaling sweep runs three seeds at 25K–200K, yielding $4\times 2\times 3=24$ runs per profile ( $96$ total across all four
profiles). These are counted as 18 distinct configuration
cells, not 96, to avoid inflating the headline number.

• 

Judge-model consistency. All ablations, subsequent
experiments, and scaling sweeps use GPT-5.1 as the RAGAS judge.
Judge-comparison Exp. 06 establishes that the choice shifts absolute
faithfulness by $\pm 0.08$ but preserves profile ranking.

• 

Environment-limited runs retained with annotation.
Three embedding configurations (Stella-1.5B, E5-large-v2,
BGE-large-EN) produced anomalous NDCG values tied to model-loading
or session-config issues in the sweep infrastructure. We retain
them in Table 52 rather than dropping them
so the reader can verify the reported failure mode; they do not
inform any quantitative claim in the main text.

Two entries deserve a note. The
Judge-model comparison row (Sonnet 4.5 vs GPT-5.1) is
deliberately not recomputed against the common judge; it is
itself the anchor establishing the $\pm 0.08$ absolute-scale bound
reported in Section C.1.4. The
length-controlled rerun row (Section C.3)
uses three prompt regimes drawn from the identical instruction
skeleton with only length guidance varied; its
faith ${}_{\textsc{{a}}}$ is reported both raw and after
regressing on log answer-token count and adding back the grand mean.
The residualized column is the object used for any cross-prompt
claim, because raw scores are confounded with answer length
( $\beta{=}{-}0.126$ per $+100$ tokens; the concise-over-verbose gap
reverses under residualization, see Table 28).

## Appendix G External Comparisons

## G.1 Prior-Evaluation Table Definitions

Table 1 compares the evaluation methodology
reported in each cited study. “Configs” gives the number and mode
of evaluated configurations, followed in parentheses by the number
of pipeline stages varied; “methods” denotes bundled methods,
“seq.” sequential component selection, “joint” simultaneous
variation, “partial” an incomplete grid, and “abl.” targeted
ablations. “Design” gives the search strategy: library, grid,
greedy search, or D-optimal fractional factorial (d-opt);
“tech.” denotes algorithmic techniques and “mix.” a mixture of
techniques and hyperparameters. “ $\eta^{2}$ ” indicates whether effect
sizes are reported, and “Interactions” counts statistically
estimated two-way effects. “Scaling” reports corpus sizes and
seeded runs; “Pareto” reports jointly optimized objectives and
hypervolume (HV); and “Pre-registered” indicates whether the study
hypotheses and analysis were specified in advance. A checkmark,
cross, and open circle denote yes, no, and partial coverage.
“Benchmarks” gives the number evaluated; lasagna uses 8 of
the 15 benchmarks supported by the framework.

## G.2 SOTA Method Comparison

We compare five retrieval methods on the BEIR evaluation split (5,132
documents, 76 queries), holding the generation pipeline constant (standard,
no agentic). Table 59 reports NDCG@10, the delta relative to the
BM25 baseline, and the per-query latency overhead. All methods use the
same hybrid fusion ( $\alpha{=}0.5$ where applicable) and identical
top- $k{=}20$ retrieval depth.

Table 59: Retrieval method comparison on the BEIR evaluation split.
Latency overhead is reported per query relative to bge-m3 dense-only
retrieval ( ${\sim}178$  ms per query, not shown as a table row).
BM25 serves as the NDCG sparse baseline.

Method
NDCG@10
 $\Delta$ vs BM25
Latency overhead

ColBERT v2

mxbai-rerank-large (cross-encoder)

ms-marco-MiniLM-L6 (cross-encoder)

Key findings.

ColBERT achieves the highest NDCG@10 (0.4616), marginally outperforming the
ms-marco-MiniLM-L6 cross-encoder reranker by $+0.005$ . However, ColBERT
requires per-token index storage that scales linearly with corpus size,
making it substantially more expensive to deploy at larger scales. The
lighter cross-encoder reranker achieves comparable quality at lower indexing
cost and is therefore used in our composed profiles.

SPLADE underperforms BM25 by $-0.012$ NDCG@10 on our domain-focused corpus.
We attribute this to the terminological consistency of BEIR domain subsets:
SPLADE’s learned term expansion provides less benefit when the vocabulary
gap between queries and documents is already small. This finding is
specific to our corpus characteristics and may not hold on open-domain
collections.

The mxbai-rerank-large model performs slightly below ms-marco-MiniLM-L6
( $-0.009$ ), despite its larger parameter count. We note that
mxbai-rerank-large was trained on a broader data mixture, which may reduce
its effectiveness on the academic and technical domains represented in our
BEIR subset.

## G.3 External Baseline Comparison

We provide two complementary external comparisons: (i) executable
baselines run within lasagna on our evaluation corpus using
common open-source RAG configurations (Table 60), and
(ii) published BEIR leaderboard scores for directional positioning
(Table 61). Paper claims about relative improvement
use the executable baselines; the literature table provides broader
context only.

## G.3.1 Executable Baselines

Table 60 reports four reference configurations run
on our private evaluation corpus (5,132 documents, 93 retrieval
queries, 56 generation queries) under identical corpus,
top- $k{=}20$ , tokenizer, and embedding-backbone conditions to the
SOTA profiles.
Parity caveat. The NDCG@10 columns for the two baseline
rows are computed on the 93-query retrieval-isolation split
(private_eval/retrieval, qrels from that split), whereas
the two SOTA rows report end-to-end NDCG@10 on the 56-query
generation split (private_eval/e2e_generation, distinct
qrels); the two splits differ in query count and gold-label set and
are therefore not strictly commensurable in absolute NDCG. On the
same retrieval-isolation split, sota_standard scores
 $0.510$ (Section J.2.2) vs. $0.436$ for
bge-m3 recommended, a $+0.074$ composition delta that
decomposes into disabling MMR ( $+0.046$ ), adding 3-variant HyDE
( $+0.026$ ), hybrid $\alpha{=}0.5$ vs. $0.7$ , and cross-encoder
top- $10$ vs. top- $5$ . The larger apparent gap in the table
(0.860 vs. 0.436) is therefore inflated by the split difference,
not the retrieval stack; we retain both numbers for practitioner
reference but note that only the retrieval-isolation comparison
( $0.510$ vs. $0.436$ ) is implementation-parity. These four rows
represent common open-source RAG deployments a practitioner might
use out of the box.

## G.4 Framework Comparison to BERGEN and FlashRAG

The related-work discussion (Table 1) positions
LASAGNA against two prominent RAG benchmarking libraries, BERGEN
(Rau et al., 2024) and FlashRAG (Jin et al., 2025), and
argues that both lack the stage isolation that controlled
interaction estimation requires
(Section H.1). In this appendix we report the operational
follow-through of that positioning: an install-and-smoke pass
against both frameworks on shared infrastructure, alongside a
LASAGNA analog on the same corpus/retriever/LLM. We frame the
result honestly: this is a positional smoke run, not a
head-to-head benchmark comparison, because the only cross-framework
corpus we could stand up in the time budget is topically mismatched
to the queries it ships with.

What ran.

FlashRAG installed cleanly in an isolated Python 3.10 environment
and executed its SequentialPipeline end-to-end on the
shipped $17$ -query Natural Questions dev subset against
FlashRAG’s own general_knowledge.jsonl corpus (15,000
short passages) using BM25 top- $5$ and a $120$ B-parameter
open-weights LLM served via a hosted inference endpoint
(wall clock $11.5$  s). A
LASAGNA-style analog was run against the same $17$ queries, same
corpus, same LLM, and same BM25 retriever, using the
exp07-verified concise-answer prompt from
Section C.2 (wall clock $7.7$  s). BERGEN installation
was blocked on Apple Silicon by a hard tensorflow==2.8.0
pin (no macOS-ARM wheel); the pinned TensorFlow dependency is
never actually imported in BERGEN’s Python source (only
referenced as strings inside a Gorilla dataset processor), so a
slimmed requirements.txt installs cleanly, but a smoke
run was not attempted in this budget.

Numbers ( $17$ -query smoke; identical retriever, corpus, LLM).

Table 62: Framework comparison, $17$ -query smoke on FlashRAG’s
shipped AI-topic corpus with open-domain Natural Questions
queries. This measures each framework’s default handling of
out-of-corpus queries under identical retriever and generator, not
RAG quality on a matched benchmark.

System
EM
Loose-F1
Refusal rate

FlashRAG SequentialPipeline (default prompt)
0.059
0.059
64.7%

LASAGNA analog (Section C.2 concise prompt)
0.353
0.126
0.0%

Interpretation and scope.

The FlashRAG default Standard-RAG prompt is stricter
(“Answer the question based on the given document. Only give
me the answer and do not output any other words.”); the LLM
correctly refuses on $11/17$ queries because the AI-topic corpus
does not contain trivia answers. LASAGNA’s concise-regen prompt
is softer, and the LLM leaks parametric knowledge (e.g., predicts
the first Nobel physics laureate without corpus grounding), so the
EM lift is prompt-strictness against a corpus mismatch,
not a RAG-quality difference. Refusal rate ( $64.7\%$ 
vs. $0\%$ ) is the only reliable signal here and simply reflects
the prompt-template difference. A true head-to-head on a
matched benchmark (e.g., wrapping BEIR NFCorpus behind each
framework, or ingesting KILT-100w into LASAGNA) is outside the scope
of this study. Accordingly, this comparison does not report matched
NDCG@10, BGE-M3, or cross-framework RAGAS results and should be read
only as positional smoke evidence.

Why this is not the head-to-head the reader might want.

BERGEN and FlashRAG expose composition but not stage isolation
(Tables 1 and H.1); a like-for-like factorial
inside either framework would require adding the same
session-config-isolated boundary that LASAGNA verifies in
Table 63 and would not be a fair test of the shipped
framework as released. We therefore treat the framework
comparison as positional rather than confirmatory, and rely
on the LASAGNA-internal factorial (Table 43) and the
executable external baselines (Table 60) for
quantitative claims.

## Appendix H Platform Engineering and Reproducibility

## H.1 Platform Isolation Verification

To empirically validate lasagna’s stage-isolation guarantees
(Section 3), we ran five automated tests targeting the
isolation properties that are critical for valid factorial
comparisons. Table 63 reports the results.

Table 63: Platform isolation verification results. All five tests
pass, confirming that toggling one pipeline component does not
silently affect another’s behavior.

Test
What it verifies
Result

Pydantic type enforcement
Stage boundaries reject invalid types
Pass

Session config isolation
Changing session A’s config leaves session B unchanged
Pass

Embedding dim detection
384-dim vectors into 1024-dim collection raises error
Pass

Chunker statelessness
No cross-document state leakage between calls
Pass

Plugin auto-discovery
New backends register via decorator without code changes
Pass

The most critical test is session config isolation: we created
two sessions with opposite HyDE settings (enable_hyde=True
vs. False), then verified that querying the session provider
returns the correct config for each session independently. In a naive
pipeline without session-level isolation, toggling HyDE for one
experimental configuration could leak into the next, introducing a
confound that invalidates the factorial comparison.

The embedding dimension detection test was additionally verified
during the scaling experiments, where the Speed profile (MiniLM-L6,
384-dim) correctly raised EMBEDDING DIMENSION MISMATCH
DETECTED when attempting to share a collection with BGE-M3
(1024-dim) profiles, forcing separate ingestion, a confound that
would otherwise silently corrupt retrieval results.

## H.2 Platform Showcase

This appendix demonstrates lasagna’s practical usability
through code examples drawn directly from the released codebase.
We highlight five capabilities that distinguish the platform from
existing RAG toolkits: (i) a configuration UI for interactive
exploration, (ii) a layered plugin architecture with decorator-based
registration, (iii) bring-your-own vector database support,
(iv) bring-your-own agentic pipeline, and (v) agent integration via
MCP. All examples are executable as shipped.

## H.2.1 Configuration UI

lasagna ships three browser-based interfaces. The
centerpiece is the RAG Configurator
(Figs. 12 and 13), a
profile-centric pipeline builder that lets a practitioner assemble a
working configuration without writing code or YAML. The left-hand
rail lists the four built-in profiles (default,
speed, standard, balanced,
max-reliable) alongside user-saved custom profiles, and the
main panel groups every configuration key exposed by the SDK into
four tabs, Essential, Search & Retrieval, Processing, and
Advanced, each labeled with plain-language explanations of what the
setting does, its cost implications, and the underlying YAML key
(e.g. enable_hybrid_search, enable_reranking,
use_agentic_rag). Every change is import/export-round-trippable
with the YAML contract used by the evaluation SDK
(Section H.2.5), so a user can prototype a profile in
the UI, export it, and run the full factorial sweep on it with no
translation. The Configurator is what gives researchers and
practitioners who are not fluent in the 127-key configuration surface
a direct path to reproducing the operational profiles in
Section 5 and to iterating on them.

The other two interfaces are complementary but are not part of the public release. The Agentic Chat workspace provides
a conversational front-end with inline document management and
citation rendering; it consumes a Configurator-produced profile and
lets changes take effect on the next query without restarting the
server. The RAG Dashboard is
evaluation-oriented, with six pages (Overview, Explorer, Impact,
Pareto, Benchmark, Timeline) that visualize per-query evaluation scores. Together, the three interfaces let
a researcher configure a pipeline, run it conversationally, and
inspect its evaluation results without writing code.

Figure 12: RAG Configurator, Essential tab. The Essential
tab surfaces the highest-impact pipeline toggles
(enable_hybrid_search, enable_reranking,
enable_mmr, enable_hyde,
use_agentic_rag, enable_hierarchical,
docling_enabled, enable_ocr) alongside the
embedding-model selector, a GPU toggle, and three quick-tuning
sliders (default_top_k, similarity_threshold,
hybrid_alpha). Profiles are listed on the left, with the
recommended standard profile flagged; user-created profiles
appear below and are round-trippable with the YAML contract used by
the evaluation SDK.

Figure 13: RAG Configurator, Search & Retrieval tab. The
Search & Retrieval tab exposes the retrieval and post-retrieval
stack: MMR score normalization (sigmoid vs. min–max), smart
result ordering, the RRF fusion constant (rrf_k), the
keyword-search method (sparse_method: BM25, SPLADE),
context compression with method and ratio controls
(compression_method, compression_ratio), and
reranker-model selection. Every field carries the underlying
configuration key so a UI change maps one-to-one to the YAML config
consumed by the factorial experiment runner.

## H.2.2 Layered Plugin Architecture

The three-step research workflow (Fig. 2, restored to
the body) is the public API surface of the framework: register with a
decorator, declare a factorial sweep in YAML, and run end-to-end for a
paired statistical test. The remainder of this appendix
documents the plugin registry that supports it.

Every pipeline component is a subclass of a typed abstract base
class (ABC). Eight component types are supported:
embedder, chunker, reranker,
retriever, document_processor,
query_enhancer, agentic_pipeline, and generator.
Registering a new component requires a single decorator:

Registering a custom reranker.

⬇

from lasagna.core.registry import registry

from lasagna.core.base_components import (

 BaseReranker, ScoredDocument,

)

@registry.register("reranker", "my_colbert_reranker")

class ColBERTReranker(BaseReranker):

 def rerank(self, query, documents, top_k=5, **kwargs):

 # Late-interaction scoring with ColBERT

 scored = self._score_colbert(query, documents)

 return sorted(scored, key=lambda d: d.score,

 reverse=True)[:top_k]

Once registered, the component is immediately available in any
pipeline configuration:

Using a registered component in a pipeline.

⬇

from lasagna.core.pipeline import KnowledgePipeline

pipeline = KnowledgePipeline(reranker="my_colbert_reranker")

results = pipeline.query("What␣is␣CRAG?", top_k=5)

The same pattern applies to all eight component types. Each ABC
defines the minimal interface that the pipeline expects
(Table 64).

Table 64: Component ABCs and their core methods. Researchers implement
one method to integrate a new component.

Component
Core method

Signature

Embedder
encode()

(texts: List[str]) -> ndarray

Chunker
chunk()

(text: str) -> List[Chunk]

Reranker
rerank()

(query, docs, top_k) -> List[ScoredDoc]

Retriever
retrieve()

(query, collection, top_k) -> List[ScoredDoc]

DocProcessor
extract()

(file_path) -> ProcessedDocument

QueryEnhancer
enhance()

(query) -> List[str]

AgenticPipeline
run()

(input) -> AgenticRAGResult

Generator
generate()

(query, contexts) -> str

## H.2.3 Bring Your Own Database

lasagna ships with six built-in vector backends
(Milvus,
Pinecone,
Qdrant,
Weaviate,
PGVector,
OpenSearch) and supports custom
backends via the VectorBackendBase ABC. An
implementation fills in the abstract connection, collection, index,
insert, delete, query, and search methods; the excerpt shows the core:

Bring-your-own vector database (excerpt).

⬇

from lasagna.sdk import (

 VectorBackendBase, BackendCapabilities,

 register_backend,

)

class MyCustomBackend(VectorBackendBase):

 @property

 def capabilities(self) -> BackendCapabilities:

 return BackendCapabilities(

 supports_hybrid_search=False,

 supports_hnsw=True,

 max_batch_size=1000,

 )

 @property

 def backend_name(self) -> str:

 return "my_backend"

 def connect(self) -> None: ...

 def create_collection(self, collection_name, schema,

 index_config=None): ...

 def insert(self, collection_name, data,

 partition_key=None): ...

 def search(self, collection_name, query_embedding,

 limit=10, **kwargs): ...

 # ... remaining collection, index, and query methods

register_backend("my_backend", MyCustomBackend)

After registration, setting vector_backend: my_backend in
the configuration routes all vector
operations through the custom implementation. The capability
declaration (BackendCapabilities) allows the pipeline to
automatically fall back to software-based BM25 when the backend does
not support native sparse search.

## H.2.4 Bring Your Own Agent

The agentic pipeline is itself a registrable component.
lasagna’s default agentic graph (built with LangGraph)
implements a multi-iteration retrieve-refine-generate loop with
hallucination checking and quality gates. Researchers can replace
or extend it by registering a custom AgenticPipeline:

Registering a custom agentic pipeline.

⬇

from lasagna.core.registry import registry

from lasagna.core.base_components import (

 BaseAgenticPipeline, AgenticPipelineInput,

)

from lasagna.agentic.agentic_rag import AgenticRAGResult

@registry.register("agentic_pipeline", "my_self_rag")

class MySelfRAG(BaseAgenticPipeline):

 async def run(self, input: AgenticPipelineInput):

 # Custom Self-RAG logic: generate, reflect, retrieve

 chunks = await self.retrieve(input.question, input.top_k)

 draft = await self.generate(input.question, chunks)

 if not self.is_faithful(draft, chunks):

 chunks += await self.retrieve_more(input.question)

 draft = await self.generate(input.question, chunks)

 return AgenticRAGResult(

 formatted_context=self.format(chunks), chunks=chunks,

 citations=[], agent_summary=draft, search_count=2,

 )

Individual graph nodes can also be overridden without replacing the
full graph, enabling targeted modifications (e.g., a custom
hallucination checker) while retaining the default orchestration:

Overriding a single graph node.

⬇

from lasagna.agentic import build_rag_graph

graph = build_rag_graph(

 session_config,

 node_overrides={

 "check_hallucination": my_custom_hallucination_fn

 }

)

## H.2.5 Research Workflow

Running a complete experiment requires two artifacts: a YAML
configuration file and a one-line command.

Experiment YAML (abbreviated).

⬇

name: "HyDE Ablation"

benchmarks:

 - name: private_eval/retrieval

 split: eval

pipeline:

 base_profile: "standard"

 config_overrides:

 - name: "hyde_on"

 enable_hyde: true

 hyde_n_docs: 3

 - name: "hyde_off"

 enable_hyde: false

mode: live

n_runs: 3

seed: 42

judge_model: "gpt-5.1"

Running an experiment from the CLI.

⬇

$ lasagna benchmark experiment hyde_ablation.yaml

[INFO] Loading benchmark: private_eval/retrieval (eval)

[INFO] Ingesting corpus (5132 docs, signature: a3f2e1...)

[INFO] Running hyde_on (run 1/3)...

[INFO] Running hyde_off (run 1/3)...

...

[INFO] Results saved to ~/.lasagna/research/results/

[INFO] NDCG@10: hyde_on=0.487 vs hyde_off=0.461 (p=0.031)

The experiment runner handles corpus ingestion (with signature-based
caching), per-query timing, RAGAS evaluation, checkpointing, cost
tracking, and statistical testing automatically. Results are saved as
JSON with per-sample scores, enabling downstream analysis without
re-running experiments.

## H.2.6 Agent Integration via MCP

lasagna’s knowledge base is exposed as a Model Context
Protocol (MCP) server, allowing any MCP-compatible agent to use it
as a tool without custom integration code. The server exposes 31
tools covering search, document management, configuration, and
lifecycle operations:

MCP server startup and tool list (excerpt).

⬇

$ python -m lasagna.mcp --port 8005

Tools exposed:

 search_knowledge - Semantic search across documents

 search_with_context - Search with formatted context

 get_document_context - Document awareness for prompts

 upload_document - Upload and index a document

 list_documents - List session documents

 delete_document - Delete document and chunks

 get_rag_config - Get current configuration

 patch_rag_config - Update configuration at runtime

 list_profiles - List operational profiles

 set_active_profile - Switch profiles (speed/balanced/...)

 get_system_status - Health check and statistics

 ...

Any agent framework that supports MCP (Claude Desktop, LangChain MCP
adapter, custom agents) can connect by pointing to the server URL.
The SDK also provides a typed Python client for direct integration:

Using LASAGNA from any agent via the SDK.

⬇

from lasagna.sdk import KnowledgeClient

client = KnowledgeClient()

# Upload documents to the knowledge base

await client.upload("report.pdf")

# Retrieve the most relevant chunks

results = await client.search_documents(

 "What␣were␣Q3␣revenue␣figures?"

)

# Get formatted context to pass to any agent or LLM

context = await client.get_context(

 "Summarize␣the␣Q3␣financial␣highlights"

)

The MCP architecture decouples the knowledge base from the agent
framework: the same indexed corpus serves Claude Sonnet 4.5, GPT-5.1, open-source
models, or custom agents without re-ingestion.

## Appendix I Operational Profiles

Figure 14: Pareto positions of the four SOTA profiles in
NDCG@10–faithfulness space (Tables 2 and 5).
Marker area is proportional to inverse mean latency
(larger = faster). sota_standard attains the
highest joint retrieval–faithfulness–latency hypervolume
at the fixed $30$  s response-time reference
(Table 2; HV $=0.523$ ) via the highest
NDCG@10 ( $0.860$ ) and the lowest latency among the
quality-focused three ( $5.3$  s).
sota_balanced ( $\bigstar$ ) attains the top-band
faithfulness ( $0.911$ , within noise of
sota_max_reliable’s $0.916$ ) while providing higher
NDCG@10 and lower response time; it wins the joint HV once
 $L_{\mathrm{ref}}$ opens past ${\sim}46$  s
(Table 39).

## I.1 Operational Profile Specifications

This appendix provides the complete configuration for each of the four
SOTA operational profiles evaluated in Section 5. Each profile
is specified as a YAML configuration overlay; keys not listed inherit
from the framework defaults described in
Section 4.

## I.1.1 sota_speed

⬇

profile: speed

embedding_model: all-MiniLM-L6-v2

embedding_dim: 384

retrieval_mode: dense

hybrid_alpha: 1.0 # dense-only

enable_reranking: false

enable_hyde: false

enable_mmr: false

enable_crag: false

enable_rag_fusion: false

enable_query_expansion: false

use_agentic_rag: false

enable_query_routing: false

routing_default_tier: fast

enable_hallucination_check: false

enable_answer_quality_check: false

enable_context_compression: false

enable_document_awareness: false

top_k: 10

Target use case.

High-throughput, latency-sensitive deployments such as autocomplete
suggestions, real-time search-as-you-type interfaces, or batch
processing pipelines where sub-second response times are required.
With MiniLM-L6 embeddings and no LLM-in-the-loop components, the
retrieval path completes in ${\sim}$ 200 ms; end-to-end latency
(including generation) is ${\sim}$ 800 ms at p95.

Deployment guidance.

This profile requires only a vector database and a generation endpoint;
no reranker model, no HyDE LLM calls, and no agentic orchestration.
It can serve as the warm-start default while heavier profiles are
evaluated. We recommend against using this profile when retrieval
quality (NDCG@10 $>$ 0.7) or faithfulness ( $>$ 0.6) are firm
requirements, as both metrics are substantially lower than the other
SOTA profiles (Table 2).

## I.1.2 sota_standard

⬇

profile: standard

embedding_model: BAAI/bge-m3

embedding_dim: 1024

retrieval_mode: hybrid

hybrid_alpha: 0.5

enable_reranking: true

rerank_model: mixedbread-ai/mxbai-rerank-large-v1

rerank_top_k: 5

enable_hyde: true

hyde_n_variants: 3

enable_mmr: false # disabling improves NDCG

enable_crag: false

enable_rag_fusion: false

enable_query_expansion: false

use_agentic_rag: true # inherited from standard base

enable_query_routing: false # override; keeps queries on direct route

routing_default_tier: standard

enable_hallucination_check: true

enable_answer_quality_check: true

enable_context_compression: false

enable_document_awareness: false

top_k: 20

# Effective generation route: direct (routing disabled, standard tier default)

Target use case.

General-purpose retrieval where precision is the primary concern:
enterprise knowledge bases, technical documentation search, and
customer support systems with moderate response times (4–7 s).
HyDE generates three hypothetical documents per query, which are
searched in parallel before cross-encoder reranking selects the top 10
passages.

Deployment guidance.

Requires a vector database with hybrid (dense + BM25) search support, a
reranker model endpoint (or local GPU for mxbai-rerank-large), and one
LLM call for HyDE generation. The three HyDE variants add
 ${\sim}$ 2 s of latency; operators who need to stay below 3 s can
reduce hyde_n_variants to 1 with a modest NDCG@10 reduction
( ${\sim}$ 0.01–0.02 based on our ablation in Section 5).
This profile achieves the highest NDCG@10 (0.860) on the SOTA-profile
generation slice, follows the direct generation route (query routing
disabled), and enables both the hallucination and answer-quality gates
at the pipeline level. Pair it with application-level guardrails if a
per-query faithfulness floor above $0.8$ is required.

## I.1.3 sota_balanced_e2e

⬇

profile: balanced

embedding_model: BAAI/bge-m3

embedding_dim: 1024

retrieval_mode: hybrid

hybrid_alpha: 0.7 # inherited from balanced base

enable_reranking: true

rerank_model: mixedbread-ai/mxbai-rerank-large-v1

rerank_top_k: 5

enable_hyde: true

hyde_n_variants: 3

enable_mmr: false # disabling improves NDCG

enable_crag: false

enable_rag_fusion: false

enable_query_expansion: false

enable_hierarchical: true # inherited from balanced base

use_agentic_rag: true # inherited from balanced base

enable_query_routing: true # adaptive routing (inherited)

max_agent_iterations: 3

enable_hallucination_check: true

enable_answer_quality_check: true

enable_context_compression: true

compression_method: extractive

compression_ratio: 0.5

enable_document_awareness: true

doc_awareness_method: first_tokens

doc_awareness_tokens: 100

top_k: 20

max_chunks: 20

# Effective generation route: agentic (3 iterations, both quality gates)

Target use case.

Recommended default for deployments that can tolerate ${\sim}$ 12–18 s
per-query latency and require the top-band faithfulness
(batch/analyst workloads, review queues, back-office QA).
Extractive context compression reduces the token count passed to
the generator by $50\%$ , lowering generation cost while
preserving the most relevant spans. Document-awareness prefixes
(first $100$ tokens) provide the generator with document-level
context at negligible additional cost. On the SOTA-profile
generation slice reported in Table 2,
balanced provides the stronger joint agentic tradeoff than
max-reliable (higher NDCG@10, essentially tied
faithfulness, $13\%$ lower mean latency) and takes the joint
retrieval–faithfulness–latency hypervolume from
standard once $L_{\mathrm{ref}}$ opens past
 ${\sim}46$  s ( $\mathrm{HV}{=}0.609$ vs $0.579$ at $60$  s;
Table 39). At the fixed $30$  s reference,
standard has the highest joint HV; balanced is
the appropriate choice when the application can absorb the
additional ${\sim}7$  s of response time for
the faithfulness advantage.

Deployment guidance.

Infrastructure adds the agentic graph execution
(LangGraph) to the sota_standard stack:
 $3$ – $7$ additional LLM calls per query for relevance classification,
hallucination checking, and quality gating, plus an extractive
compression step that runs locally (no additional LLM call).
Disabling MMR removes one source of latency variability without
harming retrieval quality in our experiments. End-to-end mean
latency on the SOTA slice is ${\sim}$ 12.3 s (p95 ${\sim}$ 26.3 s),
dominated by the agentic loop and HyDE generation. To reduce
latency, operators can disable HyDE (saving ${\sim}$ 2–3 s) at the
cost of ${\sim}$ 0.02 NDCG@10.

## I.1.4 sota_max_reliable

⬇

profile: max_quality

embedding_model: BAAI/bge-m3

embedding_dim: 1024

retrieval_mode: hybrid

hybrid_alpha: 0.3 # override; broader lexical retrieval

enable_reranking: true

rerank_model: mixedbread-ai/mxbai-rerank-large-v1

rerank_top_k: 5

enable_hyde: true # inherited from max_quality base

hyde_n_variants: 3

enable_mmr: false # disabling improves NDCG

enable_crag: false # override; adds latency, no gain here

enable_rag_fusion: false # override

enable_query_expansion: false # override

enable_hierarchical: true # inherited

enable_contextual_retrieval: true # inherited from max_quality base

use_agentic_rag: true

enable_query_routing: false # inherited from max_quality (comprehensive tier)

routing_default_tier: comprehensive

max_agent_iterations: 3 # override (base = 5)

enable_hallucination_check: true

enable_answer_quality_check: true

enable_context_compression: true

compression_method: extractive

compression_ratio: 0.5

enable_document_awareness: true

doc_awareness_method: first_tokens

doc_awareness_tokens: 100

top_k: 20

max_chunks: 30

# Effective generation route: agentic (3 iterations, both quality gates)

# Differs from Balanced primarily on retrieval-side choices:

# hybrid_alpha (0.3 vs 0.7), contextual_retrieval (on vs off),

# max_chunks (30 vs 20). Same agentic depth and same gate set.

Target use case.

High-stakes domains where answer faithfulness must be maximized and
users tolerate longer response times: medical literature review, legal
contract analysis, financial due diligence, and compliance
question-answering. The agentic pipeline performs up to three retrieval
iterations with hallucination gating; each iteration can refine or
discard unfaithful passages before the final answer is generated.

Deployment guidance.

Infrastructure matches sota_balanced_e2e (agentic graph
execution, hybrid retrieval, reranker, HyDE) with additional
contextual retrieval and a broader chunk-cite budget (up to
 $30$ retained chunks vs. $20$ ). Both profiles share the same
agentic depth (three iterations, hallucination and answer-quality
gates enabled); the differences from sota_balanced_e2e are
retrieval-side (hybrid $\alpha=0.3$ vs. $0.7$ , contextual
retrieval enabled, wider chunk budget). Mean latency on the
SOTA-profile generation slice is ${\sim}$ 14.2 s (p95
 ${\sim}$ 30.0 s), driven by the wider chunk pool and the agentic
loop; we recommend a concurrency limit of ${\sim}$ 10 queries per
instance. Notably, this profile has a weaker joint tradeoff than
sota_balanced_e2e within the agentic-route class on our
headline slice (Table 2): faithfulness is essentially tied
( $0.916$ vs $0.911$ ), NDCG@10 is $0.036$ lower,
and mean latency is $\sim{}2$  s higher (paired Wilcoxon
 $p{=}0.018$ ). We include it for completeness
and for deployments that require the broader retrieval envelope
(contextual retrieval, wider chunk budget) it provides.

## I.2 Profile Selection Guide

This appendix provides a practitioner-oriented decision framework for
selecting among the four SOTA profiles based on deployment constraints.

Figure 15: Profile selection flowchart based on response-time target, faithfulness
requirement, and corpus size. Metric values are from the 5K
retrieval evaluation (Table 2) except where corpus-size
dependent (balanced at $>$ 25K uses scaling results from
Section 5). All profiles use the same generator
(GPT-5.1); differences arise from retrieval and post-retrieval
components.

Usage.

The flowchart in Fig. 15 encodes three decision
criteria derived from our experimental findings:

1. 

Response-time target determines the first branch.
speed ( $<$ 1 s) uses MiniLM-L6 embeddings with
dense-only retrieval and no query-time LLM calls; it is the
only viable choice under a hard sub-second target and
trades away the joint quality-latency tradeoff.

2. 

Execution route + joint envelope separates
the mid-to-high latency profiles. On the SOTA-profile
generation slice (Table 2), standard
attains the highest joint retrieval–faithfulness–latency
hypervolume at the fixed $30$  s response-time reference
( $\mathrm{HV}{=}0.523$ ;
NDCG@10 $0.860$ , faithfulness $0.739$ , mean latency
 $5.3$  s) and is the recommended default when interactive
latency is required. balanced runs the agentic
route (three iterations, hallucination and answer-quality
gates enabled) at NDCG@10 $0.840$ , faithfulness $0.911$ ,
mean latency $12.3$  s; it provides a stronger joint tradeoff
than max-reliable within the agentic-route class and
takes the joint HV from standard once
 $L_{\mathrm{ref}}$ opens past ${\sim}46$  s
(Table 39); it is the recommended
default when longer response times are acceptable and the
faithfulness gain is important. max-reliable shares the
same agentic route as balanced with a broader
retrieval envelope (contextual retrieval, wider chunk
budget); it has a lower joint score than balanced.

3. 

Within the agentic-route class, prefer
balanced. balanced has $4.5\%$ higher
NDCG@10 and $13\%$ lower mean response time than
max-reliable, with a $0.005$ faithfulness difference
(Tables 2 and D.8);
max-reliable’s broader retrieval envelope
(contextual retrieval, wider chunk budget) is worth its
additional cost only when a deployment specifically requires
that envelope. Corpus size does not shift this ordering on the
range we tested.

Limitations of this guide.

This flowchart is derived from experiments on five BEIR domains and
may not generalize to all deployment scenarios. In particular:
(i) the MMR finding (“disable MMR”) may not hold for corpora with
genuinely redundant documents; (ii) the corpus-size threshold of 25K
is approximate and domain-dependent; (iii) absolute latency values
depend on infrastructure and will differ from our
measurements.

Decision rule for borderline cases (OFAT vs. hierarchical disagreement).

Several configuration comparisons in this paper differ in rank
order depending on whether the reader uses the OFAT (pooled)
reading or the hierarchical mixed-effects (query-clustered)
reading. When the two disagree by ${\geq}1$ rank position, defer
to the hierarchical reading. It respects the query-clustering
structure ( $\text{ICC}{=}0.284$ on the H1 D5 $\times$ D8 grid; see
Section D.3) and is our primary inferential
position throughout the paper. The archetypal case is the H1
D5 $\times$ D8 attenuation: the OFAT estimate is $\eta^{2}{=}0.201$ 
( $p{=}0.018$ Holm) whereas the hierarchical LRT is $\chi^{2}(4){=}4.27$ ,
 $p{=}0.371$ . The same pair reads as a large exploratory
interaction under OFAT and as non-significant under hierarchical
inference. The only exception is when a deployment’s per-query
variance is orthogonal to query-topic clustering (rare in practice
for benchmark corpora but possible in enterprise deployments with
strictly independent queries); in that regime the pooled OFAT
reading is closer to the deployment target. A separate, orthogonal
protocol-dependence axis (point-wise RAGAS-judge vs. pairwise
Bradley–Terry) affects within-tier configuration comparisons; see
Section C.5 for the tier-vs-configuration scoping.

## I.3 Hallucination Resistance

These results are reported retrospectively from historical deployment logs; they are not part of the pre-registered plan and serve descriptive purposes only.

During development and evaluation of the lasagna pipeline, we
collected faithfulness scores (RAGAS, LLM-judged by GPT-5.1) across
six pipeline profiles. Table 65 summarizes the observed
faithfulness distributions. We present these data descriptively and make no
causal claims about which pipeline components drive the observed differences.

Table 65: Faithfulness statistics across profiles, aggregated from historical
deployment logs (test split, 76 queries per profile).
These runs used the pipeline’s deployment-native answer
configuration (Section C.2); regime provenance is
documented in Table 26 and values are not directly
comparable to faithfulness reported in other tables. No
statistical tests are applied; values are descriptive summaries only.

Descriptive observations.

All six profiles achieved mean faithfulness above 0.96, indicating that the
generation pipeline (GPT-5.1 with retrieved context) produces
grounded answers across configurations. The speed profile and SOTA max
reliable profile exhibited the highest median faithfulness (1.000),
suggesting that both concise answer prompting (speed) and quality-gate
filtering (SOTA max reliable) are associated with reduced hallucination,
though through different mechanisms.

The lowest observed per-query faithfulness score (0.667) occurred in the max
quality profile, which uses agentic retrieval with aggressive refinement.
As discussed in Section 5, aggressive chunk filtering can leave
the generation model with minimal context, increasing the risk of
unsupported claims on complex queries.

We emphasize that these observations reflect correlational patterns in
historical logs. Controlled experiments isolating individual components’
contributions to hallucination resistance would require a dedicated
factorial design that was outside the scope of this study.

## Appendix J Root Cause Analysis of Profile Performance

This appendix provides a mechanistic root cause analysis (RCA) for the
performance of each SOTA profile, grounding observed successes and failures
in architectural decisions, measured interaction effects, and ablation
evidence. Table 66 provides a quick-reference guide;
the remainder of the appendix presents the supporting analysis,
following the taxonomy in Section J.1 with evidence from
the 167-configuration factorial campaign, the feature dropout analysis
(Section D.12), and the single-feature ablations
(Section D.1).
