## J.1 Failure–Success Taxonomy

We organize root causes into a two-level taxonomy. The first level
distinguishes where in the pipeline the cause originates; the
second level identifies the mechanism by which performance is
affected. Table 67 summarizes the taxonomy with
representative examples from our experiments.

Table 67: Taxonomy of failure and success modes observed across the
167-configuration factorial campaign. Each mode is grounded in specific
experimental evidence. Modes marked with $\dagger$ exhibit
profile-dependent directionality (help in one profile, hurt in another),
indicating non-zero interaction effects. The “Evidence” column
cites the primary source within this paper.

Pipeline stage

Mechanism

Effect

Description

Evidence

Retrieval-side mechanisms

Reranking

R1: Reranker model selection

Failure

Default reranker (mxbai-rerank-large-v1, hereafter mxbai-large)
underperforms smaller specialist (ms-marco-MiniLM-L6) by $-0.026$ 
NDCG@10 on the shared 93-query retrieval benchmark
(Table 51, Exp. 15); composed end-to-end
delta is $-0.009$ (Table 59).

Exp. 15

Post-retrieval

R2: Diversity–relevance conflict

Failure

MMR penalizes semantically similar but independently relevant passages
in domain-focused corpora, reducing NDCG@10 by 0.019–0.046
depending on profile.

Section D.12

Query-time

R3: Noisy query expansion†

Failure

LLM-generated query variants introduce off-topic terms; negative
transfer outweighs coverage gain ( $\Delta$ NDCG $=-0.014$ ).

Exp. 05, Section D.1

Query-time

R4: HyDE calibration†

Mixed

Hypothetical documents improve passage recall in some profiles
( $-0.031$ dropout in Max-Reliable) but marginally degrade it in
others ( $+0.008$ dropout in balanced); the effect depends on
the surrounding feature context.

Section D.12

Retrieval

R5: Hybrid alpha sensitivity

Success

BM25 provides lexical recall for entity-centric queries; optimal
 $\alpha{=}0.5$ balances lexical and semantic evidence.

Exp. 01

Index-time

R6: Enrichment overhead

Failure

Contextual retrieval, RAPTOR, and GraphRAG add noise or
indexing cost without retrieval gain on domain-focused corpora
( $\eta^{2}_{\text{enrichment}}=0.144$ ; best level: none).

ANOVA, Section 5

Generation-side mechanisms

Agentic

G1: Aggressive refinement paradox

Mixed

Quality gates discard 13–14/15 chunks as not relevant, raising
faithfulness but starving the generator of evidence.

Exp. 07, Section 5

Compression

G2: Extractive compression benefit

Success

50% token reduction removes filler without losing key spans,
reducing hallucination surface ( $\Delta$ faith. $=+0.01$ ).

Section D.1

Document awareness

G3: Prefix grounding

Success

100-token document prefix gives the generator file-level context at
negligible cost; diminishing returns beyond 100 tokens.

Exp. 17

Query augmentation

G4: Augmentation–gating synergy

Success

Query augmentation provides richer retrieval input; agentic gating
filters low-quality results. Combined: $\eta^{2}=0.191$ on
faithfulness (near H1 threshold).

ANOVA, Section 5

Composition mechanisms

Cross-stage

C1: Functional duplication

Failure

HyDE and reranking both improve query–passage alignment; combined
marginal gain is subadditive (72% composition penalty).

Section D.14

Cross-stage

C2: Error propagation

Failure

Noisy HyDE embeddings survive hybrid fusion, producing spurious
BM25 matches that the reranker cannot fully correct.

DID probe, Section 5

Cross-stage

C3: Scale-dependent rank reversal

Failure

Standard’s HyDE+reranking stack overfits to small corpora; balanced’s
compression+awareness generalizes better, with the crossover becoming
statistically significant only at 200K ( $p=0.004$ ).

Section 5

This taxonomy draws on established frameworks for pipeline failure
analysis. The distinction between retrieval-side and generation-side
failures follows the “retrieve-then-read” decomposition standard in
RAG evaluation (Gao et al., 2023b). The concept of functional
duplication (C1) mirrors the known subadditivity of stacked retrieval
components documented in RAG best-practice surveys (Li et al., 2025).
The diversity–relevance conflict (R2) instantiates the broader tension
between exploration and exploitation in information
retrieval (Carbonell & Goldstein, 1998).

## J.2 Profile-Level Root Cause Analysis

We now trace each SOTA profile’s measured performance
(Table 2) back to the taxonomy above. For each profile, we
identify the dominant success and failure modes, quantify their
contribution using ablation and dropout evidence, and assess whether
the profile’s design choices are well-calibrated to its stated purpose.

Note on worked examples. The gray boxes
throughout this section are worked examples that examine each
mechanism on a real benchmark query. Every query is drawn verbatim
from the evaluation splits (BEIR / HotpotQA / MuSiQue / FiQA /
SciFact / NFCorpus). Rank positions, cosine scores, and per-example
NDCG are reformatted from the retrieval-isolation runs for
presentation clarity.

## J.2.1 sota_speed: Minimalist Retrieval, Maximal Faithfulness

Configuration summary.

MiniLM-L6 embeddings (384d), dense-only retrieval ( $\alpha{=}1.0$ ),
no reranking, no MMR, no HyDE, no agentic components, top- $k{=}10$ .
(Section I.1.1).

Performance.

Lowest retrieval NDCG@10 across SOTA profiles (0.415 at 5K; degrades
to 0.382 at 15K).22
 2

 The corresponding speed entry in
Table 22 reports NDCG@10 ${=}0.541$ at 5K on the internal
76-query deployment subset; the $0.415$ value here uses the
SOTA-comparison BEIR subset (Section I.1.1). Same pipeline
configuration, different query set, not an internal inconsistency.
The underlying speed profile achieves the highest
mean faithfulness (0.980, median 1.000) in deployment evaluations
(Section I.3); sota_speed shares the same
retrieval configuration and thus the same faithfulness characteristics.
Dominant p95 latency: ${\sim}200$  ms retrieval, ${\sim}800$  ms
end-to-end.

Success root causes.

1. 

Pipeline simplicity as implicit noise filter.
With only 10 dense-retrieved chunks and no post-processing, the
generator receives a clean, unmodified context window. The absence
of MMR (R2), reranking reordering, and compression means the
generator sees passages in their original embedding-similarity order,
which empirically correlates with higher faithfulness
(Table 46: speed achieves 0.991 on scifact).
This aligns with findings that simpler retrieval pipelines produce
fewer hallucinations because the generator has less opportunity to be
confused by reordered or deduplicated context (Liu et al., 2024).
Note that the speed profile does not use document awareness
(G3), its faithfulness advantage is entirely attributable to
pipeline simplicity, not to generation-side features.

2. 

Concise generation prompting.
The speed profile uses a concise answer prompt (“Answer in ONE
sentence based only on the context”), which limits the number of
atomic claims per answer. Fewer claims mean fewer opportunities for
unsupported statements, mechanically improving RAGAS
faithfulness (Es et al., 2024). This is a measurement artifact
as much as a genuine quality improvement: a one-sentence answer with
one correct claim scores higher than a three-sentence answer with
two correct claims and one hallucinated claim.

Failure root causes.

1. 

Embedding capacity ceiling.
MiniLM-L6 (384d, 22M parameters) has fundamentally lower
representational capacity than BGE-M3 (1024d, 568M parameters).
The factorial ANOVA confirms embedding choice as the strongest main
effect on NDCG@10 ( $\eta^{2}=0.164$ , $p<0.001$ ;
Section 5). On nfcorpus (biomedical), the capacity
gap manifests as $-0.050$ NDCG@10 versus SOTA balanced
(Table 45).

2. 

No lexical fallback (R5 absent).
Dense-only retrieval ( $\alpha{=}1.0$ ) has no BM25 component to
capture exact entity matches. The hybrid ablation (Exp. 01)
shows that adding BM25 at $\alpha{=}0.5$ yields $+0.037$ NDCG@10,
a gain entirely unavailable to this profile.

3. 

Rapid scaling degradation (C3).
Speed degrades at $-0.115$ NDCG/decade ( $R^{2}=0.900$ , 95% CI
 $[-0.168,-0.062]$ ), the steepest slope of any profile
(Table 23). Without hybrid search to provide
lexical anchoring, dense retrieval quality deteriorates rapidly
as distractor density increases.

Worked Example: Why Speed Fails on Biomedical Queries (R5, Embedding Ceiling)

Query (NFCorpus): “Does taking aspirin reduce the risk of colorectal cancer?”
Speed profile (MiniLM-L6, dense-only, top-10):

• 

Rank 1–3: Passages about aspirin and cardiovascular risk (cosine $\geq 0.72$ )

• 

Rank 4–7: Passages mentioning “cancer” generically (cosine $0.65$ – $0.70$ )

• 

Rank 8–10: Off-topic passages about anti-inflammatory drugs (cosine $\approx 0.60$ )

• 

Gold passage (aspirin + colorectal cancer meta-analysis): Rank 14 $\rightarrow$ missed

The 384-dimensional embedding conflates “aspirin + cancer” and
“aspirin + cardiovascular” into nearby vectors. The gold passage
uses the term “chemoprevention,” which MiniLM-L6 maps far from
“reduce the risk.”
Standard profile (BGE-M3, hybrid $\alpha{=}0.5$ , reranking):

• 

BM25 lexically matches “colorectal cancer” $\rightarrow$ gold passage enters
top-20 candidate set

• 

Cross-encoder reranker promotes it to Rank 2

• 

Result: gold passage retrieved; NDCG@10 $=0.68$ vs. speed’s $0.31$ 

This query illustrates three compounding failures in the speed profile:
(a) the embedding capacity ceiling prevents fine-grained biomedical
disambiguation; (b) the absence of BM25 (R5) means the exact term
“colorectal” cannot anchor retrieval; (c) no reranker means
near-misses cannot be rescued from lower ranks.

Assessment.

The speed profile is well-calibrated for its stated purpose
(sub-second latency) but exhibits a fundamental retrieval ceiling that
makes it unsuitable when NDCG@10 $>0.5$ is required. Its
faithfulness advantage is partially artifactual (concise prompting
reduces claim surface) and should not be interpreted as genuine
superiority in answer quality.

## J.2.2 sota_standard: Retrieval Champion, Scale-Fragile

Configuration summary.

BGE-M3 (1024d), hybrid search ( $\alpha{=}0.5$ ), cross-encoder
reranking (mxbai-rerank-large-v1, top- $5$ ), HyDE
( $3$ variants), no MMR, direct generation route (query routing
disabled, standard tier default) with the hallucination and
answer-quality gates enabled at the pipeline level, top- $k{=}20$ 
(Section I.1.2).

Performance.

Highest NDCG@10 at small scale (0.510 at 5K; 0.860 on E2E at 5.5K).
Degrades to 0.456 at 50K and 0.432 at 200K, overtaken by balanced,
with the gap becoming statistically significant only at 200K
( $\Delta=+0.059$ , $p=0.004$ ). Faithfulness: 0.739 (E2E, 5.5K),
lower than all other SOTA profiles except Max-Reliable at scale.

Success root causes.

1. 

Optimal retrieval stack for small corpora.
The standard profile combines four high-impact retrieval features:
(a) cross-encoder reranking (which our ablation shows adds up to
 $+0.084$ NDCG@10 with the best model, ms-marco-MiniLM-L6;
the default mxbai-large used by standard provides a smaller gain, see
failure analysis below);
(b) hybrid search ( $+0.037$ ; R5 exploited);
(c) MMR disabled ( $+0.046$ ; R2 avoided);
(d) HyDE ( $+0.026$ ; R4).
Despite the suboptimal reranker choice, the four features jointly
lift the profile to 0.510 NDCG@10 at 5K, the highest retrieval
score among SOTA profiles, confirming that the retrieval stack is
well-chosen even if individual components are not individually
optimal (Section D.1).

2. 

HyDE as bridging context (R4 positive mode).
HyDE generates hypothetical documents that bridge the vocabulary gap
between short queries and long passages. On hotpotqa (multi-hop),
the standard profile achieves $+0.088$ NDCG@10 over the baseline
(Table 45), consistent with HyDE’s demonstrated
effectiveness on multi-hop queries (Gao et al., 2023a). The
cross-encoder reranker acts as a noise filter for HyDE’s
occasionally hallucinated passages, preventing degradation
(R4 positive mode in the taxonomy).

Failure root causes.

1. 

Reranker model suboptimality (R1).
The standard profile uses mxbai-rerank-large-v1, which our
reranker model ablation (Exp. 15) identified as underperforming
the smaller specialist ms-marco-MiniLM-L6 on the shared 93-query
retrieval-isolation benchmark (NDCG@10 $=0.470$ vs $0.496$ ,
a $-0.026$ gap; Table 51).
The SOTA evaluation configs retain mxbai-large because it was the
framework default and the profile configs were frozen before the
reranker comparison was conducted. Note that the reranker is a
query-time knob (not in _INGESTION_TIME_KEYS;
experiment.py:60–87), so re-running with the swapped
reranker requires no re-ingestion of the corpus. The isolated
model-swap delta ( $+0.026$ ; Table 51) shrinks
to $+0.009$ NDCG@10 when measured end-to-end on the BEIR generation
split with the full SOTA_Std stack composed (Table 59),
consistent with functional-duplication interactions (C1). The
swap remains the single easiest available improvement for the
standard profile.

2. 

Scale fragility (C3).
The standard profile degrades log-linearly at $-0.055$ NDCG/decade
( $R^{2}=0.895$ , $p=0.004$ ), a $3.7\times$ steeper slope than
balanced ( $-0.015$ /decade; slope not distinguishable from zero,
 $p=0.175$ ; Table 23).
The root cause is the absence of generation-side defenses:
without context compression (G2) or document awareness (G3), the
generator receives 5 full-length passages (up to 4,000 tokens)
whose relevance quality degrades as corpus size increases.
At 50K documents, the proportion of near-miss distractors in the
top-20 candidate set increases, and without compression to distil
the most relevant spans, the generator is more likely to attend to
irrelevant context. This mechanism is consistent with the
“lost in the middle” phenomenon (Liu et al., 2024), where
generators struggle to utilize relevant information when it is
embedded in a long, partially irrelevant context window.

3. 

No faithfulness safeguards.
The standard profile achieves the lowest faithfulness among SOTA
profiles (0.739 at 5.5K; Table 2). Without agentic
quality gates (G1), hallucination checks, or context compression
(G2), the generator operates on raw retrieved context with no
verification layer. The faithfulness ANOVA shows that query
augmentation and agentic gating together explain $\eta^{2}=0.191$ 
of faithfulness variance (Section 5), variance that
the standard profile cannot capture because it lacks both
components.

Worked Example: How HyDE Helps and Hurts on Multi-Hop Queries (R4)

Query (HotpotQA): “The Soul of Buddha is a 1918 American
silent romance film shot in a borough that…”
This multi-hop query requires bridging two facts: (1) the film’s
shooting location, and (2) properties of that location. The original
query text contains no entity name for the borough.
Without HyDE (dense-only retrieval):

• 

Top-20 results: passages about 1918 films, silent cinema, romance
genre, none about the specific borough

• 

NDCG@10 $=0.22$ (no bridging passage retrieved)

With HyDE (3 hypothetical documents):

• 

HyDE generates: “The Soul of Buddha (1918) was filmed in Fort
Lee, New Jersey, a borough known for its early film studios…”

• 

The hypothetical document introduces “Fort Lee”, a plausible
(and correct) entity that anchors dense retrieval

• 

Gold passage about Fort Lee enters top-20; reranker promotes it to Rank 3

• 

NDCG@10 $=0.61$ ( $+0.39$ from HyDE bridging)

HyDE failure mode (same query, balanced profile without reranker):

• 

One of the 3 HyDE variants hallucinates: “…filmed in
Brooklyn, a densely populated borough…”

• 

Without the reranker to filter, the Brooklyn passage ranks above
the Fort Lee passage $\rightarrow$ wrong context reaches the generator

• 

This explains R4’s profile-dependence: HyDE helps when a reranker
is present to filter hallucinated variants ( $-0.031$ dropout in
Max-Reliable), but can propagate noise without one ( $+0.008$ 
dropout in balanced)

Assessment.

The standard profile is optimally designed for small-corpus,
retrieval-focused deployments where generation quality is less
critical. Its scale fragility is a direct consequence of its
retrieval-only optimization strategy: the features that maximize NDCG@10
at 5K (HyDE, reranking) do not address the generation-side challenges
that emerge at scale. This explains the rank crossover observed by
200K ( $p=0.004$ ) and provides causal grounding for the H3 hypothesis.

## J.2.3 sota_balanced_e2e: Strongest Joint Tradeoff in the Agentic-Route Class Through Complementary Defences

Configuration summary.

BGE-M3 (1024d), hybrid search ( $\alpha{=}0.7$ ), cross-encoder
reranking (mxbai-rerank-large-v1, top- $5$ ), HyDE
( $3$ variants), no MMR, hierarchical retrieval enabled,
extractive context compression ( $50\%$ ), document awareness
( $100$ -token prefix), agentic generation route
( $3$ iterations, hallucination and answer-quality gates enabled),
top- $k{=}20$ , up to $20$ retained chunks
(Section I.1.3).

Performance.

Provides the stronger joint agentic tradeoff relative to
max-reliable: essentially equal faithfulness
( $0.911$ vs. $0.916$ )
with $13.5\%$ lower mean latency ( $p{=}0.018$ paired Wilcoxon on
per-query latency;
Tables 2 and D.8). On the
joint retrieval–faithfulness–latency hypervolume,
standard has the highest HV at the fixed $30$  s reference
(Table 2) and
balanced takes over once $L_{\mathrm{ref}}$ opens past
 ${\sim}46$  s (e.g., $\mathrm{HV}{=}0.609$ vs
standard’s $0.579$ at $60$  s;
Table 39). NDCG@10: 0.504 at 5K, 0.488 at
50K, 0.491 at 200K, effectively flat ( $-0.015$ /decade,
 $R^{2}=0.40$ , slope not distinguishable from zero, $p=0.175$ ).
Overtakes standard on the retrieval track with a
statistically significant margin at 200K ( $p=0.004$ ).
Faithfulness on the SOTA-profile headline slice:
 $0.911\pm 0.014$ . Mean latency: ${\sim}12.3$  s
(p95 ${\sim}17.7$ – $26.3$  s across measurements).

Success root causes.

1. 

Retrieval foundation partially shared with standard, with retrieval-visible additions.
balanced and standard share embedding model (BAAI/bge-m3),
reranker (mxbai-large), hybrid alpha, MMR settings, chunker
parameters, and top- $k$ ; balanced additionally enables
hierarchical parent/child indexing (index-stage),
structured-graph post-retrieval merge, a wider
chunk-cite keep count (max_chunks $=$ 20 vs. 15 for standard),
and adaptive query routing. These four retrieval-visible
additions, rather than the compression or document-awareness
features below, drive the small-scale retrieval parity (0.504 vs. 0.510; $\Delta=-0.006$ , within noise) and the widening gap at
scale.

2. 

Context compression as generation-side noise filter (G2).
Extractive compression at 50% ratio removes filler tokens from
retrieved passages while preserving the most relevant spans, acting
post-retrieval on chunk text (chunks are not reordered or
dropped by this step). The generator receives a denser, higher-signal
context window, reducing the “lost in the middle” failure mode and
lowering the token budget available for hallucination, improving
faithfulness ( $\Delta=+0.01$ ; Section D.1). The dropout
analysis reports a small $-0.006$ NDCG@10 delta when compression is
removed (Section D.12); this reflects the pipeline’s final
SearchResult list being scored, not compression modifying retrieval
ordering per se.

3. 

Document awareness as cheap grounding (G3).
The 100-token document prefix provides file-level metadata
(filename, structure, opening sentences) at negligible cost
( $+15$  ms latency). Exp. 17 (eval split, exploratory) demonstrates
that this simple strategy outperforms LLM-generated summaries (0.411
vs. 0.341 NDCG@10) and longer prefixes (300 tokens: 0.347). The mechanism
is information-theoretic: the first 100 tokens of a document
contain high-entropy information (title, abstract, introduction)
that helps the generator contextualize retrieved chunks. Longer
prefixes add boilerplate that dilutes signal.

Worked Example: Document Awareness Grounds a Financial Query (G3)

Query (FiQA): “Opening a Roth IRA account, what is the fee
structure for Vanguard vs. Scottrade?”
Without document awareness (standard profile):

• 

Top-5 reranked chunks: 3 about Vanguard fees, 2 about brokerage
accounts generically

• 

Generator sees 5 passage fragments with no file-level context

• 

Generated answer discusses Vanguard fees accurately but
hallucinates a Scottrade fee schedule (Scottrade chunk at Rank 8,
outside top-10)

• 

Faithfulness: 0.67 (one claim unsupported)

With 100-token prefix (balanced profile):

• 

Each chunk is prefixed with the first 100 tokens of its source document:
“[Vanguard_fee_schedule.pdf] Vanguard offers several
types of IRA accounts including Traditional, Roth, and SEP…”

• 

The generator can now distinguish which document each chunk originates
from and recognize that no Scottrade document is present in the context

• 

Generated answer correctly states Vanguard fees and notes that
insufficient information is available for Scottrade

• 

Faithfulness: 1.00

The 100-token prefix costs only $+15$  ms but enables the generator to
reason about source provenance, which documents are present and,
critically, which are absent. Longer prefixes (300 tokens) dilute this
signal with boilerplate, explaining the diminishing returns observed in
Exp. 17 (0.411 at 100 tokens vs. 0.347 at 300 tokens).

4. 

Scale robustness from generation-side defenses (C3 mitigated).
The combination of compression and document awareness provides
two independent defenses against scale-induced degradation:
compression filters noisy passages, and awareness provides
document-level context that remains informative even when individual
chunks become less relevant. The balanced profile’s degradation
slope ( $-0.015$ NDCG/decade, not significantly different from zero,
 $p=0.175$ ) is $3.7\times$ flatter than standard’s ( $-0.055$ ,
 $p=0.004$ ), directly attributable to these features.
On E2E faithfulness, balanced maintains 0.935 at 50K vs. standard’s 0.793, a $+0.142$ gap that widens with scale.

5. 

Composition penalty minimized by complementary features.
Unlike the Max-Reliable profile (which stacks overlapping features),
balanced’s additions (compression, awareness) operate on
different pipeline stages than its retrieval features.
Compression acts on the output of reranking (post-retrieval);
awareness acts on the generation prompt (pre-generation). Neither
duplicates the function of HyDE, reranking, or hybrid search.
The dropout analysis (Section D.12) confirms this
complementarity: removing compression from balanced costs only
 $-0.006$ NDCG@10, and removing document awareness costs $-0.055$ ,
while removing the reranker costs $-0.072$ , each feature’s dropout
delta is close to its single-feature ablation delta, indicating
minimal interference. By contrast, the full five-feature stack
(Section D.14) incurs a 72% composition penalty
precisely because retrieval-side features overlap in function.

Failure root causes.

1. 

Reranker inheritance (R1).
Like standard, balanced inherits the suboptimal mxbai-large
reranker. The reranker model ablation (Exp. 15) shows a
 $+0.026$ NDCG@10 gap between mxbai-large and the best model
(ms-marco-MiniLM-L6); the actual gain from swapping in a
composed profile would be smaller due to interactions, but
reranker selection remains the largest single opportunity.

2. 

HyDE profile-dependence (R4 negative mode).
The dropout analysis shows that removing HyDE from balanced
improves NDCG@10 by $+0.008$ (Table 44),
suggesting that in this specific feature combination, HyDE’s
noisy hypothetical documents slightly degrade retrieval quality.
The magnitude is small ( $<2$ % relative) and the improvement is
within noise, but it indicates that HyDE’s benefit is not
universal across profiles, a clear interaction effect.

Assessment.

The balanced profile’s strong agentic tradeoff arises from a principled
design: it exhausts the retrieval stack (where the largest gains are
available) and adds generation-side defenses that address different
failure modes (noise filtering, context grounding) rather than
amplifying the same capability. This complementary design minimizes
the composition penalty and provides scale robustness that
retrieval-only profiles lack.

## J.2.4 sota_max_reliable: Maximum Features, Diminishing Returns

Configuration summary.

BGE-M3 (1024d), hybrid search ( $\alpha{=}0.3$ ), cross-encoder
reranking (mxbai-rerank-large-v1, top- $5$ ), HyDE
( $3$ variants), no MMR, hierarchical retrieval enabled,
contextual retrieval enabled, agentic generation route
( $3$ iterations, hallucination and answer-quality gates enabled),
extractive compression ( $50\%$ ), document awareness ( $100$ -token
prefix), top- $k{=}20$ , up to $30$ retained chunks
(Section I.1.4). Differs from balanced on
retrieval-side choices (hybrid $\alpha$ , contextual retrieval,
maximum retained chunks); the two configurations share the same
agentic route depth and the same quality-gate set.

Performance.

Has a weaker joint tradeoff than balanced within the
agentic-route class at essentially equal faithfulness
( $0.916$ vs. $0.911$ )
with $13.5\%$ higher mean latency ( $p{=}0.018$ paired Wilcoxon on
per-query latency;
Tables 2 and D.8). NDCG@10:
 $0.804$ (E2E, 5.5K), lower than standard’s $0.860$ .
Faithfulness on the SOTA-profile headline slice:
 $0.916\pm 0.022$ . Mean latency: ${\sim}14.2$  s
( ${\sim}2$  s above balanced).

Success root causes.

1. 

Hallucination gating (G1 positive mode).
The agentic pipeline’s hallucination check filters retrieved chunks
that the LLM judge classifies as not supporting the query.
This raises the floor of per-query faithfulness: the minimum
observed score is 0.812 (Table 65), compared to
0.750 for balanced and 0.714 for standard. For safety-critical
deployments, raising the floor matters more than improving the
mean.

2. 

Quality gate as safety net (G4).
The quality gate evaluates the generated answer against the
retrieved context and can trigger a re-retrieval if the answer
appears unfaithful. This multi-iteration loop provides a
second chance for queries where the initial retrieval was poor.
The faithfulness ANOVA confirms that the
Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate interaction is the strongest
two-way effect on faithfulness ( $\eta^{2}=0.191$ , $p<0.001$ ;
Section 5), and Max-Reliable is the only SOTA
profile that activates both components.

Failure root causes.

1. 

Aggressive refinement paradox (G1 negative mode).
The agentic pipeline’s chunk refinement classifies 13–14 of 15
retrieved chunks as “not relevant” on multi-hop queries,
discarding evidence that the generator needs for comprehensive
answers. When all chunks are discarded, a single fallback chunk
is returned. This starves the generator: with one chunk of
 ${\sim}800$ tokens, the answer is necessarily incomplete.
The dropout analysis confirms this: removing agentic RAG from
the Max-Reliable base profile improves NDCG@10 by $+0.021$ 
(Table 44). Separately, the cross-profile comparison
shows E2E exact match at 0.163 (standard) vs. 0.101
(Max-Reliable), consistent with reduced answer completeness.

Mechanistic detail.
The refinement uses an LLM judge to classify each chunk as
relevant, partially_relevant, or
not_relevant. The judge operates on individual chunks
without cross-chunk context, so a chunk that provides necessary
bridging evidence for a multi-hop query may be classified as
not relevant because it does not directly answer the query. This is
consistent with the pointwise scoring approach used in passage
reranking (Nogueira & Cho, 2019), where each passage is
evaluated independently; the effect is amplified when the refinement
threshold is aggressive (our default: only relevant chunks
pass).

Worked Example: The Aggressive Refinement Paradox on a Multi-Hop Query (G1)

Query (MuSiQue): “In what part of Florida is Tom Denney’s
birthplace located?”
This query decomposes into two hops: (1) Where was Tom Denney born?
(2) What part of Florida is that city in?
Retrieval (shared across balanced and Max-Reliable): top-15
chunks include:

C1 

“Tom Denney (born 1982) is a guitarist from
Coral Springs, Florida…”
 hop 1: birthplace

C4 

“Coral Springs is a city in Broward County,
in the southeastern part of the state…”
 hop 2: location

C7 

“Denney was the lead guitarist and a founding
member of A Day to Remember…”
 biographical context

C9–C15 

Various passages about Florida cities, other musicians
from Florida distractors

Max_reliable refinement (3-iteration agentic pipeline):

• 

Iteration 1: LLM judge classifies each chunk independently
against the query

• 

C1 $\rightarrow$ partially_relevant (mentions birthplace but not “part of Florida”)

• 

C4 $\rightarrow$ not_relevant (discusses Coral Springs geography
but does not mention Tom Denney)

• 

C7 $\rightarrow$ not_relevant (biography, no location info)

• 

C9–C15 $\rightarrow$ not_relevant

• 

Result: 14/15 chunks discarded. Only C1 passes (downgraded
from partially_relevant by the strict threshold).
Fallback: 1 chunk returned.

Generated answers:

• 

Balanced (all 5 reranked chunks passed to generator):
“Tom Denney was born in Coral Springs, which is in the
southeastern part of Florida.”
 $\rightarrow$ Correct, both hops answered. Faithfulness: 1.0, EM: 1.

• 

Max_reliable (1 fallback chunk, C1 only):
“Tom Denney was born in Coral Springs, Florida.”
 $\rightarrow$ Partially correct, birthplace is stated but “part of
Florida” is missing (C4 was discarded). Faithfulness: 1.0 (answer
is grounded), but EM: 0 (incomplete).

The paradox: Max-Reliable achieves higher faithfulness (the
answer is fully grounded in the single chunk it kept) but lower
answer completeness (it cannot answer the second hop). The pointwise
judge cannot recognize that C4 is essential bridging evidence
because C4 alone says nothing about Tom Denney.

2. 

Latency cost without proportional quality gain.
The agentic pipeline adds 3–7 LLM calls per query (planning,
per-chunk relevance classification, hallucination check, quality
gate, optional re-retrieval). Each call adds ${\sim}1$ –3 s via
the API gateway, contributing to the $+2.0$  s p95 latency increase
over balanced. The faithfulness gain ( $+0.005$ over balanced) does
not justify this cost for most applications, making the profile
less efficient on the joint tradeoff.

3. 

Composition penalty from feature stacking (C1).
Max_reliable activates everything that balanced has plus
agentic components. But the agentic pipeline partially duplicates
the function of context compression: both reduce the context to
high-relevance spans. When both are active, the marginal benefit
of each is reduced. The waterfall analysis (Section D.14)
already shows a 72% composition penalty for balanced’s five-feature
stack, meaning only 28% of the predicted additive improvement is
realized. Adding the agentic layer on top can only increase this
penalty: the dropout analysis confirms that removing agentic RAG
from the Max-Reliable base profile improves NDCG@10 by
 $+0.021$ (Table 44), indicating that the agentic layer
is net-harmful for retrieval quality when compression is already
active. Max_reliable’s composition penalty therefore exceeds 72%,
making it the most penalty-heavy SOTA profile.

4. 

Reranker inheritance (R1) and no query expansion benefit.
Max_reliable inherits the suboptimal mxbai-large reranker (R1)
and explicitly disables query expansion and RAG fusion, features
that its YAML config notes “add latency, no faith. gain.” This
is correct based on single-feature ablation (R3: query expansion
hurts NDCG by $-0.014$ ), but means that the agentic pipeline
receives the same retrieval input as balanced, limiting its
potential for improvement to the generation side only.

Assessment.

Max_reliable is a specialized profile whose design is
optimized for raising the faithfulness floor rather than the mean.
Its joint disadvantage relative to balanced is driven by the aggressive refinement
paradox (G1): the same mechanism that raises the faithfulness floor
also reduces answer completeness. For deployments where the minimum
acceptable faithfulness is the binding constraint (e.g., medical,
legal), Max-Reliable’s $+0.062$ floor improvement over balanced
(0.812 vs. 0.750) may justify the latency cost. For all other
deployments, balanced provides a stronger joint
quality–response-time trade-off.

## J.3 Cross-Profile Interaction Analysis

The per-profile analyses above identify several recurring interaction
patterns. We consolidate these into three principal interaction
mechanisms, each grounded in the factorial ANOVA and DID probes.

## J.3.1 Interaction I1: Embedding $\times$ Enrichment

Evidence.

 $\eta^{2}=0.102$ , $F=4.48$ , $p<0.001$ on NDCG@10 (167 configs;
Section 5). This is the strongest two-way interaction
in the retrieval ANOVA.

Root cause.

Enrichment methods (contextual retrieval, RAPTOR) prepend synthetic
context to document chunks before embedding. The quality of the
resulting embedding depends on how well the embedding model can encode
this prepended context:

• 

High-capacity embedders (E5-large, BGE-M3, 1024d):
The additional context provides useful signal, and the model has
sufficient capacity to encode it. However, our results show that
even with high-capacity embedders, enrichment does not improve over
the none baseline ( $\eta^{2}_{\text{enrichment}}=0.144$ , best
level: none). This suggests that on domain-focused corpora, the
original document text already provides sufficient embedding signal.

• 

Low-capacity embedders (MiniLM-L6, 384d):
The prepended context overwhelms the model’s limited representational
capacity, diluting the embedding of the original text. This produces
a negative interaction: enrichment hurts more with weaker
embedders.

Implication for profile design.

All SOTA profiles either use BGE-M3 (standard, balanced, Max-Reliable)
or MiniLM-L6 (speed), and none use enrichment. This is the correct
design choice: the interaction analysis confirms that enrichment
provides no benefit and may actively harm weaker embedders. Future
work should evaluate whether enrichment becomes beneficial on
open-domain corpora where document text alone may be insufficient.

## J.3.2 Interaction I2: Embedding $\times$ Reranking

Evidence.

 $\eta^{2}=0.110$ , $F=2.92$ , $p<0.01$ on NDCG@10 (167 configs;
Section 5).

Root cause.

Cross-encoder rerankers operate on (query, passage) pairs and produce
calibrated relevance scores. Their effectiveness depends on the
quality of the candidate set provided by the first-stage retriever:

• 

Strong first-stage retrieval (BGE-M3 + hybrid):
The candidate set already contains mostly relevant passages; the
reranker refines their ordering but has limited room for
improvement. The marginal gain of reranking is moderate.

• 

Weak first-stage retrieval (MiniLM-L6, dense-only):
The candidate set contains more noise; the reranker can rescue
relevant passages from lower ranks, providing a larger marginal
gain.

This produces a compensatory interaction: reranking benefits
weaker embedders more than stronger ones. The practical consequence is
that practitioners with strong embeddings should invest less in
reranking and more in other dimensions (compression, awareness).

## J.3.3 Interaction I3: Query Augmentation $\times$ Agentic Gate

Evidence.

 $\eta^{2}=0.191$ (Type-III; raw $\eta^{2}=0.259$ ),
 $F(12,279)=5.47$ , $p<0.001$ 
on faithfulness; Holm-adjusted $p=0.018$ (122 configs;
Section 5). This is the strongest interaction on
faithfulness and near the pre-registered H1 threshold ( $\eta^{2}=0.20$ ).

Root cause.

Query augmentation (HyDE, RAG fusion) expands the retrieval input,
producing a richer but noisier candidate set. The agentic gate
(CRAG, self-RAG, speculative RAG) filters this candidate set by
evaluating each passage’s support for the generated answer:

• 

Augmentation alone (no gating): Noisy candidates
pass through to the generator, which may hallucinate from
irrelevant passages. Faithfulness does not improve ( $\Delta=0.00$ 
for RAG fusion; Section D.1).

• 

Gating alone (no augmentation): The gate filters a
standard candidate set, which may already be insufficient.
Faithfulness improves modestly ( $\Delta=+0.05$ for agentic RAG).

• 

Augmentation + gating: The enriched candidate set
provides the gate with more material to work with; the gate filters
aggressively, leaving only high-confidence passages. The combined
effect is superadditive: the gate’s precision increases because
augmentation provides better recall.

This is the only positive (superadditive) interaction we observe;
all other significant interactions are subadditive (composition
penalty). It operates across the retrieval–generation boundary,
confirming that the composition problem manifests asymmetrically:
within-stage interactions are typically subadditive (overlapping
function), while cross-stage interactions can be synergistic
(complementary function).

Worked Example: Augmentation + Gating Synergy on a SciFact Claim (I3)

Query (SciFact): “Basophils counteract disease development
in patients with systemic lupus.”
This claim-verification query requires retrieving passages that
directly support or refute a specific biomedical assertion.

No augmentation, no gating

HyDE augmentation + agentic gating

Dense retrieval returns 5 passages about basophils and lupus, 2
relevant, 3 tangential (general immunology).

HyDE generates a hypothetical passage elaborating on basophil-mediated
immune regulation $\rightarrow$ retrieves 8 candidates including 4
relevant and 4 tangential.

Generator uses all 5 unfiltered. Produces answer citing one tangential
passage $\rightarrow$ one unsupported claim.

Agentic gate evaluates all 8: keeps only the 4 that directly discuss
basophils in lupus contexts. Discards 4 tangential.

Faithfulness: 0.75

Generator receives 4 high-relevance passages $\rightarrow$ every claim
grounded. Faithfulness: 1.0

Why this
is superadditive: HyDE alone would add noisy candidates that reduce
faithfulness (net $\Delta=0.00$ ). Gating alone filters the original
5 passages but cannot improve recall beyond what dense retrieval
provides ( $\Delta=+0.05$ ). Together, HyDE provides the richer
candidate pool that the gate needs to select from, while the gate
removes the noise that HyDE introduces, each component compensates
for the other’s weakness.

Implication for profile design.

Max_reliable is the only SOTA profile that activates both query
augmentation (via HyDE) and agentic gating, which explains its
faithfulness advantage. However, the aggressive refinement paradox
(G1) limits the practical benefit: the gate is too aggressive,
discarding passages that would improve answer completeness.
A promising direction is to relax the refinement threshold
(partially_relevant passes) while maintaining the
hallucination check, which would preserve the I3 synergy without
triggering G1.

## J.4 Scaling Root Causes

The corpus scaling experiments (Section 5) reveal two
phenomena that require mechanistic explanation: (1) log-linear
degradation for retrieval-side profiles (speed,
standard) contrasted with effectively flat behavior for
generation-oriented profiles (balanced,
max-reliable), and (2) a rank crossover between
standard and balanced that is visible from 25K and
statistically significant at 200K ( $p=0.004$ ).

Log-linear degradation.

As corpus size increases from 5K to 50K documents, distractor density
in the candidate set increases proportionally. For a fixed top- $k$ 
retrieval, the probability that a given candidate slot is occupied by a
distractor increases as $1-R/N$ , where $R$ is the number of relevant
documents and $N$ is the corpus size. Since $R$ is fixed (the same
queries target the same documents), the signal-to-noise ratio in the
candidate set degrades logarithmically with $N$ .

The degradation rate varies by profile because different features
provide different levels of noise robustness:

• 

Speed ( $-0.115$ /decade, $R^{2}=0.90$ ): No noise filtering;
degradation rate matches the raw retriever’s sensitivity to distractors.

• 

Standard ( $-0.055$ /decade, $R^{2}=0.89$ ): HyDE and reranking
provide partial noise filtering, but the reranker sees the same degraded
candidate set.

• 

Balanced ( $-0.015$ /decade): Context compression and
document awareness provide generation-side noise filtering that is
independent of candidate set quality; even when the retriever
returns noisier candidates, compression distils the relevant spans.

• 

Max reliable: Not measured at 50K due to computational
budget, but the agentic pipeline’s iterative refinement provides
an additional noise filter.

Rank crossover between standard and balanced.

The standard profile’s advantage over balanced at 5K ( $+0.006$ NDCG@10)
reverses between 15K and 25K. The gap trends in the predicted
direction at 50K–100K but is not statistically significant through
100K ( $p>0.17$ , Welch’s $t$ , $n=3$ seeds per cell); it firms at
200K ( $\Delta=+0.059$ ; $p=0.004$ ). The root cause is the
asymmetric scaling of retrieval-side vs. generation-side
defenses:

• 

Retrieval-side defenses (HyDE, reranking) operate on
the candidate set, whose quality degrades with scale. HyDE’s
hypothetical documents are generated from the query, not the corpus,
so they do not adapt to increasing distractor density. The
reranker scores each candidate independently, so its effectiveness
degrades linearly with the fraction of distractors in the
candidate set.

• 

Generation-side defenses (compression, awareness)
operate on the output of retrieval, after reranking has already
selected the top-10. Even when these top-10 passages contain more
noise at scale, compression can extract the relevant spans, and
document awareness provides stable grounding that does not degrade
with corpus size.

Worked Example: Scale Crossover Mechanism at 50K Documents (C3)

Query (HotpotQA): “Cooperative Living Organization is
located in a city that is the county seat of…”
At 5K documents (base corpus):

• 

Standard: top-20 candidates contain 6 relevant passages
(low distractor density). Reranker selects 4/5 relevant.
NDCG@10 $=0.71$ .

• 

Balanced: same retrieval quality (shared stack), but
compression removes 50% of tokens $\rightarrow$ some context lost.
NDCG@10 $=0.69$ .

• 

Standard wins by $+0.02$ : compression discards useful
tokens when passages are already high-quality.

At 50K documents ( $+$ 45K distractors injected):

• 

Standard: top-20 now contains 12 distractors about
other cooperatives and other county seats (near-miss semantic matches).
Reranker promotes 3/5 relevant, but 2/5 are distractors it
cannot distinguish. Generator sees 2 irrelevant full-length
passages (1,600 tokens of noise). NDCG@10 $=0.44$ .

• 

Balanced: same degraded top-20, same 2/5 distractors
in the reranked set. But: (a) extractive compression removes
the irrelevant spans from the 2 distractor passages, retaining
only sentences mentioning “county seat”;
(b) 100-token document prefix tells the generator which
documents are about cooperatives vs. county government.
NDCG@10 $=0.48$ .

• 

Balanced wins by $+0.04$ : compression and awareness
filter noise that the reranker cannot.

The crossover mechanism: at small scale, the reranker alone
provides sufficient noise filtering and compression adds unnecessary
information loss. At large scale, the reranker’s candidate set degrades
(fixed top-20 from a larger, noisier pool), and generation-side
defenses become the binding constraint.

This analysis provides causal support for the H3 hypothesis premise:
profile rankings are not scale-invariant because different features
have different scaling slopes. A profile optimized for small-scale
retrieval (standard) will be overtaken by a profile with better
generation-side defenses (balanced) as scale increases.

four SOTA profiles, mapping each mechanism to the profiles it affects
and its measured impact.

Design principles emerging from the analysis.

Three actionable principles emerge from this root cause analysis:

1. 

Complement, don’t duplicate.
Features that operate on different pipeline stages (retrieval vs. generation) compose better than features within the same stage.
The balanced profile exemplifies this by pairing retrieval features
(HyDE, reranking, hybrid) with generation features (compression,
awareness).

2. 

Validate composed configurations end-to-end.
Single-feature ablations consistently overestimate composed
performance (composition penalties of 7–72% are observed;
Section 5). The only reliable guide is factorial
evaluation of the composed system.

3. 

Choose features for your scale.
Retrieval-side features (HyDE, reranking) provide the largest
gains at small scale ( $<$ 10K documents) but degrade faster.
Generation-side features (compression, awareness) provide smaller
gains initially but maintain their effectiveness at scale.
Profile selection should account for the target corpus size.

## Appendix K RAG Methods Catalog

This appendix catalogs the techniques associated with the eight
component dimensions varied in the factorial experiment. These
dimensions sit within the nine-stage workflow described in
Section 3: document processing and the base answer generator
were held fixed, while D8 varies agentic control around generation.
Practitioners seeking implementation details for any method can refer
to the cited papers; our implementations follow the original
algorithms unless noted.

## K.1 Embedding Models (D1)

all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384-dim)

A lightweight sentence-transformer distilled from
MiniLM (Wang et al., 2020) via knowledge distillation on 1B
sentence pairs. Fast inference ( ${\sim}$ 10K sentences/s on GPU)
at the cost of lower representation capacity. Used in the
speed profile.

BAAI/bge-m3 (1024-dim)

A unified embedding model supporting dense, sparse (lexical
weights), and ColBERT-style multi-vector
retrieval (Chen et al., 2024a). Trained on multilingual data
with 8K context window. Our default embedding for all non-speed
profiles.

intfloat/e5-large-v2 (1024-dim)

A text embedding model trained with contrastive learning on
large-scale text pairs, achieving strong performance on the MTEB
benchmark (Wang et al., 2022).

BAAI/bge-large-en-v1.5 (1024-dim)

An English-focused embedding model from the BGE
family (Xiao et al., 2024), optimized for retrieval via
retromae pre-training and contrastive fine-tuning.

## K.2 Chunking Strategies (D2)

Fixed-size chunking

Splits documents into fixed-length segments (512 tokens in our
experiments) with configurable overlap. Simple and deterministic.

Semantic chunking

Groups consecutive sentences whose embeddings are within a
similarity threshold, producing semantically coherent
chunks (Chen et al., 2024b). Chunk boundaries align with topic
shifts.

Sentence-window chunking

Each chunk centers on a single sentence but includes a
configurable window of surrounding sentences as context.
Preserves local coherence while maintaining fine-grained retrieval
units.

Hierarchical chunking

Creates parent chunks (2000 tokens) containing child chunks
(400 tokens). Retrieval operates on children; auto-merge
promotes to parents when multiple children from the same parent
are retrieved, recovering broader context (Sarthi et al., 2024).

Structure-aware chunking

Splits documents along structural boundaries extracted during
document parsing (headings, sections, tables, code blocks)
rather than by fixed token counts. Preserves layout semantics
from the source format (Markdown, HTML, PDF) and
keeps related structural units, table rows, list items, code
blocks, in a single chunk; parsed via
Docling (Auer et al., 2024).

Recursive chunking

Splits documents hierarchically using a prioritized list of
separators (paragraph $\to$ sentence $\to$ clause $\to$ token),
recursively subdividing any segment that exceeds the target
chunk size. Produces variable-length chunks that respect
natural text boundaries while bounding chunk size, a pattern
popularized by LangChain and LlamaIndex as the default
general-purpose splitter.

## K.3 Index-Time Enrichment (D3)

None

Chunks are indexed as-is without additional processing.

Contextual retrieval

Prepends a brief document-level summary (generated by an LLM) to
each chunk before embedding, improving retrieval precision without
changing the retrieval model (Anthropic, 2024).

RAPTOR

Recursive Abstractive Processing for Tree-Organized Retrieval.
Clusters chunks and generates recursive summaries at multiple
levels of abstraction, enabling retrieval at varying
granularity (Sarthi et al., 2024).

GraphRAG

Extracts entities and relationships from documents to build a
knowledge graph. Graph traversal augments vector search with
structured reasoning over entity
connections (Edge et al., 2024). Excluded from the factorial
experiment due to entity-extraction cost ( ${\sim}$ 14h per
ingestion combo).

## K.4 Retrieval Modes (D4)

Dense-only

Approximate nearest-neighbor search over embedding vectors using
cosine similarity.

BM25 (sparse lexical)

The classical Okapi BM25 ranking function (Robertson & Zaragoza, 2009),
a term-frequency / inverse-document-frequency score with
length normalization and saturation parameters. Serves as the
sparse baseline in our executable comparisons
(Table 60) and as the lexical component of all
hybrid retrieval modes below.

SPLADE (learned sparse)

A learned sparse expansion model (Formal et al., 2021; Formal et al., 2022)
that produces weighted term vectors by expanding queries and
documents over a BERT vocabulary, enabling lexical match on
semantically related terms.

Hybrid-BM25

Combines dense retrieval with BM25 lexical
matching (Robertson & Zaragoza, 2009) via Reciprocal Rank Fusion
(RRF) (Cormack et al., 2009). The fusion weight $\alpha$ 
controls the dense–sparse balance.

Hybrid-SPLADE

Replaces BM25 with SPLADE (Formal et al., 2021), combined
with dense retrieval via RRF.

Hybrid (RRF fusion)

Reciprocal Rank Fusion (Cormack et al., 2009; Bruch et al., 2023)
is the rank-aggregation algorithm that underlies all
lasagna hybrid modes: each result list contributes a
score $1/(k+r_{i})$ for document at rank $r_{i}$ (we use the
standard $k=60$ ), and the fused ranking sorts by summed score.
Robust to per-retriever score scales and the default fusion
for combining dense and sparse signals.

Graph-based retrieval

Augments vector retrieval with traversal over a knowledge graph
extracted at index time. For a query, entities and neighbors
within $k$ hops are materialized as additional passages
alongside the top- $k$ vector hits, broadening recall on
multi-hop questions (Edge et al., 2024). Shares the
ingestion pipeline with the GraphRAG enrichment
(GraphRAG) and was excluded from the
factorial on cost grounds (Section A.4).

Structured-RAG comparators (extension).

A parallel line of work replaces flat vector retrieval with graph-
or schema-based indexes: HippoRAG (Gutiérrez et al., 2024) runs
personalized PageRank over an entity graph, KAG (Liang et al., 2024)
fuses schema-aware knowledge-graph traversal with a semantic layer,
and hierarchical extensions such as TreeRAG (Tao et al., 2025)
add document-tree structure. These systems are complementary to
lasagna: each replaces the retrieval-stage internals while
preserving the stage-isolation interface, so they can be dropped in
as an additional D4 (retrieval) level and evaluated under the same
factorial protocol. We did not include them in the 242-cell design
because the D-optimal set was locked at pre-registration; a natural
extension is to test whether graph-based indexing changes the
D5 $\times$ D8 composition patterns reported in
Table 42.

## K.5 Query Augmentation (D5)

None

The original query is used as-is for retrieval.

HyDE (Hypothetical Document Embeddings)

An LLM generates hypothetical answer documents; their embeddings
are averaged with the query embedding to improve recall for
underspecified queries (Gao et al., 2023a).

RAG-Fusion

Generates multiple query variants via an LLM, retrieves for each,
and merges results via RRF (Rackauckas, 2024).
Broadens the retrieval scope at the cost of additional LLM calls.

Query decomposition

Decomposes complex queries into sub-questions, retrieves for each,
and aggregates evidence. Effective for multi-hop
reasoning (Press et al., 2023).

Step-back prompting

Generates a more abstract “step-back” question that captures
the high-level intent, retrieves for both the original and
abstracted queries (Zheng et al., 2024).

## K.6 Reranking (D6)

None

Retrieved passages are returned in their original retrieval order.

Single cross-encoder (CE)

A cross-encoder model (mxbai-rerank-large-v1 in our experiments)
scores each query–passage pair jointly, reranking the top- $k$ 
results (Nogueira & Cho, 2019).

Ensemble reranking

Combines scores from multiple rerankers (cross-encoder +
ColBERT-style) via score fusion.

MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance)

Iteratively selects passages that are both relevant and diverse,
penalizing redundancy (Carbonell & Goldstein, 1998). Our experiments
find this consistently hurts NDCG@10 in domain-focused corpora
(Section 5).

## K.7 Context Compression (D7)

None

Full retrieved passages are passed to the generator.

LLMLingua (extractive)

Extractive compression that removes tokens with low information
content according to a small language model, reducing context
length while preserving key
information (Jiang et al., 2023a).

## K.8 Agentic Quality Gates (D8)

None

Single-pass generation without quality verification.

CRAG (Corrective RAG)

A lightweight evaluator grades retrieval quality; if confidence
is low, the system triggers web search or knowledge
refinement (Yan et al., 2024).

Self-RAG

The generator emits reflection tokens that gate retrieval and
self-assess generation quality, iteratively refining the answer
through multi-turn retrieval and
verification (Asai et al., 2024).

Speculative RAG

Generates multiple draft answers from different retrieved subsets
and uses a verifier to select the best, trading latency for
answer diversity (Wang et al., 2025).

Quality gates (hallucination filter)

A gate that sits between retrieval and generation and accepts a
chunk only if an LLM judge classifies it as supporting the
question; chunks below the threshold are dropped. Shared
machinery across CRAG, Self-RAG, and Speculative-RAG, and used
directly in the max-reliable profile
(Section I.1.4). Raises the faithfulness floor
but can starve the generator on multi-hop queries
(Section J.2.4).

Multi-hop refinement loop

An iterative retrieve–refine–generate loop: after an initial
retrieval and draft answer, the system issues follow-up retrievals
based on what the draft is missing, repeats for up to $K$ 
iterations (3 in our experiments), and generates the final
answer from the aggregated context. Related
schemes include IRCoT (Trivedi et al., 2023) and
FLARE (Jiang et al., 2023b); our implementation runs inside
the same agentic graph as the quality-gate filter.

## Appendix L Terms, Common Questions, and Released Resources

This appendix gathers the paper’s terms in one place, answers the questions a
reader is likely to bring to it, each with a pointer to the full account, and
lists what is released.

Keywords.

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG); RAG evaluation;
compositionality of RAG techniques; ablation study; factorial design;
pre-registration; corpus scaling; reranking; hybrid retrieval; query
augmentation; agentic RAG; answer faithfulness; NDCG@10; BEIR.

## L.1 Terms

lasagna.

An open-source framework for evaluating new RAG
components inside a complete modern pipeline, with a fixed interface per
stage that the framework enforces, so a new component drops into one stage
and is measured against the same surrounding stack used by every reported
configuration (Section 3).

Dimension.

One of the eight experimental factors of the study
(Table 10).

Profile.

One of the four operational RAG compositions distilled from
the factorial and ablation sweeps as the best-observed configurations for
distinct points on the retrieval-faithfulness-latency envelope:
speed, standard, balanced and
max-reliable (Section I.1).

Track.

One of the two evaluation surfaces: retrieval or end-to-end
(Section 4).

NDCG@10.

The retrieval-quality metric: whether the right documents are
retrieved, following the BEIR convention (Thakur et al., 2021)
(Section C.1).

Answer faithfulness.

Whether the generated answer is grounded in the
retrieved context, scored with RAGAS (Es et al., 2024)
(Section C.1).

Composition shortfall.

How far a combined pipeline’s gain falls below
the gain predicted by adding up its techniques measured one at a time; $63\%$ 
for the three strongest retrieval techniques (Section 5).

Joint hypervolume (HV).

 $\mathrm{NDCG@10}\times\mathrm{Faith}\times\max(0,1-\mathrm{latency}/L_{\mathrm{ref}})$ at $L_{\mathrm{ref}}{=}30$  s,
the joint quality-latency envelope used to compare
profiles (Zitzler et al., 2003) (Table 2).

## L.2 Common Questions

Do RAG improvements measured one component at a time hold when the
components are combined?

Not reliably for retrieval. The three strongest single-component retrieval
improvements predict $+47\%$ NDCG@10, and the pipeline containing all three
realizes $+17\%$ , a $63\%$ shortfall. Across 17 tested combinations the
shortfall ranges from $7\%$ to $72\%$ , with a median of $41\%$ . Faithfulness
gains combine nearly as measured: $+28\%$ predicted, $+32\%$ realized
(Sections 5 and D.14).

Do retrieval quality and answer faithfulness favor the same RAG
design choices?

No. In the factorial study, embedding, enrichment and reranking dominate the
retrieval-quality metric (NDCG@10), whereas query augmentation and agentic
gating dominate answer faithfulness, which measures whether generated claims
are supported by the retrieved context. Optimizing a RAG pipeline for one metric
can hurt the other, so new components should be evaluated on both
(Section 5).

Does the best RAG pipeline on a small corpus stay the best at large
scale?

Not necessarily. Across corpora from 5K to 200K documents, standard
leads at 5K and 15K, while balanced leads from 25K through 200K, where
it exceeds standard by $13.7\%$ relative NDCG@10 ( $p=0.003$ , three
seeds). Rankings from small-corpus ablation studies should therefore be
revalidated at the corpus scale a deployment will use
(Sections 5 and B.3).

Is there one best RAG pipeline?

No complete RAG profile is best on every metric. speed is fastest,
standard leads NDCG@10 and achieves the highest joint hypervolume
( $0.523$ at a $30$  s reference), and the agentic balanced and
max-reliable profiles lead faithfulness at a higher response time. The
best configuration depends on the target metrics, and adding components does not
guarantee a better pipeline (Section 5).

How was the study designed?

Three hypotheses were pre-declared before the experiments
ran (van Miltenburg et al., 2021). The study tests 450 configurations
across eight benchmarks: a 167-configuration D-optimal
design (Fedorov et al., 1972) over the eight dimensions, 138 non-factorial
ablation configurations across 24 targeted ablations, and a corpus-scaling sweep
from 5K to 200K documents. Deviations from the pre-registration are reported
(Sections 4 and A).

Which benchmarks, metrics and models does the paper use?

Five BEIR retrieval sets (Thakur et al., 2021): NFCorpus (Boteva et al., 2016),
FiQA (Maia et al., 2018), SciFact (Wadden et al., 2020),
ArguAna (Wachsmuth et al., 2018) and HotpotQA (Yang et al., 2018); and
three question-answering sets: SQuAD v2 (Rajpurkar et al., 2018),
HotpotQA and MuSiQue (Trivedi et al., 2022). Retrieval is scored with
NDCG@10 and answers with RAGAS faithfulness (Es et al., 2024), judged by
GPT-5.1. A generator-substitution check replaces GPT-5.1 with Claude
Sonnet 4.5 (Sections B.2, C.1 and F.9).

Was the pre-declared interaction test (H1) met?

No. It asked whether pairwise interactions explain at least $20\%$ of the
variance in faithfulness, and they do not. A focused follow-up, pre-specified
before it ran, confirms that the interaction the broad analysis flagged is real
( $p=0.007$ ) but far below that bar. The retrieval composition shortfall above is
measured separately and does not depend on this test
(Sections 5, D.3 and D.9).

How should a new RAG technique be evaluated?

Inside a representative complete pipeline, on both retrieval quality and
answer faithfulness, and at the corpus scale it will be used at. Gains measured
in isolation are not reliable predictors of realistic performance.
lasagna supports this: a component registered with one decorator is
measured against the same surrounding stack as every reported configuration,
with a paired statistical test (Sections 3 and 6).

What are the limitations?

The evaluation uses one constructed English multi-domain test bed, at most 93
retrieval queries and 200 generation queries per experiment, in five focused
domains. It does not test multilingual corpora, real enterprise or private data,
open-weight generators, or cross-organizational large corpora. Faithfulness is
measured under two generator models, a sensitivity check rather than a sweep,
and absolute faithfulness scores rest on one judge’s calibration
(Limitations, after Section 6).

Where are the code and the project page?

The lasagna code is at https://github.com/dolev31/lasagna, and
the project page, with figures and a summary of the results, is at
https://dolev31.github.io/lasagna/. The pre-registration is archived at
https://github.com/dolev31/Lasagna-Retrieval-Augmented-Generation-Compositionality.

## L.3 Where This Work Sits

The paper sits between RAG methods research (Lewis et al., 2020; Gao et al., 2023b),
RAG evaluation frameworks and metrics (Es et al., 2024; Saad-Falcon et al., 2024; Yu et al., 2024),
retrieval benchmarking (Thakur et al., 2021), toolkits that compose RAG
pipelines (Jin et al., 2025; Rau et al., 2024) or search over
them (Kim et al., 2024), and the design of experiments for comparing many
configurations (Montgomery, 2021; Fedorov et al., 1972). Its contribution
is a measurement of how techniques compose, not a new technique.
